"I know."

"I mean it. They told me you were good. They didn't tell me you were — " He seemed to look for the word. "Attentive."

"I'm a tug pilot," she said. "You fly forty tonnes of sled on a bad tow, you get attentive or you get dead."

The bead was pulling.

She noticed it somewhere in the last few minutes, the way you notice a sound that has been there for a while. The little nub of glass at her throat had swung on its cord and was lying, not against her breastbone, but out from it, a finger's width from her skin, the cord stretched taut, leaning toward the mouth like a dog straining at a leash. She put her hand over it. It pressed into her palm, very gently, insistently.

"Mr Lyle."

"Ambrose."

"Ambrose. Does glass — " She felt stupid. "My brother's a cutter. He says glass knows where the Needle is."

He leaned forward in the jump-seat and looked at the bead in her hand, and then at her, and for a moment he looked so interested that she forgot to feel stupid.

"Your brother is right," he said. "It does. It's the same stuff, you see — the throat and the glass. Negative energy. That's what holds the Needle open: a ring of something like brine-glass, only stranger, at the waist, pushing outward. The glass in the salt is a kind of — cousin. It feels the throat the way a compass feels a pole." He smiled. "Cutters have known that for fifty years. Physicists keep rediscovering it and writing papers."

"Is that why it's narrowing? The ring at the waist?"

The smile went. "I don't know why it's narrowing," he said. "That's why we're here."

They came to the inner markers — two old beacon buoys, dark, dead for decades, hanging in the grey-gold glow like drowned men — and Teo brought the Mercy round between them and held her there, heading into the shear, mains just feathering, while the tug shuddered and wallowed and the wall of light leaned over them.

"Here?"

"Here. Hold her as steady as you can."

He went back to the cargo bay. She heard him in there, the clank of straps, the grunt of a man too old for this hauling something heavy. She heard the inner airlock cycle and the outer one open. Then she felt the buoy go — a small lurch as the mass left the tug — and in her rear camera she saw it tumble away from the Mercy's stern, a little grey cylinder turning end over end, slower and slower, until the mouth's pull caught it and it stopped turning and hung there, quite still, nose to the dark, as if it were listening.

Its lattice of wires opened, like an umbrella. Like a flower.

Ambrose came back up and dropped into the jump-seat and pulled his headphones on, and plugged the reader into something, and closed his eyes, and his face changed.

"Oh," he said.

"What?"

He did not answer. He was listening. She watched him in the mirror. She watched the colour go out of his face and come back, and his lips move as if he were counting, and his hands, which had been loose on his knees, close slowly into fists.

"What is it?" she said. "Ambrose. What is it?"

"It's — " He opened his eyes. They were wet. "It's beautiful. I didn't know. I didn't know it sounded like this up close. I've spent twenty-three years listening to it from the back of the room." He gave a sort of laugh. "And the waist is going down. It's going down faster than the harp says. The harp's too far away. It smooths everything out." He listened again. "It's like a drawstring. Like someone's pulling a drawstring. Very slowly. Very evenly."

"Can it close?"

"I don't know." He took the headphones off. "But there's — there's something else. Under it. I heard it in the Lectern the other night and I thought it was the instrument. It isn't the instrument. It's here too, it's louder here. A kind of — "

The shear hit them from behind.

There had been no warning. He had had his headphones off. It came out of the mouth at their stern like a fist, and the Small Mercy went over on her side and then on her back and the canopy was full of grey-gold light and then of stars and then of light again, and the proximity alarm was shrieking, and somewhere behind her Ambrose had been thrown against his straps and was saying something she could not hear. The yoke was dead in her hands. The tug was tumbling toward the dark — she could see it in the canopy each time they came round, the bruise, nearer, nearer, the coils of starlight going past like the walls of a drain.

She did not think. She cut the mains, and fired the starboard attitude jets in one long burn until the tumble slowed to a spin and the spin to a wallow, and then she put the nose, not away from the mouth, but along it — skimming the edge of the wall of light, letting the pull take her round in a long falling curve the way you let a current take a boat round a rock — and when the curve brought her nose round to face the inner markers again she gave the mains everything they had and a little more, and the Small Mercy screamed and shuddered and clawed her way up out of the pull, metre by metre, until the wall of light fell away behind them and the shear-waves were only waves again, and then only shudders, and then they were past the last beacon and in the lanes and it was over.

She sat with her hands on the yoke. They were shaking. She could not make them stop.

Behind her, Ambrose Lyle was laughing. It was not a happy laugh, exactly. It was the laugh of a man who has been very frightened and has found himself alive.

"Six hundred marks," he said. "You could have died for six hundred marks, Miss Kesh."

"I die for less than that every day," said Teo. "Usually it's slower."

He stopped laughing. In the mirror she saw him looking at her, really looking, as if he had just seen something he had not expected to see.

"Thank you," he said.

"Write the waiver," she said, and her voice came out wrong, and she turned the Mercy for home.

* * *

The Assembly Hall in Tallow was the oldest room in the Reach: a cavern cut into the salt by the first Concession machines, its walls still scored with their long curved grooves, its ceiling so low at the back that tall men stooped. Nobody had built anything in it. Nobody had put up a gallery or a terrace or dressed the salt with stone. There were benches, and a platform at the front, and a single bank of floodlights that made everyone look ill, and that was all. The Concession held its notices here, and its inquiries, and its debt hearings, and on the fifteenth of March it held the Director's address, and the room was so full that people stood in the doorways and sat on the floor between the benches and climbed on each other's shoulders at the back.

Teo stood against the wall halfway down, with Corrigan beside her and Nico on her other side, and watched Hesper Vane walk out onto the platform without any notes.

She did not hurry. She walked to the front of the platform and stood there with her hands folded and waited for the room to be quiet, and it took a long time, and she waited, until it was.

"You know there is a Narrowing," she said. "I told you so on the second. I told you it would be deep. I'm here to tell you how deep."

She told them. She told them in numbers. She did it the way Teo imagined she did everything, precisely and in order, without softening anything: the waist at sixty-one per cent of nominal on the second, at fifty-two on the ninth, at forty-eight this morning. The rate of narrowing. The projections. She said that the Concession's throat-readers did not know whether the waist would stop narrowing before it reached zero. She said that if it reached zero the Needle would close, and that a closed Needle had never been observed, and that no one could say whether it would ever open again.

The room had been quiet. It became something else, a silence with a held breath in it.

"I am not here to frighten you," Hesper Vane said. "I am here to tell you what we are going to do about it."

She told them that too.

She said that the throat of the Needle was held open by a ring of exotic matter at its waist — that this was known, that it had been known for fifty years. She said that brine-glass was the same kind of matter, in a cruder form. She said that the Concession's analysis indicated that the waist was narrowing because the ring was weakening: because it was, in effect, starving. And she said that if brine-glass were delivered to the waist during the Narrowing, released there at the narrowest point, it would brace the ring as a splint braces a bone, and the throat would hold.

"We will feed it," Hesper Vane said. "That is the long and the short of it. We have the glass. We are sitting on the largest deposit of brine-glass in human space. We will take it out to the Needle in the Narrowing and we will feed it to the throat, and the throat will hold. I am calling it the Keeping." She let the word sit. "It will require pilots. It will require pilots who are willing to take a small ship, loaded with glass, into the throat during the Narrowing, to the waist, and release their load there, and come back. I will not pretend to you that this is safe. It is not safe. It has never been done. It is the most dangerous flight anybody in the Reach has ever been asked to make."

She looked out over the room.

"So I will make this offer, and I will make it once. Any pilot who flies a Keeping run — any pilot who goes to the waist and releases their glass — will have their indenture cancelled. In full. Not reduced. Cancelled. And not only theirs. Their family's. Every debt held by the Concern against every member of their household, struck off the books on the day they fly." A pause. "That is my offer. Thank you."

The noise came up out of the room like water out of a burst pipe.

Teo did not hear most of it. She was looking at Hesper Vane, standing quite still on the platform with her hands folded, waiting for it to subside the way she had waited for the silence; and she was thinking of Ambrose Lyle's face in the mirror with the headphones on, two days ago, at the inner markers. *Like a drawstring. Very slowly. Very evenly.* He had not said anything about starving. He had not said anything about a ring weakening. He had said *I don't know why it's narrowing*, and he had been the man who would know.

"Feed it," said someone at the front, very loud. Mother Salome. She had stood up on her bench, tall and stooped, a head above everyone round her, and the floodlights made her face look like a carving. "You want to feed it. Feed it what, Director? Our glass, you said. Our glass, that our children cut out of the salt and die of. Our pilots, that you'll send into the dark in little ships. You want us to feed it our children."

"I want you to keep it open," said Hesper Vane. "So that your children have a door."

"A door for what? Your ships leave in six weeks. What's on the other side of that door for us, Director? The Board? Ardent? The people who hold our paper?"

"Everything is on the other side of that door," Hesper Vane said evenly. "Medicine. Machines. Seed stock. Every ship that will ever come to the Reach. Every ship that will ever leave it. If the Needle closes, Mother, there is no other side. There is the Reach, alone, forever. Ask yourself whether your children would thank you for that."

Salome stood on her bench and said nothing. Teo had never seen her with nothing to say.

"It's a lie," said Nico.

He said it quite quietly, beside Teo, and then much louder, and then he was on the bench too, and the cutters round him were looking up at him with their faces lit by the floodlights. "It's a lie. She doesn't know it's starving. Nobody knows. She's just — she's *saying* it. She needs the glass shipped and she needs us scared and she needs us to — "

"Nico," Teo said. "Get down."

"— to keep sending it after they've *gone*, that's what this is, it's a way of keeping us — "

Marshal Brandt's men came through the crowd from the doorway, four of them, big, unhurried, in the grey of Concern Security, and the crowd parted for them the way water parts for a hull. Nico saw them coming and kept talking. He was still talking when they reached him. Two of them took his arms and lifted him down off the bench as easily as lifting a child, and he kicked and shouted, and one of the cutters beside him swung a fist, and Brandt himself, who had come up behind his men, put out one large hand and caught the fist in mid-air and held it, without apparent effort, and looked at the cutter until he lowered his eyes.

"Out," said Brandt. "All of you. Now. Quietly."

They took Nico out. Teo went after him. She shoved her way along the wall with Corrigan behind her, and caught them in the passage outside, where the Security men had put Nico against the salt and were going through his pockets. Brandt was watching. When he saw Teo he raised one finger, not to her, but to his men, and they stopped.

"Kesh," said Brandt. "The pilot."

"He's my brother."

"I know who he is." Brandt's voice was mild. "Take him home. He's not charged. The Director doesn't want anybody charged tonight. Take him home and tell him the Director doesn't want anybody charged tonight." He looked at Nico. "Tonight."

They let him go. He came off the wall with blood on his lip and his eyes blazing, and Teo took him by the back of the neck the way their mother used to, and walked him away down the passage, and he let her.

Corrigan walked with them in silence as far as the Lantern Row lift. Then, at the lift door, with Nico gone ahead into the car, he put his hand on Teo's arm.

"Teo."

"Don't."

"I haven't said anything."

"You're going to say *cancelled in full*. You're going to say *Lulu's too*."

He was quiet. His hand stayed on her arm. Under the lift's bad light his face looked older than it was, and she remembered all at once, for no reason, a night eleven years ago on the Brannock with the canopy full of stars and his laugh in the dark.

"Lulu's too," he said softly. "Teo. She's nine. She'll be paying off Mira's medical till she's fifty. Mine. My father's. She'll never — "

"It's never been done, Corr. You heard her. Nobody knows what's at the waist. Nobody's ever *been* to the waist in a Narrowing. I was at the inner markers two days ago in the Mercy and I nearly didn't come back, and that's the *edge*."

He looked at her strangely. "You were at the inner markers?"

"Charter."

"In a *tug*?"

"Don't change the subject."

"I'm not." He let go of her arm. He smiled, a little, the old smile. "I'm just thinking that if you can do it in a tug," he said, "so can I."

The lift door opened. Nico was inside, holding the door, with blood on his lip. Teo got in. Corrigan did not.

"Corr," she said.

"I'll see you on the lanes," he said, and the door closed.

* * *

"There is no basis for it," Ambrose said.

The Director's Gallery was at the top of the station's spindle, the Needle-ward end, a long low room with a curved window that took up the whole of one wall, so that the mouth of the Needle hung outside it like a vast dark painting. Hesper Vane had furnished it with almost nothing: a desk, two chairs, a cabinet, a rug of some deep red Inward wool that must have cost more than Ambrose's harp. She sat behind the desk now with her hands folded on it. He had not sat down.

"I never said it was starving," he said. "I never said anything about a ring weakening. I never said that feeding glass to it would do anything at all. Nobody has ever said that. There is no data. There is no theory. You made it up."

"I extrapolated."

"From what?"

"From what you told me." Her voice was level. "You told me the waist is narrowing. You told me you don't know why. You told me the throat is held open by exotic matter. You told me brine-glass is exotic matter. You told me you have no model and no theory and four data points and a lot of hope." She spread her hands slightly on the desk. "I have given them a model. I have given them a theory. I have taken away the hope and given them something better."

"Which is what?"

"Something to do."

He stared at her.

"People can bear almost anything, Ambrose," Hesper Vane said, "except not knowing what to do. You know this. You've watched it, these last two weeks — the bars, the dorms, the shafts. Forty thousand people who have just been told that the company that owns them is leaving and that the door to the rest of the universe may be closing behind it. What do you think they will do with that? If they have nothing to do with it?" She did not wait for an answer. "They will riot. They will stop cutting. They will tear Anchorage apart looking for someone to blame and they will find me, because I am the one who is staying. I have given them something to do. I have given them a way to save themselves. Whether it works — "

"It won't work."

"You don't know that either." She looked at him, and her eyes were very steady. "Do you? Can you tell me — with data, Ambrose, with a theory — that it won't?"

He opened his mouth and shut it.

"No," she said. "You can't. You've said so to me twice." She leaned back in her chair. "So. The Narrowing will do whatever it does. If the Needle opens again, the Keeping will have kept it open. If it doesn't, nothing would have. Either way, for forty-seven days, the Reach will have something to do other than burn itself down. That is the whole of my policy. You may disapprove of it. I would think less of you if you didn't."

"And the pilots?"

Something moved in her face, very briefly, and was gone. "The pilots will be volunteers. Every one. I will not order anyone into the throat. They will be paid more than anyone in the Reach has ever been paid for anything, and the ones who come back will be heroes, and their families will be free." A pause. "And the ones who don't come back will be heroes too."

Ambrose turned away and looked out of the window at the mouth. He thought of the buoy out there at the inner markers, two days in the pull now, singing to him across two million kilometres: the long, slow, even drawing-in of the waist, and under it the other thing, the swell, the tide. Louder there. Real. He had sat in the Lectern last night with the headphones on and listened to it until three in the morning. It had a period, he was almost sure, of a little over four minutes. He did not know what it was. He did not know what it meant.

He had not told her about it. He realised, standing at her window, that he was not going to — not yet, not until he knew. He did not examine why.

"Your berth on the *Providence*," Hesper Vane said behind him, "is confirmed for the twenty-eighth. Senior grade, as I said. Cabin twelve. I checked." He heard her slate click. "Forty-four days, Ambrose. Do your job until then. Read the throat, give me the numbers every six hours, don't speak to the bars. And then go home."

He put his hand in his jacket pocket and closed it round the fork.

"Yes, Director," he said.

# Chapter 5: Substitution

*The Severance · 2608*

The petitions for substitution came to Adaeze Quill because nobody else wanted them, and because she was the archivist, and the archivist was where things went when the order did not know what to do with them.

There had been eleven so far, in the ten days since the Draw. There were always petitions. A mother for her son, a husband for his wife, a woman of thirty-nine for her nephew of eighteen; they came up from Tallow Under on the supply shuttle in envelopes of good paper, or in person, in their best clothes, and sat in the chair across Adaeze's desk in the little office off the archive and explained, very carefully, as if she might not have understood the Articles, that they were of age and willing and asked only to take the place of the one they loved. Adaeze listened to every one. She wrote them out fair, in the old hand, on the order's vellum, and sealed them with her own seal, and sent them up to the High Warden, and the High Warden declined them. He had declined every one for nineteen years. Four had been granted in the whole history of the Compact. It was not a door, the substitution; it was a painting of a door on a wall. Everyone knew it. They came anyway. Adaeze thought that the coming was the point — that people needed to have knocked.

The twelfth was in naval greys, and sat down across the desk without being asked.

"Commander Asante," said Adaeze.

"You know me."

"Everyone in the Anchorhold knows your sister's name." Adaeze folded her hands. "And I saw you, once, at your commissioning. You were nineteen. You had your hair much shorter. Your sister was very small and kept wandering off into the harp hall."

Juno Asante looked at her properly for the first time. Adaeze saw herself being looked at: a woman of forty-four in Warden's grey, round-faced, with ink on her fingers and her hair tied back in the plain knot of the order and a pair of reader's spectacles on a cord round her neck. Nobody remarkable. She had never minded being nobody remarkable.

"I've come to petition," said the Commander.

"I know."

"I'm thirty-one. I'm of Draw age. I'm a volunteer. Under the Articles, Section Nine — "

"I know the Articles, Commander." Adaeze said it gently. "I'll write it out. I'll write it out as well as anybody has ever written one, and I'll seal it, and I'll take it up to the High Warden myself, which is more than I do for most of them. I want you to know that before I tell you the rest."

"Which is?"

"That he will decline it."

The Commander's jaw set. "You don't know that."

"He has declined every petition for substitution since he was invested. Two hundred and six of them, at the last three Draws. I've written every one." Adaeze pulled a sheet of vellum toward her and uncapped her pen. "I've read the reasons too. They're always the same. The Needle chose; it is not for the Wardens to correct the Needle. It's theologically sound. I'm afraid it's also unanswerable."

She began to write. *To the High Warden of the Throat, from Juno Efua Asante, Lieutenant Commander of the Compact Navy, of the Speaker's house, being of age and of sound mind — .* She wrote in the old hand, the slanting formal script the order had used since before the Compact, and she could feel the Commander watching the pen move.

"You believe that," Juno Asante said. "That the Needle chooses."

Adaeze kept writing. "I believe the Wheel is a lottery and the Needle is a wormhole," she said, "and that between them, every four thousand days, nine people are asked to do something very terrible on behalf of everyone else. I believe it has kept the door open for three hundred years. Whether the Needle *chooses* — " She dipped the pen. "I'm the archivist, Commander. I believe in records. The records say that every time we have kept it, it has opened. That's what I believe."

"And the people who don't come back?"

Adaeze stopped writing.

"I have their names," she said. "Every one, every Keeping. A hundred and twelve. They're in the archive behind that door. I read them, on the anniversaries." She looked up. "I'm not a fool, Commander. I'm not a fanatic. I didn't take the grey because I think it's beautiful. I took it because somebody has to keep the records honestly, and I thought it might as well be someone who could bear to read them."

The Commander was quiet. Adaeze finished the petition and sanded it and folded it and dripped the grey wax and pressed her seal into it, and held it up.

"Would you like to see the harp?" she said. "While you're here. Your sister did, when she was eight. She stood in front of it for an hour. We had to carry her out."

They went through the archive — a long vaulted room, cold, lined floor to ceiling with racks of harp-records on spools and crystal and the oldest of them on paper, three hundred years of the Needle's voice written down — and through the iron door at its end into the harp hall.

It had been the Lectern once, in the Concession's time. The dome was the same dome, a hemisphere of old glass looking out on the mouth, and the floor was the same floor, worn into hollows by three centuries of feet. The harp stood in the middle of it. Adaeze had served here for twenty years and it still stopped her breath, sometimes, coming through the door. A frame of fused silica taller than a man, gone faintly gold with age, strung with nine courses of resonator wire that caught the grey light of the mouth and held it. It was singing. It was always singing. The long, wide, many-stranded drone of the throat, low and soft, filling the hall, so that coming into it was like walking into the inside of a held breath.

"The frame is original," Adaeze said quietly. "The First Warden's. The wires have been restrung many times. We tune them every morning to the Hunger Tone." She nodded to a small alcove off the hall, behind a grille of black iron, where a lamp burned. "The Fork is kept there, between Draws. It's struck once, at every Keeping, here, at the moment the Keepers go in. That's all. It's never used for tuning. It's too precious."

"What's it for, then? A tuning fork you don't tune with."

Adaeze smiled. "That's the sort of question your sister would ask."

The Commander did not smile. She stood in the middle of the hall with her hands at her sides and looked at the harp, and then up through the dome at the mouth, the bruise-dark wound with its rim of bent light, and Adaeze watched her face and saw on it something that she had seen on many faces in twenty years and had never been able to name. Not fear. Something more like grief, arriving early.

"She'll be up here," the Commander said. "Training. For seven weeks."

"Yes."

"Will you — " She stopped.

"I'll look in on her," said Adaeze. "I look in on all of them."

* * *

The High Warden received her the next morning, which was itself, Juno understood, a kind of answer.

His rooms were at the very top of the Anchorhold's spindle, at the Needle-ward end: a long low gallery with a curved window filling one wall, so that the mouth hung outside it vast and dark, like a painting of nothing. It was almost bare. A desk. Two chairs. A cabinet of old black wood with a lock. On the floor a carpet of some deep red stuff so old that its pattern had worn to the weft in a path from the door to the desk, as if the same feet had walked that line for centuries. Cassius Mor stood at the window with his back to her when the novice showed her in, and did not turn round at once.

"Commander Asante," he said to the glass. "Sit down. Please."

She did not sit. After a moment he turned, and smiled at her — a warm, tired, genuinely kind smile, the smile of a man who has been expecting a difficult conversation and is sorry it has to happen — and came and sat behind the desk himself, and gestured again at the chair, and this time she sat, because standing had become ridiculous.

Her petition lay on the desk between them, its grey seal broken.

"Sister Adaeze writes a beautiful hand," Mor said. "And she has put your case as well as it could be put. Better. I've read it twice." He laid his palm on the vellum, gently, as if it were a living thing. "I'm going to decline it, Commander. I expect you know that. I wanted to tell you so myself, rather than send it down with a seal on it, because I think you are owed that, and because I wanted you to understand why."

"The Needle chose," Juno said.

"The Needle chose." He nodded slowly. "You think that's a formula. A thing we say. It is a thing we say. But it is also true, and I would like you to try, just for a moment, to hear it as true." He leaned forward. "The Draw is the only thing in the Reach that is perfectly fair. Do you understand? The only thing. The Speaker's daughter and the dock clerk and the Shoals girl in a rock with no name — every one of them a slip of foil in the same drum. No money buys you out of it. No family. No office. It is the one place where we are all the same. That is why it works, Commander. That is why the Reach has borne it for three hundred years. Because it is fair." He spread his hands. "And if I let the Speaker's daughter out, because the Speaker's other daughter is a decorated officer who has come in her greys to ask me — then it is not fair any more. Then it is a thing the powerful can get their children out of. And the next time the Wheel turns, every mother in the Fourth Terrace will know it. And the Draw will be broken. And the Keeping with it."

It was a good speech. Juno had heard admirals give worse. She sat and let it go past her and watched his face.

"There are four substitutions in the record," she said.

"There are. In three hundred years. Each of them for reasons which I would not wish on your family." He sighed. "Commander. I know what you are. I know what you've done in this war. I know you've been out in the Shoals for six years taking names off registries for the Draw — " a small, delicate pause — "and I know what that has cost you, because I have read your reports, and you write them very honestly. You of all people know what the Draw costs. You of all people know what it would mean if it were seen to bend."

She did not answer.

"How is your mother?" Mor said.

The change of direction was so smooth that it took her a second to feel it.

"She's well."

"I'm glad. She carries a great deal. I know it." He looked at her with what seemed to be real concern. "The appropriation for the Shoals campaign comes before the Assembly next week. I understand she hasn't yet said how she'll vote."

"I don't discuss my mother's votes, High Warden."

"No. Quite right." He smiled again. "I mention it only because the Keeping cannot be flown if the Shoals can raid the glass stores at will. You know better than I how close they came last month. The Keeping must be protected, this time of all times. The Thirtieth is deep. Deeper than anything in two hundred years. If the escort is not funded — " He let it trail away, and shook his head slightly, as if at the size of the thought. "Well. I hope she will remember that her daughter flies in it. I'm sure she will."

Juno looked at him. Outside the window, beyond his shoulder, the mouth of the Needle hung huge and patient, and she thought of the Hall, ten days ago: the seventh slip of foil held up to the light; the High Warden's eyes lifting from it, before he read, across the floor to the Speaker's terrace. Only for a moment.

"Thank you for seeing me, High Warden," she said, and stood.

"Commander." He stood too, courteous. "Your sister is extraordinary. I've met her. She has a gift for this that I have rarely seen. She is not afraid. You should be very proud."

"I am."

"Then let her go," said Cassius Mor gently. "It is the last thing you can give her."

* * *

They gave the nine a dormitory on the second wheel, nine narrow cells off a common room with a window on the mouth, and they gave them each a bead.

It was the first thing, the night they arrived: a Warden going round the common room with a little wooden box, and each of them taking one out, a nub of cloudy brine-glass the size of a pea on a black cord. You wore it from the night you came up until the night you flew. Pell put hers on at once and felt it settle into the hollow of her throat, cool, and lie there, very slightly to the left. Toward the mouth. The Warden said it always would.

"It's to remind you," the Warden said, "that you are already part of it."

Ezra Lind, sitting on the bench beside her, turned his bead over in his ink-stained fingers and said, under his breath, "Part of what, though," and then looked horrified at himself, and put it on very fast.

By the fourth day Pell liked him best of all of them. There was Tavish Orme, who was thirty-eight and had three children and did not speak to anyone; there was Abbe Sorrell, the Shoals girl they had brought in on a Navy ship with her hands cuffed, who had stopped fighting on the second day and stopped eating on the third; there were five others whose faces Pell was learning, a baker and a lift-mechanic and a woman who had been a Warden novice once and left. And there was Ezra, who was twenty-two and a clerk in the dock office and had never flown anything bigger than a cargo sled across a hangar floor, and who was so frightened, all the time, that it came out of him as jokes.

"The thing is," he said to her in the simulator bay on the fourth day, strapped into the mock cockpit of a Keeper's skiff with the screens full of grey-gold throat and his hands white on the stick, "the thing is that I failed my sled licence twice. Twice. They told me I didn't have the spatial reasoning. And now the Needle has looked into my soul and said, *Ezra Lind, you are the man to fly into my throat.* I think the Needle's made a clerical error. I think I should file a complaint."

"File it with Sister Quill," Pell said. "She does the paperwork."

"I tried. She said the Needle doesn't accept complaints. She said it very kindly." He banked, too hard, and the simulator screamed at him and the screens went red. "Oh, for — that's the fourth time I've died today. Do you think it counts? Do you think if you die enough times in the simulator the Needle lets you off the real one?"

"I don't think it works like that."

"No," said Ezra, and looked at the red screen, and his face changed for a second, and he said in a different voice, "No, I don't suppose it does," and then he reset the simulator and made a joke about the instructor's moustache.

The skiffs were very simple. That was what surprised Pell. She had imagined something grand, something like the ships in the old paintings in the Compact Hall; but a Keeper's skiff was twelve metres of narrow hull with a single seat and a cradle under the belly for the glass, and an engine, and a release lever painted white. You flew it into the mouth at the start of the Long Night, when the throat was tightening, following the reader's line, and you flew it down the throat as far as the waist, and at the waist you pulled the white lever and the glass went, and then you turned round and flew back the way you came. That was all. The instructors went over it and over it. The line, the waist, the lever, the turn. The line, the waist, the lever, the turn.

They did not talk about how many turned and did not come back. Nobody did. Pell knew the number anyway, the way everybody in the Reach knew it: a little under half. A hundred and twelve names in Sister Quill's archive. She thought about it sometimes, at night, in her cell, with the bead lying cool against her throat. She waited to be frightened. She was not frightened. She was something else that she did not have a word for, something that hummed in her like the harp.

She went to the harp hall on the sixth night, very late, because she could not sleep and because it was the only place in the Anchorhold she wanted to be.

Sister Quill was there. She was sitting on the floor with her back against the wall under the dome, in the dark, with her spectacles in her lap, listening. She looked up when Pell came in and did not seem surprised.

"I come here too," she said. "When I can't sleep. Sit down, if you like."

Pell sat down beside her. The harp sang in front of them, gold-wired, the long low many-stranded drone of the throat, and above them in the dome the mouth hung, huge, its rim of light trembling very faintly. It had begun to tremble on the fourth day. The readers said it was the Narrowing beginning in earnest. You could see it if you watched for long enough: a slow shiver at the edges, like heat over a road.

"Can I ask you something?" Pell said.

"You can ask."

"What does it look like? The waist. Inside."

Adaeze Quill was quiet for a while.

"I don't know," she said. "Nobody does. The ones who come back don't speak of it. It's a rule of the order — they're asked not to, and they don't. I've asked some of them myself, privately, when I was younger and more curious. They wouldn't tell me." She turned the spectacles over in her lap. "One of them — an old man, he'd flown the Twenty-Sixth — told me that it wasn't a thing that had words. That's all he'd say."

"I've painted it," Pell said.

The Warden turned her head.

"I've been painting it since I was eight. Since I heard this." Pell nodded at the harp. "I never get it right. I brought one with me. They said one bag." She did not know why she was saying this. "Do you want to see it?"

She went and got it from her cell. When she came back Sister Quill had lit the little lamp in the alcove beside the reliquary grille, and Pell held the canvas up in its light. It was the throat one, the one she had chosen on the floor of her room in Tallow Under while Juno knelt among the canvases: the long tunnel of folded light, gold and grey and a colour that was not quite either, opening and opening down its length toward a place at the centre where everything drew in, tight as the iris of an eye, and in the middle of that, very small, something that was not quite white.

Sister Quill looked at it and did not speak.

"Where did you see that?" she said at last. Her voice was odd.

"I didn't see it. I heard it. I just — that's what the harp sounds like. To me. That's what it sounds like inside."

"The iris," said the Warden softly. "In the middle. Drawn in."

"That's the waist. I think. That's where it goes tight." Pell looked at her. "Why? What's wrong?"

"Nothing's wrong." Sister Quill took off her spectacles, which she had put on to look, and folded them carefully. "There's a description in the archive. Very old — one of the oldest things we have. An account of the throat in a Narrowing, written by a reader, from before the Compact. Most of it's lost. But there's a passage — " She stopped. "It says almost exactly that. *Drawn in like an iris. And at the centre, a light I have no name for.*" She gave a small, uncertain laugh. "I've read it a hundred times. I never saw it until now."

They sat together in the lamplight in front of the singing harp, the painting propped against Pell's knees, and neither of them said anything for a long time. Pell's bead lay against her throat, cool, leaning very slightly toward the mouth.

"I'm not afraid," Pell said finally. "Everyone keeps waiting for me to be afraid."

"I know," said Adaeze Quill. "I've noticed." She put a hand, briefly, on the back of Pell's neck, the way you would with a child. "It's all right not to be. And it will be all right, if you are."

* * *

The order came through the day after she got back aboard: the *Vigil* was detached from the Shoals patrol and assigned to the close escort of the Thirtieth Keeping, with station at the Anchorhold from the twentieth of November until the Narrowing.

"They're giving us the Keeping," said Marsh, reading it off the slate in her day cabin with his eyebrows up. "That's — Captain, that's an honour. That's a flag posting. Six weeks parked at the Anchorhold guarding the glass stores and then we fly the Keepers to the mouth." He looked up and saw her face, and his own changed. "Oh. Oh, Captain. I didn't think. I'm sorry."

"Don't be. It's an honour." Juno took the slate off him. "Somebody pulled a string for it. I'd like to know whose."

"The Admiral, probably."

"Probably," Juno said, and did not believe it.

Mor, she thought. Or her mother. Either of them could have done it, for opposite reasons. She did not know which would be worse.

The other message came in on the evening watch, on a channel it should not have been able to use: a Shoals relay, bounced off three rocks and an abandoned ore station, unencrypted, plain text, addressed to her by name. Marsh brought it to her himself, with the door shut behind him.

"It came in on the old Academy frequency," he said. "The cadet band. Nobody's used it in years. Comms only caught it because Siddiq's a sentimental idiot and still listens to it." He put the slate down in front of her. "Captain, if this gets logged — "

"It's logged," Juno said. "Everything's logged. Leave it with me."

He left it with her. She read it once.

*JUNO. I HEARD ABOUT PELL. I'M SORRY. YOU KNOW I AM. ASK THEM HOW THE NAMES ARE CHOSEN. — R.*

She sat with the slate in her hands until the watch changed.

Rhee Calder had been in her year at the Academy. They had shared a simulator pod for two years and a bed for six months of the third, and he had been the best pilot in their intake and the worst at doing as he was told, and she had loved him in the uncomplicated way you love someone at twenty. He had a younger brother, Callum. At the Twenty-Ninth Draw, eleven years ago, when Juno was twenty and Rhee was twenty and Callum was eighteen, the Wheel had turned and Callum Calder's name had come out of it. He had flown. He had not come back. Rhee had gone very quiet, and stayed quiet through their last year, and two days after commissioning he had taken a Compact patrol cutter out on a routine flight to the Shoals and not brought it back either. Six years ago, when the Shoals refused their quota, the Free League's first fleet commander had been a man the Compact papers called the Saltjack, and the face in the grainy picture had been his.

She had fired on his ships. He had fired on hers. They had never spoken since.

*Ask them how the names are chosen.*

She thought of the Hall. The seventh slip of foil. The High Warden's eyes lifting to the Speaker's terrace before he read. *I hope she will remember that her daughter flies in it.* She thought of Sister Quill at her desk: *two hundred and six petitions. He has declined every one.* The one place in the Reach where everyone was the same.

She logged the message, as regulations required, with a note — *unsolicited enemy contact, no reply sent* — and sent it up the chain to Squadron. Then she sat for a while longer in the dark of her cabin, with the *Vigil*'s engines thrumming through the deck and the slate's afterglow fading on her palm, and found that she had memorised it anyway.

# Chapter 6: The Anchor

*The Ebb · 2937*

The Anchor looked, from the outside, like something that had died with its eyes open.

Isra had seen it before, from the Ossuary — a far-off spindle with two wheels, dark against the bruise of the mouth — but she had never been close, and coming in on it in the Gristle she found that she did not like it. It was too big. It had been built for thousands of people and was lived in by a dozen, and you could see which parts they lived in, because those parts had lights: a cluster of windows at the near end of the spindle, a band of them round one wheel. The other wheel did not turn. It hung there motionless, its windows black, its spokes furred with frost, and somewhere on its rim a loose panel was flapping in a wind that did not exist, opening and closing, opening and closing, catching the grey light of the mouth each time like a slow wink.

"Gristle, Anchor." An old man's voice on the radio, dry, unhurried. "State your business."

"Bone-picker. I've got wire. Resonator wire, good, a full spool. I was told the Wardens buy it."

A pause. "Brother Lucan buys it. Dock Four. Mind the second ring of the dock, it's loose."

The dock was huge and empty and cold and the second ring was very loose. Isra tied the Gristle up with her own lines rather than trust the dock clamps, and climbed out with the spool of wire over her shoulder in its sack, and stood in the middle of the great dark hangar under the one working light, and waited, and felt the place around her: miles of corridor, hundreds of rooms, all of it silent, all of it cold. She had stripped Returns with dead men in them and felt less watched.

The man who came for her was younger than she had expected. A Warden — the grey robe, the silver at the cuffs gone dull and the cuffs themselves frayed — but not much older than her, thin and quick, with a long face and ink on his fingers and a pair of reader's spectacles shoved up into untidy dark hair. He came across the hangar at a sort of half-run and stopped in front of her a little out of breath.

"You've got wire," he said.

"I've got wire."

"Is it — sorry. Brother Lucan." He held out an inky hand and then seemed to think better of it and put it back in his sleeve. "Is it really resonator wire? Not cable? People bring cable. They think because it's thin — "

Isra swung the sack down and opened it. The spool inside was a hand across, wound tight with wire so fine it looked like a strand of light, silver, unoxidised, because she had found it sealed in a cannister in the bowels of an old Compact ore-hauler that had been drifting at the edge of the Ossuary for longer than anybody remembered. She had known what it was because Hanne had told her what to look for, years ago. *If you ever find wire that rings when you pluck it, girl, don't sell it to the Pans. Take it to the Wardens. They'll give you anything.*

Brother Lucan knelt down on the hangar floor and took a length of the wire between his fingertips, very carefully, and plucked it. It rang. In the cold empty hangar the note went up into the dark like a bird. He shut his eyes.

"Where did you get this?"

"Return. Old one. Before the Quiet."

"It's Compact. It's real Compact resonator stock. We haven't had any in — " He opened his eyes and looked up at her. "What do you want for it?"

She had planned to ask for a lot. She found she did not quite know how much was a lot to a man in a frayed robe in a station that was mostly dark. "What've you got?"

"Not much," he said honestly. "Food. We have food. We have a greenhouse on the near wheel that does better than anything in Tallow, and the Prior lets me trade from it. And medicine, some. And — " He hesitated. "I don't suppose you want to see the harp? People sometimes do. Before they decide."

She had not meant to. She went.

He led her up through the spindle, through long dim passages where his footsteps echoed and the air smelled of old metal and incense and cold, past doors that opened on dark rooms full of nothing, until they came to a vaulted gallery lined with empty racks, and an iron door at its end, and through it into the harp hall.

Isra stopped in the doorway.

The mouth filled the dome. She had seen it every working day of her life, from the Ossuary, from the Pans; she had never seen it like this, so close, so huge, leaning over the glass like something looking in. And under it, in the middle of the worn floor, the harp. A frame of something like gold glass, taller than Pike, strung with long lines of wire that held the grey light of the mouth and glowed with it faintly; and it was singing. A low, wide, many-stranded sound that she felt more than heard, in her chest and her teeth and the soles of her feet. It was the Hunger Tone — she knew it from Hanne's cittern, from the Lantern House speakers — only it was not quite. It was the Hunger Tone the way a person's voice in another room is their voice.

"It reads the throat," Lucan said quietly beside her. "The throat makes a sort of — noise, all the time. Gravitational noise. The wires pick it up and sing it. A reader listens and works out the shape of it. Where the waist is. How narrow." He went to the harp and laid his palm flat against the frame, the way you would touch a horse. "This frame is the First Warden's. It's the oldest made thing in the Reach. The wires are whatever we can get." He grimaced. "Which hasn't been much, lately. Three of the low courses are spliced. Two of them are just — wrong. I tune them every morning and by the evening they've drifted. Your wire would — " He stopped, and looked at the spool on her shoulder, with an expression on his face that she recognised from the Pans buyers when they saw good Return glass. Hunger. "It would make a difference."

"Show me," she said.

He restrung one of the spliced courses while she watched. It took an hour. He did it on his knees on the cold floor with his spectacles on and a little pair of pliers, talking the whole time, half to her and half to himself: how the wire had to be tensioned, how it had to be seated, how you could hear a bad seating as a kind of buzz in the chord. When he had finished he stood back and tuned it. He did that by ear. He stood with his eyes shut and his head on one side and *sang* — a low long note, the drone she knew — and turned the peg at the top of the frame a hair at a time until the new wire stopped fighting the note and lay down inside it.

"The Hunger Tone," he said, when he had finished. "That's what we tune to. Every reader learns it at investiture. The Prior sings it, and you sing it back, until you can't not."

"What about that?" Isra nodded toward a little alcove off the hall, behind an iron grille, where a lamp burned in front of something small and brass on a grey cushion. She had seen it when she came in.

"That's the Fork. The First Warden's Fork." He said it with a kind of embarrassed reverence, as if he were introducing her to his grandmother. "It's — well. It's the holiest thing we've got. It's said to be what the First Warden heard the hunger with. It's struck once at every Keeping, here, when the Keepers go in. You can see the motto on it, if you get close. *For Clarity.*" He made a face. "Mostly worn off. It says FOR CL."

"Don't you tune with it?"

"Oh, no. No. It's a relic."

"It's a tuning fork."

"It's a *relic* tuning fork." But he was smiling a little. "You sound like me when I was a novice. The Prior used to send me to the chapel for a day for saying things like that."

She did not smile back. She was thinking of something else. Without meaning to, she had put her hand flat against her chest, over the shard of banded glass that she had worn inside her shirt for twelve days now, on a cord, against her skin. She had been holding it there since he started tuning. It had not stirred. Not once. Not when he plucked the new wire, not when the whole harp took the note and sang it back.

"Can I tell you something stupid?" she said.

He looked surprised. "Of course."

She took the shard out and showed him, and told him. The Return, the layered root, the bands like a cliff. Hanne's cittern. The low string, and the glass humming back.

Brother Lucan held the shard in the light of the reliquary lamp, turning it, his spectacles down on his nose.

"It hummed," he said. "At your grandmother's cittern."

"Every time she plucked the low string."

He went to the harp and held the shard against the frame, close to the new wire, and plucked it. The note sang out. The shard lay in his hand, dull, silent.

He plucked it again. Nothing.

"Maybe it's broken," Isra said. "Maybe whatever it did, it's stopped."

"Maybe." He did not sound as if he thought so. He gave it back to her, and his eyes went for a second, she noticed, to the reliquary, to the little brass fork on its cushion; and then away again, quickly, as if he had caught himself looking at something he should not. "What's your grandmother's cittern tuned to?"

"I don't know. Whatever she tunes it to. It was her gran's. She tunes it by ear."

"Huh," said Lucan.

He was quiet for a moment. Then, in a different voice, brisk, as if changing the subject on purpose: "Would you like to see the Bell? Before you go? It's the other old thing we've got. Most people don't know it's there."

He took her to a telescope at the side of the dome, an old heavy brass thing on a pivot, and fussed with it, and stepped back. She put her eye to it.

At first there was only the mouth, filling everything, grey-gold at the rim. Then, very small, at the edge, she saw it: a little grey cylinder hanging in the glow, motionless, nose toward the dark, with something at its tail end that might once have been a frame of wires, open like the ribs of an umbrella, now bent and furred with frost.

"It's a buoy," said Lucan. "A listening buoy, we think, like a little harp. It's been there longer than the order's records go back. Longer than the Quiet, certainly. The mouth's pull holds it. It hasn't moved in three hundred years that we know of. It doesn't do anything now. It's dead." He paused. "The old Wardens used to say the First Warden put it there. To listen to the Needle from close by. So they called it the Bell."

Isra looked at it for a long time through the cold brass eyepiece: a small dead thing, holding its place at the edge of the dark, listening to nothing.

"I'll take food," she said, straightening. "And medicine. For a man with glass-lung. Whatever you've got. And you can have the spool."

* * *

The Lamplighter came to the Anchor two days later, and Lucan watched the Prior go to meet her the way you watch an old dog go out to meet a bigger one.

She came with forty people. Her own ship — a converted ore-hauler painted black with the yellow lamp on its bow, bigger than anything else flying in the Reach — and two cutters, and the Deacon, and a crowd of Lantern faithful in black with lamps stitched on their shoulders who filled the hangar and the passages and stood in the harp hall in a silent double rank along the walls, watching the harp sing. Lucan had been given no warning. He was on his knees at the base of the frame with the last of the new wire when the doors opened and they came in, and he had not had time to get up.

Prior Saar came in front of her. He was eighty-one and walked with two sticks and had been Prior of the Anchor for thirty years, and in all that time Lucan had never seen him bow to anyone. He did not bow now. But he stood aside, which was worse.

"The harp hall," said Prior Saar. "As you asked."

Mercy Oduya came past him into the middle of the hall and stopped and looked up.

She was not what Lucan had expected. He had heard her voice for years, on the speakers, in Tallow, on every channel; he had imagined somebody tall, iron, a prophet from a painting. She was small. Smaller than him. A broad, plain, dark-faced woman of fifty in a black coat, her hair grey at the temples, her hands bare and cracked like a cook's. She stood under the dome with the mouth leaning over her and the harp singing in front of her, and she looked up at the Needle, and Lucan saw her face change, and saw — he could not have said how — that she had never been here before. That she had been preaching the Needle for twenty-five years and had never stood this close to it.

"Oh," she said, very softly.

Nobody moved. The harp sang.

She went to it. She walked up to the frame, right up, as close as Lucan was, and he scrambled back on his knees out of her way, and she did not seem to see him. She put out one cracked brown hand and laid it on the old gold glass, flat, the way he did himself. She stood there with her palm on the harp and her eyes closed and the drone going through her, and her lips moved, and Lucan, who was nearest, heard what they said. It was a name. *Tobin.* She said it three times.

Then she opened her eyes and took her hand away and turned, and she was the Lamplighter again.

"Prior," she said. "Thank you. I wanted to stand here. I wanted to hear it." Her voice was the voice from the speakers, warm and low; in the hall it was very quiet and carried to every wall. "Now I'd like to hear from your readers."

"Brother Lucan is our reader," said the Prior.

She looked down at him, on his knees, with wire in his hands. "Brother."

He got up. He was aware of his frayed cuffs, of the ink on his fingers, of the double rank of black coats along the walls. Of the Deacon, standing just inside the door: a thin, neat, pleasant-faced man with his hands clasped behind his back, watching Lucan with polite interest, as if he were an item on a list.

"What does it say?" said the Lamplighter, and nodded at the harp.

"It — " Lucan cleared his throat. "The waist is at nineteen per cent of nominal. It's been below twenty-five since the Fifty-Ninth. It hasn't recovered between Narrowings, the way it used to. Each one leaves it a little narrower." He hesitated. "The readings are noisy. I've only just restrung it. Some of the courses were — I'm not sure how much to trust what it was telling us before."

"At the Sixtieth. What will it do?"

"I don't know."

"Brother." She came a step closer. She was looking up at him; she had to. Her eyes were very dark and very steady. "I didn't ask what you know. I asked what it will do. You listen to it every day. You've listened to it longer than anybody living. What will it do?"

He looked at the harp. He thought of the long nights, the headphones, the drone. He thought of the waist line on the old screen, creeping down, creeping down, year by year, a little lower after every Narrowing, like a tide going out that never came back in.

"I think it will close," he said. "I think — I think it's dying."

"Of what?"

"I don't know."

"I do," said Mercy Oduya.

She did not raise her voice. She said it quite gently, as if she were sorry to have to. "It's hungry, Brother. It's been hungry for a hundred years. We feed it less and less, and it gives us less and less, and now it's giving back what we gave it because it hasn't the strength to keep it. You've seen the Returns. I've seen them. My son is in there." She did not look away from him. "He was seventeen. He went in at the Fifty-Ninth with a cradle of glass and a bead at his throat, and I sang him off from the Lantern House steps, and I have not slept a night through since. And I have asked the Needle every night for eleven years whether it was enough. Whether he was enough." Her voice did not break. "It wasn't. Nine skiffs at the Fifty-Eighth. Seven at the Fifty-Ninth. It wasn't enough. So we will give it enough."

Lucan found he had nothing to say. The black coats along the walls were silent. Somewhere in the double rank someone was weeping, very quietly.

"I'll need your readings," the Lamplighter said, turning to the Prior. "Every day from now until the Sixtieth. Where the waist is. When it narrows. When the Long Night begins, to the hour. I'll need a reader on my ship when we go in, to give the line. And I'll need the Wardens on the steps of the Lantern House at the Great Keeping, in your grey, with your Fork, to bless the Keepers as they go." She paused. "As the Wardens always used to."

Prior Saar leaned on his two sticks.

"The Wardens will bless the Keeping," he said. "As they always have."

"Thank you, Prior." The Lamplighter inclined her head, which was more than he had done. Then she looked once more at the harp, and at the mouth above it, and at Lucan, standing beside it with the wire in his hands.

"Brother," she said. "Thank you for telling me the truth. Most people don't. I could hear that you were." And she went out, with her black coats behind her, and the Deacon last, who smiled at Lucan pleasantly on his way through the door, and the door closed.

The harp sang. Prior Saar stood in the middle of the hall on his two sticks, very old, looking at nothing.

"Prior," Lucan said.

"Not now, Brother."

"She's going to put a hundred people in the throat. A hundred, Prior, and every gram of glass in the Reach, and I *don't know* — "

"*Not now.*" The old man's voice cracked like a whip, and Lucan stopped. Saar closed his eyes. When he opened them again he looked only tired. "There are twelve of us, Lucan. Twelve. In a station built for six thousand. We eat because the Lantern lets us trade in Tallow. We have air because the Lantern lets the Pans sell us scrubbers. When she asks us to stand on her steps in our grey, we will stand on her steps in our grey. That is what it is to be twelve." He turned toward the door on his sticks. "Finish your wire. Read your throat. Give her the numbers. And for the love of whatever you still believe in, Brother, stop telling people you don't know."

* * *

The Lantern came into the Gutter at the change of the shift, when everybody was home, which was how Isra knew they had planned it.

They came down Rope Street in a long black line with lamps, real lamps, oil lamps on poles, swinging, so that the light ran ahead of them along the salt walls in long wavering yellow tongues and the shadows of the doorways jumped. Two by two. Knocking. Every door. Isra heard them coming from the far end of the tunnel and had time to do one thing, and the thing she did was take the shard out of her shirt and put it in her mouth.

It was cold. It tasted of salt and metal and the deep cold of the mouth. She tucked it into her cheek and sat down on the end of Hanne's bed and folded her hands.

"Girl," said Hanne, watching her.

"Shh."

They knocked on the ship-door. Pike, who had been sitting on the floor by the heater with his back to the wall, half-asleep, looked at Isra, and got up slowly and opened it.

There were four of them, young, in black, with the lamp on the shoulder. Behind them, in the tunnel, Deacon Rusk, holding no lamp, his hands clasped behind his back.

"Good evening," said the Deacon. "Old Hanne. Isra. And — Pike, isn't it? Ostap Pike. I didn't know you'd come down to the Gutter." He smiled. "I'm sorry to call so late. It's the levy. You'll have heard."

"We heard," said Pike.

"Then you'll know what we're looking for. Glass. Any glass at all. Cut, raw, dust, Return, beads, bowls, buttons, it doesn't matter. All glass is tithe." He nodded to the four young ones, and they came in.

It did not take them long. Hanne's room was full of things, but there was not much in it worth anything, and almost nothing of glass. They found a jar of glass-dust that Hanne kept for the cittern strings, to rub on them, she said, it made them speak. They found three old beads on a thread in the bottom of a tin of buttons. They found Pike's inhaler, which had a little cartridge of fine glass powder in it that the Pans doctor said eased the lungs, and the boy who found it looked at it and then at Pike, and Pike looked back at him and said nothing, and the boy put it in his bag.

"That's his medicine," said Isra. It came out muffled, round the shard. She did not care.

The boy looked at the Deacon. The Deacon looked at Isra, pleasantly, for a long moment.

"All glass is tithe," he said. "I'm sorry. I'm sure the Pans doctor can find him something else."

"There isn't anything else."

"Then he'll have to manage," said Deacon Rusk. "As we all will. We're all giving something, Isra Venn. The Lamplighter's giving her son. Some of us are giving our lives. A man's cough is not very much to ask." He tilted his head. "Is there anything in your mouth?"

The room went still.

"No," said Isra.

"Open it."

She did not. Pike moved — a small movement, just a shift of his weight, toward the Deacon — and two of the young ones moved too, toward Pike, with their hands going to their belts, and for a second it was all there in the little warm room, ready to happen.

"Oh, for the love of the door," said Hanne loudly.

Everyone looked at her. She was sitting up against the wall with her cittern across her knees, small and brown and folded, and she was glaring at the Deacon with her bird's eyes as if he were a child who had trodden mud on her floor.

"I knew your mother," she said. "Hettie Rusk. She sang flat. She sang flat at the Fifty-Seventh and she sang flat at her wedding and she sang flat at your naming, I was there, I heard her. And she'd be ashamed of you, standing in an old woman's room in the middle of the night going through her button tin. There's nothing in the girl's mouth but her tongue. It's the only thing in this family that's ever been worth anything, and you can't have it." She plucked the low string of the cittern, hard, a single angry note. "Get out."

The Deacon looked at her. Then, to Isra's astonishment, he laughed — a real laugh, light and surprised.

"My mother did sing flat," he said. "Good night, Old Hanne." And he nodded to the four, and they went, with the jar and the beads and Pike's inhaler in their bags, and the lamps went away down Rope Street swinging, and the next door along began to be knocked on.

Isra spat the shard into her palm. She was shaking.

Under the low string's dying note, in her cold wet hand, she felt it hum.

Pike sat down on the floor by the heater, slowly, the way he did everything now, and put his face in his hands. When he coughed, it went on and on. She sat down beside him and held the rag for him, and when it was over he did not take his hands from his face.

"I could have had him," she said. "Rusk. I could have — you shouldn't have stopped me."

"I didn't stop you."

"You were going to."

"I was going to stop *them*," Pike said through his hands. "Stop them stopping you. There's a difference." He lowered his hands. His eyes were red. "Isra. Listen. Don't fight the Lantern. You don't know what they are."

"I know what they are. They're Rusk and a lot of boys with lamps."

"I was one of the boys with lamps." He said it flatly. "Hanne told you. I know she did. I flew escort for the Lamplighter at the Fifty-Ninth. I was on the *Patience* with Rusk." He looked at the heater, at its little orange glow. "We took the Keepers out to the mouth. Seven skiffs. Kids, mostly. Volunteers. They'd been in the Lantern House for weeks, singing, they'd been — " He moved his hand. "They were happy. You've never seen anything like it. They were so happy. And we took them out to the mouth at the start of the Long Night and they went in one after another, in a line, like birds. And one of them turned round."

Hanne had gone very still, on the bed. Isra did not move.

"Just turned round," said Pike. "Right at the edge. Pulled up and turned and came back out toward us. We didn't know if something was wrong with the skiff. Or with him. And Rusk got on the channel and told him to turn back to the mouth, and he didn't, he just kept coming, and Rusk told him again, and he said something. On the open channel. We all heard it." His voice was very quiet. "He said, *I don't want to. I'm sorry. I don't want to.*"

"Pike," said Isra.

"And Rusk said, *Fire,*" said Pike. "And we did. The *Patience*'s forward guns. I was on the second gun. I fired." He looked at his hands, the big ruined hands that were so gentle on the grapple arm. "We hit the engine. The skiff went dead. And it drifted. Just drifted, back toward the mouth, very slowly, with the pull. We watched it go in. Rusk said, *Well, he's a Keeper now,* and we turned for home." He was quiet for a while. "I never knew his name. They didn't give us names, on the escort. I asked, after. Nobody would tell me."

The heater ticked. Down Rope Street the knocking went on, door after door, and the lamps swung, and somewhere far off somebody was shouting.

"That's what they are," said Ostap Pike. "That's the Lantern. Don't fight it, Isra. It doesn't lose."

Isra sat with the shard in her fist, and her other hand on his back, and said nothing. On the bed Hanne put the cittern aside, very gently, and lay down with her face to the wall, and after a while Isra realised that the old woman was crying, without a sound, the way you do when you have been doing it all your life.

# Chapter 7: The Second Rhythm

*The Concession · 2291*

At nine hundred metres the salt sweated.

Nico Kesh had been cutting in Shaft Nine for ten years and he still noticed it every shift, the moment the cage dropped past the eight-hundred mark and the air turned thick and warm and the walls began to glisten: a fine bright beading of brine on the grey-white rock, catching the lamps, running in slow threads down the cuts. The old cutters said the salt was crying. The older cutters said it was the glass breathing, down in the seams, and that if you put your ear to the wall in the deep galleries at the end of a shift when the machines were off you could hear it. Nico had tried, at sixteen. He had heard something. He had never been able to say what.

The seam they were working that week was the Lower Nine, a long low gallery where the glass ran in a vein as thick as a man's thigh between two beds of hard salt, and the Concession had put two shifts on it since the Director's address. Then three. Keeping glass, the overseers called it, with a kind of embarrassed reverence, as though it were for a church. The quota boards at the cage-head had been repainted with a new line at the bottom, in red: *KEEPING ALLOCATION*. The number on it went up every day.

Fennick lost his hand at the end of the second shift on the twenty-third.

Nico did not see it happen. He was twenty metres along the gallery with his own cutter in the vein, and he heard the burst — a flat hard crack like a pistol shot in a small room, which was the sound glass made when it let go — and then the scream, and by the time he got there Fennick was sitting in the dust with his back against the wall holding his right arm up in front of his face and looking at it with an expression of polite surprise, and there was nothing on the end of it below the wrist but a mess, and the vein beside him was a starred white crater the size of a dinner plate where the glass had shattered under his blade.

"Too fast," Fennick said, to nobody. "I was going too fast. They said go fast."

They got him up in the cage. They got a tourniquet on him. Nico rode up with him, holding his good hand, and Fennick talked the whole way up, very calmly, about his daughter's birthday, and passed out at the six-hundred mark.

The vote was that night, in the shaft canteen, with the dust still on everyone.

Nico stood on a table to call it. He had not planned what he was going to say. He did not need to. The canteen was full, three shifts' worth, a hundred and forty cutters and loaders and cage-men crammed in among the long tables in the bad light, and every one of them had heard the burst or heard about it, and the red line on the quota board was in every one of their heads, and Nico stood on the table and said Fennick's name and then said nothing at all for a while, and let them look at him.

"They want us to cut faster," he said at last. "So they can take what we cut and throw it into the dark."

Nobody said anything.

"That's what the Keeping is. You heard her. She's going to put our glass in little ships and send it out to the Needle and drop it down the hole. Our glass. That we cut. That Fennick cut. And the price of it — " He held up his own right hand and turned it, slowly, in the light. "The price of it's this. And she doesn't even know if it'll work. Nobody knows. She *said* nobody knows. She just — needs us to do something with our hands so we don't use them for anything else."

"What else would we use them for?" somebody called from the back.

"That's the question, isn't it," Nico said.

There was a ripple of laughter, rough, not quite comfortable. He waited for it to die.

"I'm not asking you to do anything stupid," he said. "I'm asking you to stop. That's all. When the Keeping shipment's due — when they come down to load the glass for the throat — we stop. We put down the cutters and we sit down in the galleries and we don't cut. Not for the Keeping. We'll cut for the Inward, we'll cut quota, we'll cut what pays the debts. But not for the dark. Not one more hand for the dark." He looked round at them. "That's it. That's the vote. Who's with me?"

They voted with their hands. It was not unanimous. It was not close.

Teo was waiting for him at the cage-head when he came up, sitting on an upturned ore bucket in her flight jacket with her arms folded, and he knew from her face that she had heard.

"You're out of your mind," she said.

"Hello, Teo."

"A *strike*. In the middle of the — Nico, do you know what Brandt's going to do?"

"Arrest me, probably." He sat down on the next bucket. He was very tired, suddenly; the whole day was in his shoulders. "Fennick lost his hand."

"I heard."

"They told him to go fast. For the Keeping."

"I *heard*, Nico." She rubbed her face. "Listen to me. Six weeks. That's all it is. Six weeks and the *Providence* sails and the Narrowing comes and goes and the whole thing's over one way or another. Keep your head down for six weeks. Please. For me."

"And then what? Then the Concern's gone and she's still here and the debts are still here and we're still cutting, and nothing's changed except we did what we were told one more time." He looked at her. "You know what she's doing. You know it better than me. You fly the lanes, you've seen the Needle. Does it look *hungry* to you?"

Teo did not answer. She was thinking, he could see, of something. Her hand had gone, the way it always did, to the bead at her throat.

Mother Salome came out of the canteen behind him with the Tally under her arm in its blanket. She stopped beside them, tall and stooped, and looked down at the two of them on their buckets for a long moment, the way she had looked at them when they were children and their mother was alive and they had been fighting over something in the passage.

"He's right," she said to Teo. "And you're right. That's the trouble with this family. You're both always right, and you never listen to each other." She shifted the Tally under her arm. "I'm going up to Fennick's wife. Somebody should tell her before the Concession does."

When she had gone, Teo said quietly, "I'm frightened for you, Nic."

"I know."

"No. You don't." She looked at the cage-head, at the quota board, at the red line. "I was at the inner markers ten days ago. In the Mercy. With the harp man — Lyle. Ambrose Lyle. He was putting a buoy out." She was quiet. "He doesn't know why it's narrowing, Nico. The man who reads it. He doesn't know. He said so. And she stood up in the Assembly two days later and told everybody it was starving."

Nico stared at her.

"I'm not saying you're right," Teo said. "I'm saying I don't know. And when I don't know, I keep my head down." She got up off the bucket. "Keep your head down. Please."

* * *

The old traces were on paper.

The Lectern's archive was a narrow room behind the dome, lined with steel drawers, and in the bottom drawers, under fifty years of spools and crystals, were the harp records of the first Narrowings: long rolls of thermal paper from the days before the station had memory to spare, printed with the traces in faded violet ink. Ambrose had not looked at them since he wrote his paper on the Narrowing of fifty-eight, twenty years ago. Nobody had. He spent the night of the twenty-fourth on the floor of the archive with all four of them unrolled around him, weighted at the corners with coffee mugs, his reading glasses on and a lamp on the floor beside his knee, while Bettany slept at her console in the dome with her head on her arms.

He was looking for the swell.

He had listened to it every night since the buoy went out. It was there. It was not the instrument; the buoy heard it louder than the harp did, from two million kilometres closer, and the buoy and the harp were different instruments built ten years apart and agreed about nothing except what they were both hearing. A long slow rise and fall under the throat's chord, with a period of a little over four minutes. Not steady. That was the thing. The period was *drifting* — lengthening, very slightly, day by day, the way a pendulum's swing lengthens when you let out the string. He had a column of figures for it in his notebook. He did not like what the figures were starting to look like.

If it was real now, it had been real before.

The trouble with the old traces was that they were made on the first harp, the little one, the prototype he had built in Ardent and shipped through in crates before the real harp was finished; and the little harp had drifted badly. Its strings had crept, the way strings do. Every trace on those old rolls was a little out — sharp, flat, smeared — by an amount that changed from day to day. You could not lay them over each other and compare them. Everyone who had studied the old Narrowings had said so, including Ambrose, in print.

But he had tuned the little harp, every morning, to the fork.

He had forgotten that until three nights ago, lying awake in his bunk. He had tuned it every morning to the fork, and logged the tuning, and the tuning logs were on the same paper rolls, in the margins, in his own younger handwriting: *0600, ref 440, strings 3 and 7 flat 2c, corrected.* Every morning for five years. Which meant that every trace on those rolls could be referred back to the same note — to the fork, to a scream in a delivery room in Ardent — and corrected, day by day, for exactly how far the strings had drifted. It was tedious work. It took him all night. He did it with a pencil and a slide rule and the fork itself lying on the paper beside him, as if it might help. The two oldest rolls, from forty-seven and fifty-eight, had not been made on any harp of his at all; they came off the survey gravimeters the Concession had run before he arrived. But in sixty-nine the gravimeter and the little harp had recorded the same Narrowing side by side, and the gravimeter's drift could be read off against the harp, and the harp's against the fork. A chain of corrections three links long. He did that too.

At four in the morning he stopped and sat back on his heels and took his glasses off and looked at what he had done.

The swell was there. In all four.

Faint, under the drift; but there, once you had taken the drift away. The same slow rise and fall under the throat's chord. In forty-seven, in fifty-eight, in sixty-nine, in eighty. And in each of them it was at a different place in its own long cycle — he could see that now, laying the corrected traces side by side — rising in one, falling in another, near its crest in fifty-eight, near its trough in eighty. And the depth of each Narrowing followed it. Exactly. Where the swell had been high, the Narrowing had been shallow. Where it had been low, the Narrowing had been deep. Eighty, the deepest before this, had come with the swell near its lowest.

Two rhythms. That was what he was looking at. The breathing — the Narrowing, every four thousand days, regular as a clock. And under it something much slower, a long tide with a period of decades, rising and falling, so that sometimes the breath came at high water and was hardly a breath at all, and sometimes it came at low water and went deep. Two rhythms beating against each other, the way two strings a hair out of tune beat against each other, *wah-wah-wah*, louder and softer, louder and softer.

And if the long tide had been falling through eighty, it would be lower now. At its lowest. At the very bottom.

He did the sum on the back of an envelope with his hands not quite steady. He did it three times. The swell's drifting period, the slow lengthening he had been logging all week, was exactly what you would expect from a tide approaching its turn — slowing, as a pendulum slows at the top of its swing, before it comes back. If the pattern held — *if* — the long tide would turn somewhere in the last days of April. And the waist would go down, and down, to a little above twenty per cent, perhaps, on or about the first of May. And then, on its own, because the tide had turned, it would begin to come back.

He did not know that. He had four Narrowings and a hypothesis. He had a swell he could not explain and a sum on the back of an envelope. If he had seen it in a student's paper he would have written *suggestive* in the margin and sent it back. To know it he would have to watch: watch the swell's period, every night, for the next five weeks, and see whether it slowed and turned exactly when the sum said it would. If it did, he would know.

He sat on the archive floor in the lamplight among the paper rolls and the coffee mugs, and the fork lay on the paper beside his knee, and he thought: *nobody has to fly into it.* And then, more carefully: *nobody may have to.*

He picked up a clean sheet of paper and wrote at the top of it, *Dear Clare*. He had begun a great many letters that way over the years. He had sent very few of them. He wrote:

*I think I have found something. I can't tell anybody yet, because I'm not sure, and because the person I'd have to tell first is not someone who would use it kindly.
