So I'm telling you, because you are a long way away and won't read this for months, if I send it, which I probably won't. The Needle breathes, love. It has two breaths, a short one and a long one, and they beat against each other like strings. Your note is what let me hear it. The note you were born screaming, that I tuned everything to. I don't know if that makes any sense. It is four in the morning.*

*I'm coming home on the Providence. I'll bring the fork. I'll explain.*

He folded the letter and put it in his breast pocket with the others, and put the fork in the pocket of his jacket, and went out into the dome, where the harp was singing its thin high strained chord under the bruise of the mouth and Bettany was asleep at her console with her cheek on her slate. He stood and listened to the harp for a while. Then he woke her, gently, and sent her to bed, and sat down at his own console and opened a new file and named it, after some thought, *SWELL*, and locked it to his own key.

* * *

They were cutting a cradle into the Brannock's belly when Teo came onto Dock Four, and she knew before she saw Corrigan's face.

The tug sat in her cradle with her undersides opened up and a team of Concern fitters crawling over her with torches, welding a frame of new steel girders into the space where her sled-coupling had been. A cradle. For a load, carried under, released by a lever. Teo had seen the drawing on the dispatch board that morning, pinned up beside the notice: *TRIAL KEEPING. ONE VESSEL. VOLUNTEER PILOT ACCEPTED.* She had read it and gone cold and told herself it was somebody else.

He was sitting on a crate at the edge of the dock with Lulu beside him, eating fried salt-eel out of a paper cone and watching the fitters work. He saw Teo coming and stood up and wiped his hands on his coveralls and did not try to look as if it was anything but what it was.

"Lulu," he said, "go and see if Fat Diz's boy is on the dock office, would you? Ask him if he's got any bolts."

Lulu looked at her father and then at Teo, with the expression of a nine-year-old who knows exactly why she is being sent away, and went.

"You signed," Teo said.

"I signed."

"The *trial*. Corrigan. The trial. Not even the Keeping. She's sending one ship in, in the middle of the Narrowing, to see what happens. To *see*. You're the experiment."

"I'm the experiment," Corrigan agreed. "Experiment gets the same as everybody else. Debts cancelled in full. Mine, Lulu's, my father's. It's in writing. I've got it in writing, Teo, with the sunburst on." He patted his chest pocket. "And the trial goes on the second of April, so I don't have to sit around for five weeks thinking about it. I just go."

"You might not come back."

"I might not come back," he agreed again, gently. "I know that."

She wanted to hit him. She stood on the dock with the fitters' torches spitting behind him and wanted to hit him so badly that her hands shook. "Then *why*?"

He was quiet for a while. He looked over at the Brannock, at her opened belly and the new steel going in.

"I'm tired, Teo," he said.

"Everybody's tired."

"No. I mean — " He rubbed the back of his neck. "I'm forty. I've been flying glass since I was seventeen. I owe more now than I did when I started. Mira's been dead four years and I'm still paying for her dying. Lulu's going to start paying for it the day I stop. That's — that's it. That's the whole of my life, laid out in a line, and I can see the end of it from here." He looked at her. "And somebody's just come along and said, *fly one run, Corr. One. And none of that's true any more.* One run. And maybe I come back and maybe I don't, but either way, Lulu's free. Either way. Do you know what that's worth? You do. You know exactly what that's worth. You've got a chit on your console too."

She did know. That was the worst of it. She could feel it in her own chest like a hand: *cancelled in full.* Forty thousand six hundred and eighty marks, even after Lyle's six hundred. Struck off. Gone.

"I flew the inner markers with Lyle," she said. "The harp man. In the Mercy. You know what it's like out there? The shear? I nearly lost her. I nearly went *in*. And that was the edge, Corr. That was two weeks ago. It's narrower now."

"Then I'll be careful."

"You'll be — " She stopped. She found that she was crying, which she had not done in front of him in eleven years, and was furious about it. "You'll be *careful*."

He came and put his arms round her, there on the dock, with the fitters looking and pretending not to, and she let him, with her face in his shoulder, which smelled of salt-eel and coolant and him.

"Look after Lulu," he said into her hair. "If."

"Don't."

"If," he said. "Just if."

She went to the Lectern that evening. She had never been up the spindle before; contract pilots did not go to the Needle-ward end. Nobody stopped her. She found the dome by following the sound — a thin high strained singing that got louder as she climbed — and found Ambrose Lyle alone at a console under the bruise of the mouth with his headphones round his neck and a column of figures on his screen.

He looked up and saw her and his face did something complicated.

"Miss Kesh."

"Teo."

"Teo." He stood. "Is something wrong?"

"Corrigan Dace has volunteered for the trial," she said. "The Brannock. Second of April. He's my — he's a friend. I need to know. You're the one who'd know." She heard her voice and steadied it. "How dangerous is it? Really?"

Ambrose looked at her. He looked, she thought, like a man who had been asked a question he had been dreading, and had decided, somewhere in the dread, that he was going to answer it honestly.

"Very," he said. "The waist on the second will be about thirty-five per cent of nominal, if the trend holds. No one has ever flown a throat that narrow. The shear at the waist will be — I don't know. Worse than anything you saw at the inner markers. Much worse." He paused. "And the glass. When he releases it. Nobody knows what brine-glass does when you put it into the throat at the waist. Nobody's ever done it. It might do nothing. It might — " He spread his hands. "It's negative energy, Teo. Raw. In a place where everything is already bent. I don't know what it does."

"So he might die for nothing."

Ambrose was quiet.

"Is there anything," she said, "anything at all, that you know, that would stop it? That would make her call it off?"

Something moved in his face. She saw it. She saw him almost say something, and stop, and look away from her at the screen with its column of figures, and back.

"I don't know yet," he said. "I'm sorry. I don't know yet."

* * *

He told her the next morning, because of Teo Kesh's face.

He had not meant to. He had meant to wait five weeks and watch and know. But he had lain awake all night with the pilot's question in his ears — *is there anything at all that you know* — and the honest answer was that there was, a little; that there was a sum on the back of an envelope and a swell in four old paper traces, and that a man was going to fly into the Needle in six days and it might be for nothing. So at nine in the morning he went up to the Gallery, without an appointment, and Hesper Vane's secretary looked at his face and let him in.

He told her everything. The swell. The old traces, the fork, the drift corrected. The two rhythms. The long tide at its bottom. He laid the envelope on her desk with the sum on it. He told her it was not proven; that it was a hypothesis; that it would take five weeks to know. And he told her that if it was right, the Needle would reach its narrowest on or about the first of May and then begin to recover on its own, whatever anyone did, and that therefore there was no reason to send Corrigan Dace into the throat on the second of April. Or anyone. Ever.

She listened to all of it without interrupting, as she always did. She did not touch the envelope. When he had finished she sat for a while with her hands folded on the desk, looking at it.

"Who else have you told?" she said.

He blinked. "No one."

"Miss Shore?"

"No. She knows I've been looking at the old traces. Not why. Not what I found."

"Your daughter?"

He thought of the letter in his breast pocket, unsent, with the others. "No."

"Good," said Hesper Vane.

She picked up the envelope then, and turned it over, and looked at the sum on the back, and Ambrose watched her read it and understood, slowly and with a sensation like cold water rising in a room, that she was following it. All of it. Every step.

"If this is right," she said at last, "the waist will narrow steadily until the end of April. And then turn."

"If it's right."

"And between now and then? Does your swell do anything else? Are there smaller turns? Places where the waist pauses, or recovers a little, before it goes on down?"

"There — " He stopped. "There's a short ripple on the long one. A few days' period. The waist narrows in steps, not smoothly. It'll pause, sometimes. Recover a point or two. Then go on down."

"When is the next pause?"

He looked at her.

"The trial is on the second of April," Hesper Vane said evenly. "If there is a pause in the narrowing at or about the second of April, then the trial will be followed by a pause in the narrowing. People will see the trial, and then they will see the throat hold. Perhaps even open, a little." She laid the envelope down. "That would be very helpful, Ambrose. You understand that? That would be extremely helpful. To everyone. It would make the Keeping work."

"It wouldn't *work*. It would — it would just be timed to — "

"It would be seen to work," she said. "Which is what matters to the people who will be asked to fly it. And to cut for it. And not to burn Anchorage down in the meantime." She looked at him with her steady grey eyes. "When is the next pause?"

He said nothing.

"Ambrose."

"I'd like to go back to the Lectern now, Director."

She held his eyes for a long moment. Then she nodded, as if he had answered.

"Of course," she said. "Thank you for bringing this to me. It's important work. I'd like you to keep working on it, and I'd like it to stay between the two of us until it's proven. Lock the file. You've locked it already, I expect. Good." She picked up her slate. "I'll ask Miss Shore for the short-term projections. She can read a trace as well as anyone, I'm told. You trained her."

He went out. He walked back down the spindle to the Lectern with his hand in his jacket pocket round the fork, and he could not afterward remember a single step of the way.

# Chapter 8: The Blank Year

*The Severance · 2608*

It was the archivist's duty, at every Keeping, to read the First.

Not the whole of it. Nobody had the whole of it. But on the night of the Long Night, while the Keepers flew and the Reach sang, the archivist stood in the harp hall beside the High Warden and read aloud from the harp records of the First Keeping — the waist's narrowing and its holding and its opening again, three hundred and seventeen years ago, in the numbers the First Warden's own readers had written down — so that the Reach would hear that it had been done before and had worked. Adaeze had read them at the Twenty-Ninth, eleven years ago, her first Keeping as archivist, with her voice shaking. She meant to read them better this time. So on the twenty-eighth of October, with thirty-eight days to go, she went down into the deep stacks to find them and copy them out fair.

The First Keeping's records were where they had always been: in the oldest rack, at the far end of the archive, in the cold, on spools of a crystal so old it had gone faintly yellow. The first spool's label was in the slanting hand of the order. *Harp. 2 May 2291. First Day After the Keeping.* She took it down and fed it into the old reader and listened to it while she copied: the throat's chord, steadying, opening; the readers' voices, flat with exhaustion, calling numbers. *Waist twenty-four. Twenty-six. Holding.* She knew it by heart.

It was only when she went to the rack for the spool before it — the Long Night itself, the night of the Keeping — that she found there was not one.

She stood in front of the rack for some time. Then she went and got a lamp.

The spool for the second of May was the first spool in the rack. She had always known that, the way you know the first page of a book is the first page, without ever thinking about it. There was nothing before it. No Long Night. No first of May. No April, no March, none of the readings from the weeks before the First Keeping, when the waist was narrowing and the First Warden heard its hunger and the Reach answered. The archive began on the morning after. It began, Adaeze realised, standing in the cold with the lamp in her hand, as if the Keeping had worked and somebody had then started writing it down.

But the Concession had had readers. The liturgy said so. *The readers of the Concession heard it narrow.* There had been a harp — this harp, the First Warden's harp, singing in the hall above her head. There had been forty years of records before the First Keeping. Where were they?

She went to the catalogue. The catalogue of the archive was itself a historical document, three hundred years of archivists' entries in a dozen hands, and its first page was older than the Compact. She found the entry she wanted in the very first column. *Concession harp records, 2247 to 1 May 2291: transferred to the keeping of the First Warden.*

And beside it, in a different and later hand, very small: *Not returned.*

That was all.

She went back to the rack with the lamp and looked at it properly, as an archivist looks, and saw what she had walked past for twenty years. At the end of the oldest rack, before the first spool, there was a space a hand's breadth wide where no spools were; and on the rack's old steel, in that space, there were marks. Bright scratches, long since dulled, where something had been pulled out hard. Clamps, sheared. The bolt-holes for a drawer that was not there.

The older archivists had a joke, when she was a novice. When you could not find a thing, they said it was in the Blank Year. It meant the time before the First Keeping, about which the order had nothing: nothing in the stacks, nothing in the catalogue, nothing anywhere but the liturgy and a single loose sheet in the reliquary inventory, a reader's description of the throat that someone had once found tucked into a book and kept. *Drawn in like an iris.* Adaeze had laughed at the joke, at twenty-four. She had never once thought to ask why there should be a Blank Year at all, in an order whose whole purpose was to keep the record.

She did not go back up to the harp hall until evening. When she did, it was to clean the Fork.

That was her duty too. Once a month, and before every Draw and every Keeping, the archivist took the Founder's Fork from its cushion in the reliquary and cleaned it with a soft cloth and a little oil, and laid it back. She had done it two hundred times. She went into the alcove behind the iron grille and lifted it from the grey velvet and held it to the lamp, as she always did, and turned it: fifteen centimetres of old brass, the tines worn bright, the stem's engraving worn to ghosts. FOR CL. She had always loved that. *For Clarity.* As if the First Warden had cut the order's whole purpose into the instrument with which the hunger was first heard.

She cleaned it. The harp sang behind her in the hall, its long wide drone. And when she had finished, standing at the grille with the oiled Fork in her hand and the drone in her ears, Adaeze Quill did a thing that no archivist had done in her lifetime, and very likely in anyone's. She struck it.

Just lightly. Against the heel of her hand, the way you would strike any fork. It was a tuning fork. It had been made to be struck.

The note came up out of it, pure and thin, and went out into the hall, and met the harp.

They did not agree.

It was not much. A hair. A quarter of a tone, perhaps less. But the Fork's note and the harp's drone were not the same note, and where they met they fought: a slow wavering throb in the air, *wah, wah, wah*, the sound two strings make when they are almost but not quite in tune, beating against each other. She stood in the alcove and listened to it beat until the Fork's note died away in her hand.

The harp was tuned every morning to the Hunger Tone. The Hunger Tone was the note the First Warden had heard the Needle's hunger in. The Fork was the instrument with which the First Warden had heard it.

And they were not the same note.

She laid the Fork back on its cushion with great care. Her hands were steady; she noted that, distantly, the way she would have noted the condition of a spool. She went back to her little office off the archive and closed the door and sat down at her desk and opened, not the order's daybook, but the small private notebook that she had kept since she was a novice, in which she wrote the things she did not know what to do with. She wrote the date. She wrote:

*No harp records before 2 May 2291. Forty-four years transferred to the First Warden. Not returned. Rack forced.*

*The Fork is not tuned to the Hunger Tone. I struck it. They beat.*

She looked at the two lines. Then she wrote a third, and looked at it, and did not cross it out:

*Why would anyone tune the harp away from the Fork?*

* * *

The runner was a fast little packet out of Corrie Rock, and it tried to outrun the *Vigil* across the Shoals approaches, and it very nearly did.

"She's good," said Marsh at tactical, with reluctant admiration, as the packet jinked behind a tumbling ice-rock and came out the other side on a vector nobody had predicted. "She's really good, Captain. That's a Navy pilot. That's somebody we trained."

"Then she knows what happens next," said Juno. "Forward battery. Across her bow. Close."

The battery fired. The packet's pilot knew what happened next. She killed her drive and put her hands up, as far as a ship can, and the *Vigil* came alongside.

There were three aboard. Two were Shoals smugglers, sullen and scared, who sat where they were told. The third was a woman of fifty in a grey cardigan, who came out of the packet's tiny hold with her hands raised and a data slate held up in one of them, high, so that the boarding party could see it, the way you would hold up a white flag.

"Commander Asante," she said, as soon as Juno came through the lock. "I asked them to find me your ship. I've been trying to reach you for nine days. My name is Mara Teague. I was a lay clerk in the Draw office at the Compact Hall for nineteen years. I need to show you something."

They put her in Juno's day cabin, because it was the only room on the *Vigil* with a door that locked from the outside. Juno sat across the little table from her with Marsh standing at the door. Teague put the slate on the table between them and pushed it across with one finger.

"That's every name drawn in the last four Draws," she said. "The Twenty-Sixth to the Twenty-Ninth. And beside each one, how it was drawn."

"Drawn from the Wheel," Juno said. "That's how they're drawn."

"Some of them." Teague's voice was steady, low, a clerk's voice, precise. She had the hands of someone who had spent her life with paper. "Commander, I prepared the foils for the Wheel. That was my job. Every name in the Reach between eighteen and forty, on a strip of steel foil, cut and stamped and counted and fed into the drum. Every name the same weight, every foil the same size, so that the Wheel is fair. That's what the Articles say." She leaned forward. "The Shoals foils were cut at half-thickness. For the last four Draws. On written instruction from the High Warden's office. Thinner foil rides higher in the drum when it turns. Every clerk in the office knew. We were told it was a matter of security — the Shoals had to be seen to bear their share. Look at the numbers. The Shoals are a sixth of the Reach. Of the last thirty-six Keepers, nineteen were Shoals names."

Juno looked at the slate. Nineteen of thirty-six names had a small mark beside them.

"That's the first thing," said Teague. "The second thing is worse."

"Go on."

"At every Draw," said Mara Teague, "there is one foil that is not fed into the Wheel by the clerks. It is given to the High Warden in an envelope, by his secretary, on the morning of the Draw. He carries it in his sleeve. And when the Wheel stops and he reaches in, one of the times he reaches in, he does not take a foil from the drum." She was watching Juno's face. "We called it the sleeve name. Every clerk knew that too. Nobody spoke of it. It was — it was the price of the Draw, we told each other. The Wheel was fair, nearly. The High Warden just needed to be sure of one name. For the good of the Reach." She put one fingertip on the slate. "Those are the sleeve names. One a Draw. I copied them from the secretary's file before I ran, two years ago. Look at who they are."

Juno looked.

*Twenty-Sixth: Orla Venning, daughter of Assembly Member Tomas Venning (Treasury Committee; voted against the Wardens' appropriation, 2573).*

*Twenty-Seventh: Hal Brisk, son of the Shoals delegate Ana Brisk (moved the motion for Shoals quota relief, 2584).*

*Twenty-Eighth: Imre Saltash, brother of Commodore Lise Saltash (refused escort duty for the Twenty-Seventh Keeping on grounds of conscience).*

*Twenty-Ninth: Callum Calder, brother of Lieutenant Rhee Calder (Academy record: formal complaint to the Wardens' liaison regarding the treatment of Shoals cadets, 2596).*

She read the last line three times. Her mouth had gone dry.

"Rhee filed a complaint," she said. "In our third year. About the Shoals cadets. They were being given the worst postings. I — I signed it. Half our year signed it." She looked up. "Rhee's was the name on top."

"Yes," said Teague quietly. "That's how it works. It's never you. It's always the one you love."

The cabin was very quiet. Marsh, at the door, had gone pale.

"Why are you showing me this?" Juno said.

"Because I heard who was drawn seventh at the Thirtieth." Teague held her gaze. "I wasn't there. I don't have this year's file. I can't prove it. But I know how the Wheel works, Commander, and I know who your mother is, and I know what vote is before the Assembly this week. And I thought — " For the first time her voice wavered. "I thought, if I could get it to one person who could do something. Just one. Not the League. Not the papers. Somebody inside. Somebody who'd have a reason."

The comm on Juno's desk chimed. Squadron. Admiral Greaves.

She took it with the slate face-down under her hand. Greaves had been told about the runner. He had been told who was on it — the clerk's name was on a Navy watch-list as a League courier — and he was very clear. Mara Teague was a defector to the enemy in time of war and a carrier of enemy materials. She was to be transferred, with all materials, to the Wardens' guard at the Anchorhold, who had jurisdiction over offences against the Draw. The *Vigil* would make the transfer on her way in to her station. He was very clear about the materials.

"Understood, sir," said Juno.

When the screen went dark she sat for a moment with her hand flat on the slate. Then she said, without looking round, "Danny. Go and get me a clean slate from stores. A blank one. Take your time."

Marsh looked at her. Then he went, and took his time.

She copied it. All of it. It took four minutes. When Marsh came back with the blank slate she gave him the copy and said, "Put that somewhere it won't be logged. Not your quarters. Somewhere stupid," and he went white and nodded and put it inside his jacket, and she gave the original back to Teague.

"They're going to take this from you," she said. "At the Anchorhold. I can't stop that."

"I know," said Teague. She looked at the slate in her hands. "I always knew. Thank you for reading it."

The *Vigil* docked at the Anchorhold the next morning. Juno walked Mara Teague down the gangway herself, and handed her and her slate to a Warden guard-captain with a polite, bored face, and signed the transfer, and watched them walk her away across the hangar between two guards, small in her grey cardigan, not looking back.

Two days later, on the evening watch, a routine notice came through the Navy channel from the Wardens' office: *Mara Teague, held for offences against the Draw, died in custody of a heart condition on 31 October. Next of kin notified.*

Juno read it and logged it and went to her cabin and was sick in the little steel basin, quietly, so that no one would hear.

* * *

Pell called on the first of November, on the Anchorhold link, at the hour the nine were allowed calls.

She looked well. That was the first thing. Her face filled the little screen in Juno's cabin, flushed and bright-eyed, her hair cut short now in the Keepers' fashion, the bead at her throat on its black cord, and behind her a common room with a window on the mouth and somebody laughing off-screen.

"You look terrible," Pell said cheerfully. "Are you eating?"

"I'm eating."

"You're not. You've got your not-eating face. Mum's got it too, I saw her on the Assembly broadcast. You've both got it." Pell grinned. "I'm eating enough for all three of us. They feed us like we're being fattened for something."

"Pell."

"It's a joke, Juno." Pell's grin softened. "Sorry. It's what we do up here. You have to. Ezra says if you don't laugh about it you have to think about it, and if you think about it you'll go out of the airlock without a suit." She glanced off-screen. "He's very funny. He does an impression of the flight instructor that makes Tavish laugh, and Tavish doesn't laugh at anything. You'd like him."

They talked for twenty minutes. Pell did most of the talking. The simulators, which she was good at, it turned out — she had a feel for the throat's line, the instructors said; she had the second-best scores in the nine. Abbe Sorrell, the Shoals girl, who had started eating again and had told Pell, at three in the morning, about her mother's ice-farm on Corrie Rock. Sister Quill, who came to the harp hall most nights and sat with Pell under the dome and talked to her about the records, and had looked at the painting for a long time and said it matched something old. The harp itself. *I go and sit with it every night. I can't sleep anyway. I just sit and listen. It's like being inside it already.*

Juno listened, and nodded, and said the things you say, and the slate lay face-down on the desk beside the screen where Pell could not see it, with its list of names in a dead clerk's careful hand.

"You sound strange," Pell said, near the end.

"I'm tired."

"No. Strange. You keep looking at something." Pell peered at the screen as if she could see round its edge. "What's wrong?"

*They chose you,* Juno thought. *The Needle didn't. A man chose you, with a foil in his sleeve, to make our mother vote for a war. And I can't prove it, and if I tell you, you'll either not believe me or you'll believe me, and I don't know which is worse.*

"Nothing's wrong," she said. "The war's wrong. I'm tired. That's all."

Pell looked at her through the screen, with the mouth of the Needle behind her in the common-room window, huge and dark. Then she smiled, the listening smile.

"Thirty-four days," she said. "Then you'll be at the Anchorhold. On escort. You'll be right there when I go in. Sister Quill says the escort ships are close enough to see the skiffs go into the mouth. You'll see me go." Her voice was very gentle. "I'm glad it's you. I'm glad you'll be the one watching."

The link closed. Juno sat in the dark of her cabin until the watch changed.

Then she turned the slate over, and read the list again — all four Draws, all four sleeve names, the four careful lines of who each one had belonged to and why — and then, because she could not help it, she read the last line again, the one about Rhee's brother, and the complaint that half their year had signed, and she put her hand over her mouth.

*It's never you,* the clerk had said. *It's always the one you love.*

She thought of Rhee's message, sent on the old cadet band. *Ask them how the names are chosen.* He had known. Perhaps not this, not the sleeve, not the foils. But he had known something, for eleven years, and he had tried to tell her in the only way he could.

She did not answer him. Not yet. But she took a blank sheet of paper from her desk drawer — paper, not a slate, nothing that could be logged — and wrote on it in pencil the frequency of the old cadet band, and folded it small, and put it in the breast pocket of her uniform, over her heart, where she would know it was there.

# Chapter 9: The Choke

*The Ebb · 2937*

The new wire would not lie down.

Lucan had strung four courses of it now, the whole of the girl's spool, and every morning he tuned them to the Hunger Tone the way he had been taught, standing with his eyes shut and the note in his throat, turning the pegs a hair at a time until the wire stopped fighting and lay down inside the sound. And every evening, when he came back to the hall, the new courses had risen up again. Not drifted. That was what he kept coming back to, lying awake in his cell. Old wire drifted: it went slack, it went flat, it crept, and you heard it creep. This was not that. The new wire stayed exactly as tight as he had left it. It was the *note* that would not hold. As if the wire knew some other note, and kept leaning toward it, the way a compass needle leans north however you turn the box.

And the girl's glass had hummed at an old woman's cittern, and lain dead in his palm at the harp.

He thought about that for ten nights. On the eleventh, a little after two in the morning, he got up and put on his robe and went down through the dark spindle to the harp hall, and stood in front of the reliquary grille, looking at the Fork.

It lay on its grey cushion in the lamplight the way it had lain all his life: fifteen centimetres of brass, worn bright at the tips. *The First Warden's Fork.* He had been taught that it was the holiest thing in the Reach. He had been taught that it was struck once at every Keeping and never otherwise touched except by the Prior's own hand, to be cleaned. He had been taught, by the Prior, at seventeen, kneeling on this floor, that the Hunger Tone was the note the First Warden had heard in the Needle, and that the Fork was the instrument with which the First Warden had heard it.

He had never once been taught that they were the same note. He had assumed it. Everybody had.

The grille was locked. It had been locked for as long as he had been at the Anchor, and the Prior had the key on a chain round his neck. But the Anchor had been built for six thousand people and was lived in by twelve, and Lucan had spent twelve years repairing it with whatever came to hand, and he knew that the grille's hinge-pins on the left side had rusted half through in the damp of the hall and could be lifted out with a pair of pliers and a prayer. He had brought the pliers. He said the prayer anyway.

The Fork was heavier than he had expected. Cold. He held it in his palm in the lamplight and his hand was shaking.

He went out into the middle of the hall, in front of the harp, under the huge dark leaning dome of the mouth, and struck it against the heel of his hand.

The note came up out of it, thin and clean. It went out into the hall and met the harp's long drone, and the two of them fought. *Wah. Wah. Wah.* The slow throb of two notes not quite the same. It went on until the Fork died in his hand.

And the four new courses — the girl's wire, the Compact wire — *rang*. Not much. A faint bright shimmer in the chord, as if they had heard something they had been waiting for.

Lucan stood in the middle of the hall with the Fork in his hand.

Then he took off his robe, because it was in the way, and knelt down at the base of the frame in his shirt, and began.

It took him the rest of the night. Nine courses, two hundred and sixteen wires, each one tuned not to his own throat but to the Fork: strike it, hold the stem to the frame, listen to the beat, turn the peg a hair, strike it again, until the beat slowed and slowed and stopped and the wire and the Fork were one note. It was slow and his hands were cold and twice he had to stop and sit back on his heels and breathe, because what he was doing was, by every rule he had ever been taught, a sacrilege — retuning the First Warden's harp, with the First Warden's relic, against the Hunger Tone. He did it anyway. Somewhere around the fifth course he stopped being frightened. Somewhere around the seventh the harp began to sound different, and he stopped thinking about anything at all.

At a little before six, with the grey light of the mouth beginning to brighten in the dome the way it did at what the Anchor called morning, he turned the last peg on the last wire of the ninth course, and stood up, and listened.

It was not the Hunger Tone.

He had known that it would not be. He had not known what it would be instead. The harp did not drone now; it *rang*. The long wide many-stranded chord he had listened to every day of his life, the sound he had always heard as a held breath, an ache, a hunger, was gone, and in its place there was something he had never heard: a deep, hard, layered resonance, like a great bell struck very far away, and struck again, and again, each time a little differently, so that it seemed to have no end. Under it the old throat-noise still ran, faint now, small, like wind in the cracks of a door. The bell was over it. The bell was enormous.

He went to the old reader's console at the side of the hall and woke it, and its screen came up, and the waist trace drew itself across the glass in pale green as the harp sang into it.

For twelve years he had looked at that trace. Every reader had. A long line — the throat — narrowing toward a point, the waist, and at the waist a narrow gap, the passage, which had been getting narrower Narrowing by Narrowing for as long as anybody could remember. Nineteen per cent of nominal. A starving throat. An empty, closing door.

The trace on the screen now was not that.

The throat narrowed toward the waist as it always had. But at the waist there was no gap. There was a shape. A mass — he had no other word — filling the waist almost wall to wall, so dense that the trace went bright and solid across it, and so large that the throat bulged around it like a gullet around something swallowed whole. It was not smooth. Its outline was ridged, banded, layered, like the side of a cut cliff, like the root of the girl's shard. And through the middle of it, not where the passage should have been but squeezed off to one side, crooked, there ran a single narrow crack: all that was left of the way through.

He sat and looked at it while the harp rang its great slow bell above him.

The throat was not empty. It was full. All the readings of all the readers for all the years he knew of — *the waist at nineteen*, *the waist at twenty-four*, *it is starving, it is closing* — had been readings taken with the harp out of tune; and out of tune by exactly enough, he understood now, slowly, his skin going cold, that the ringing of the thing at the waist fell between the strings and was not heard. Tuned to the Hunger Tone, the harp heard a gap closing. Tuned to the Fork, it heard what was closing it.

"It's not starving," Lucan said aloud, to nobody. His voice was very small in the hall. "It's choking."

* * *

The message came to the bone-pickers' board at the Pans the next morning, scrawled on a scrap of order vellum and sent down on the Anchor's supply shuttle in a sealed tube addressed, in a hasty hand, to *Isra Venn, Gristle, bone-picker — URGENT — please*. It said: *Bring the glass. Come today. Don't tell anyone. — L.*

She went. She went without Pike, because Pike had a fever, and because something in the hand made her want to go alone. It took her six hours. The Gristle's heater died twice on the way and she kicked it back to life twice, and she came in to Dock Four with her teeth chattering, and he was waiting on the dock, and he looked as though he had not slept in two days.

"You came," he said.

"You said *please*."

"I did. Sorry. I'm — come and see. Come and see, and then tell me I'm mad. I'd quite like someone to tell me I'm mad."

He took her up to the harp hall at something close to a run, and at the iron door he stopped, and put his hand on her arm to stop her too, and said, "Listen."

She listened.

It came through the door before she was even in the hall: a sound she had never heard. Low, huge, layered. Like a bell, but no bell she knew — like a bell the size of a town struck once at the bottom of a sea, and the sea carrying the sound up to you slowly, in waves, each wave a little different from the last. She felt it in the bones of her face. The shard against her chest, under her shirt, on its cord, gave a single sharp *tick* against her breastbone, like a fingernail on glass, and then began to hum.

She took it out. It was humming in her palm like a live thing, so hard that it buzzed against her skin. Stronger than at Hanne's cittern. Much stronger.

Lucan looked at it and let out a long breath.

"I tuned the harp to the Fork," he said.

She stared at him. "You said it was a relic."

"It is a relic. I tuned the harp to it anyway. All night." He pushed the door open. "Come and look."

He showed her the screen. He explained it twice, the second time more slowly, with his hands, drawing the throat in the air between them: the long line, the narrowing, the waist. The gap that every reader since the Quiet had been watching close. And then the thing that was not a gap — the great ridged mass on the screen filling the waist almost from wall to wall, the crooked thread of passage squeezed round its edge.

"That's glass," Isra said.

He looked at her.

"That's glass." She was sure. She did not know how she was sure, except that she had spent ten years cutting it off the hulls of dead ships and knew its shapes the way Hanne knew songs. She held up the humming shard and turned it so the light went into its root, into the fine pale bands packed thin as pages. "Look at the edge of your thing. On the screen. Look at the lines. It's the same as this. It's layered like this." She heard her own voice rising. "It's glass, Lucan. There's a — a lump of glass in the throat the size of — "

"The size of the Anchor," he said quietly. "Rather larger, I think."

She lowered the shard. It hummed on in her fist.

"The Returns," she said slowly.

"Yes."

"They're all coated in it. They all come out with it grown on them backwards. Swept back. Like hair in a wind." She was working it out as she said it. "Like they were *pushed*. Like something pushed them out through all that, through the glass, and the glass grew on them while they were — "

"The throat's trying to clear itself," said Lucan. "I think. I think that's what the Returns are. The throat flexes, every Narrowing, and things that are stuck in it get squeezed loose, a few at a time, and come out through the mouth. Ships, mostly. Whatever's been put in it. And the glass — " He rubbed his eyes under his spectacles. "I don't know about the glass. I don't know why there's so much. I don't know where it came from. I don't know anything except that for at least as long as the order's books go back, every reader at the Anchor has been looking at that thing through a harp tuned so it couldn't be heard, and calling it hunger."

They stood side by side in front of the screen with the great slow bell ringing over them.

"The Lamplighter's going to put a hundred skiffs into that," Isra said. "A hundred. With every bit of glass in the Reach."

Lucan said nothing.

"Into *that*." She pointed at the screen with the humming shard. "Into a throat that's choking on glass, she's going to — "

"Yes," Lucan said. "I know."

* * *

The Prior came into the hall at noon, on his two sticks, alone, and stood in the doorway, and listened.

Lucan had known he would come. The whole station could hear the harp now; it rang through the spindle like a bell through a church, and the other Wardens had been gathering in the passages all morning, whispering, and not coming in. The Prior did not whisper. He stood in the doorway in his grey with the frayed cuffs and his white head lifted, listening, for a long time. Then his eyes went to the reliquary grille, with its hinge-pins lying on the floor beneath it, and the empty grey cushion.

"Where is it?" he said.

Lucan took the Fork out of his sleeve and held it up.

Isra, beside him, felt the old man's look go over her and dismiss her, the way you would dismiss a chair. It rested on Lucan. It rested on the Fork. Then, slowly, on the screen.

"Explain," said Prior Saar.

Lucan explained. He did it better the third time; Isra listened to him do it and thought that he had found the words now, standing in front of the man who had taught him to sing the Hunger Tone at seventeen. The new wire that would not lie down. The shard. The Fork, and the beat. The night's work. The bell. The thing in the throat. He took the Prior to the screen and showed him, and the old man stood leaning on his sticks and looked at it, at the great banded mass in the waist and the crooked thread of the passage, for a very long time without speaking.

"Twelve years," the Prior said finally, to the screen. "I've looked at that trace for fifty-eight years, Brother. Twelve with you." He did not say anything else for a while.

"Prior," said Lucan. "The Lamplighter — "

"I know."

"If she takes a hundred skiffs into that and releases — "

"I *know*, Lucan." The old man turned from the screen. His face, Isra saw, was grey, and very calm, the calm of somebody who has been hit and has not yet fallen down. "Put it back."

Lucan stared at him.

"The Fork. Put it back on its cushion, and put the pins back in the grille. Then retune the harp. To the Hunger Tone. All of it. Tonight."

"Prior — "

"Her reader is coming tomorrow." Saar's voice was perfectly level. "The Lamplighter's. For the readings. You know she is; she said so. He will come into this hall and he will hear that." He moved his head slightly toward the ringing harp. "And then he will look at that screen. And then he will go back to Tallow and tell Mercy Oduya that the Wardens of the Throat have desecrated the First Warden's Fork, and tuned the harp to a heresy, and are saying that the Needle is not hungry but sick — sick with *glass*, Brother. Sick with what she has spent twenty-five years telling the Reach to give it. Sick with what her son flew into it." He did not raise his voice. "What do you think she will do?"

Lucan was silent.

"I'll tell you what I think," said the Prior. "I think she will come here with her black coats, and her Deacon, and she will burn this hall. And the harp. And the Fork. And us. And then she will fly her hundred skiffs into the throat anyway, with nobody left alive who can read it, and that will be the end of it." He shifted on his sticks. "There are twelve of us. I keep telling you. *Twelve.*"

"So we say nothing."

"So we wait. So we think. So we do not hand her the match." Saar held out his hand, old and shaking, palm up. "The Fork, Brother."

Lucan looked at the Fork in his own hand. Isra watched him look at it. Then he stepped forward and laid it in the Prior's palm, and the old man's fingers closed over it.

"Retune the harp," Saar said. "Tonight." He turned toward the door on his sticks, and stopped, and without looking round said, in a different voice, an old man's voice, almost gentle: "It says *For Clarity*, you know. On the stem. I used to think that was the finest thing ever written on anything." He was quiet for a moment. "Retune the harp."

He went out. They heard his sticks go away down the passage, tap and tap, very slowly.

Lucan stood in the middle of the hall with his empty hands at his sides. Isra waited. After a long time he went to the console and, without a word, plugged a little data-stick into its side, and copied the trace onto it, and pulled it out, and put it in her hand, and closed her fingers round it.

"Not here," he said. "Not in the Anchor. Somewhere stupid. Somewhere nobody would look."

"I live in the Gutter," said Isra. "Everywhere's somewhere stupid."

* * *

She got back to the Pans after midnight and found the bone-pickers' dock full of black coats.

They had come at the shift-change again. They had come down on the dock with lamps and crowbars and gone through every skiff tied up there — the Coopers', the Mallon girls', old Teodric's, the Gristle's empty berth — and taken up the deck plates and opened the tanks and cut into the insulation, looking for glass. They had found some. Bone-pickers always kept some back. They had found enough to be angry about the rest, and they had dragged three people out onto the dock and beaten them in front of the others, slowly, under the lamps, so that everybody would remember.

One of the three had been Pike.

He had not even been on the dock. He had come down from the Gutter with his fever on him, the Mallon girls said, because someone had run up Rope Street shouting that the Lantern was on the boats, and he had thought of the Gristle; and he had walked out onto the dock in his shirt, coughing, to stand in front of her empty berth, as though there were something there to protect. And the boys with the lamps had asked him where the Gristle was and where her glass was, and he had told them he didn't know, and they had asked him again.

Isra found him on Hanne's bed in the room at the end of Rope Street, with Hanne sitting beside him holding a wet rag to his face. His ribs were strapped with strips torn off one of Hanne's old shawls. One eye was shut. When he breathed it whistled, and when he coughed it was so bad that Hanne had to hold him up.

"Didn't tell them," he said, when he saw Isra. It came out in a wet whisper. "Didn't know. You didn't tell me where you were going. Good girl."

She sat down on the floor by the bed with the data-stick in her fist and her other hand on his big broken hand, and for a long time she did not say anything at all.

Hanne watched her over the rag with her bird's eyes.

"Well?" said the old woman at last, low. "Where *were* you going?"

Isra opened her fist and looked at the little stick lying in her palm.

"To see what's in the throat," she said. "Gran. I saw it. It's not hungry." She looked up. "It's full of glass. It's choking on it. And she's going to give it more."

Hanne did not say anything. She put the rag down on the edge of the bowl and reached across Pike and took her cittern from where it leaned against the wall, and set it on her knee, and plucked the low string, once, very softly, so as not to wake anybody in the tunnel.

Inside Isra's shirt, against her skin, the shard hummed back.

# Chapter 10: Trial

*The Concession · 2291*

They gave Corrigan a speech, and a band, and a blessing from a Concession chaplain nobody had ever seen before, and a sunburst pin for his collar, and they gave Teo the inner markers.

She had asked for it. Hesper Vane's office had said that the trial would go with one escort to the edge of the shear — a pathfinder, to fly the line ahead of the Brannock as far as the markers, so that Corrigan would not have to read the outer waves and the throat both — and the Concession throat-haulers had all found reasons to be in the yards that day, and Teo had gone up the spindle to the Director's outer office and put her name down in pencil on the form before she could think about it. Nobody had argued. Nobody else had wanted it.

So at eleven in the morning on the second of April she sat in the Small Mercy at the last beacon of the in-lane, with her mains feathering and the Needle filling the canopy, and waited for him.

The mouth had changed since she had last been out here with Ambrose. She could see it from the beacon, before the shear even began: the wall of light at the rim was brighter, tighter, the coils of starlight around it wound so close that they had blurred into a single band of grey-gold fire, and the bruise at the centre was smaller and darker and deeper, like a pupil in a bright room. It looked, she thought, like an eye that was squinting. Like an eye in pain.

"Mercy, Lectern." Ambrose's voice in her ear, very calm. He had asked to read the line himself. "The waist is at thirty-four. Shear is heavy from two to five o'clock, lighter from the left. I'll call you in. Is the Brannock with you?"

"Coming up now."

He came up on her starboard side, the Brannock, ugly and familiar, her new cradle slung under her belly like a pregnancy, packed with two tonnes of the best glass Shaft Nine had ever cut. Teo could see the cradle-frame through the gap in the hull, and the dull blue gleam of the crated glass inside it, and the white release lever painted on the frame where Corrigan would be able to see it through the floor-port of his cockpit. They had made him practise pulling it. He had told her so the night before, laughing, in the Lantern, with Lulu asleep against him. *Thirty times. Like I might forget which way's down.*

"Morning," he said on the private channel.

"Morning."

"Lovely day for it."

"Shut up, Corr."

"I've got a pin," he said. "Did you see? They gave me a pin. I've never had a pin." A pause. "Lulu's on the gallery with Mother Salome. She's got her bolts. She said she'd bring them for luck."

Teo did not trust her voice. She ran the mains up.

"Lectern, Mercy. Brannock's with me. Going in."

They went in.

It was worse than before. She had known it would be. The first shear-waves came at them two kilometres past the beacon and they were not waves, they were walls, coming one on top of the other with no space between, and the Small Mercy bucked and shuddered and screamed in every seam, and twice Teo felt the tow-hook in the stern tear half out of its mount and hang there by a bolt. Ambrose called them. His voice in her ear never changed. *Two o'clock, high, thirty seconds. Left, now, now. Hold her. Big one from behind in fifty, turn into it, Teo, turn into it.* She turned into them. Behind her on her stern camera the Brannock came on in her wake, heavier, slower, lurching under the weight of the cradle, Corrigan following her line so close that she could see the scratch on his canopy where Lulu had once dropped a spanner.

At the inner markers she stopped. Those were the orders. The two dead beacons hung in the fire on either side of her like drowned men, and she brought the Mercy round between them and held her there, nose into the mouth, mains screaming, and the Brannock came up beside her and slowed and hung there too, for a moment, side by side, the way they had hung at a hundred markers in twenty years waiting for Approach to wave them through.

"Well," said Corrigan.

"Corr."

"Don't." He said it quite gently. "I know. Me too." She could hear him breathing. "Look after her."

"I will."

"If."

"If," Teo said. "Just if."

The Brannock went forward.

She watched it go. She would watch it go for the rest of her life; she would see it every time she closed her eyes in the dark for the rest of her life. A small ugly tug with a cradle under its belly, going away from her into the fire. It got smaller. It did not get smaller the way things get smaller when they go away from you; it got smaller *wrong* — stretched, pulled long, thinned to a line of light and then not even that, as the throat took it, until it was a bright scratch on the face of the dark and then a point and then nothing she could see at all.

"I'm in," said Corrigan's voice in her ear. It was faint, and it had begun to waver, rising and falling as if he were speaking from the bottom of a well. "I'm in the throat. Lectern, are you getting me?"

"We have you, Brannock." Ambrose. "You're on the line. Hold your heading. The waist is nine kilometres ahead."

"It's — " A rush of static. "It's not like anything. Teo. Are you there?"

"I'm here."

"It's not like anything. The walls are — it's folded. It's all folded light. Like the inside of a shell. And it goes in. It goes in and in." His voice went away and came back. "There's a — it's narrowing. Up ahead. It's drawing in. Like an iris. Like an eye shutting." A long breath. "And there's a light in the middle of it, Teo. In the middle where it shuts. A light. I've got no name for it."

"Three kilometres," said Ambrose. "Two. Brannock, you're at the waist. Release when you're ready."

"Releasing."

Teo did not hear the release. But she saw it.

The mouth flared. Not the rim — the dark at the centre, the bruised pupil, which had never in her life shown anything but dark; it went white, for an instant, a hard bright white like a cutter's arc, and then a sickly blue, and then dark again, and a shockwave came out of it that hit the Small Mercy like a thrown rock and threw her back on her tail between the two dead beacons and set every alarm on the console screaming at once. Through the channel, through the static, she heard the harp in the Lectern — Ambrose had his line open to it — let out a sound she had never heard an instrument make, a scream, high and thin and long, all its strings at once.

"Brannock," said Ambrose. "Brannock, report."

Static.

"Brannock, Lectern. Corrigan. Report."

"Corr," said Teo. "Corr, talk to me."

Static. And under the static, very faint, faint as something heard through a wall — she would never be sure, afterward, that she had heard it at all — a voice, not saying anything. Breathing. Or laughing. Or something else.

Then nothing.

She waited at the inner markers for three hours. The orders were twenty minutes. At thirty minutes Approach told her to return. At an hour the Director's office told her to return. At two hours Ambrose said, very quietly, on the private channel, *Teo. Come home. Please,* and she switched him off. She sat between the two dead beacons with the Mercy screaming and shuddering under her, the tow-hook hanging by a single bolt, every alarm on the console red, and watched the dark at the centre of the mouth for a small ugly tug with a scratch on its canopy coming back out of it.

At three hours the Mercy's port attitude jet failed, and she had to come home, because if she did not she would go in herself.

* * *

The waist came up two points at a quarter past two.

Ambrose watched it happen. He had been watching the trace since the Brannock's signal died, sitting at his console with his headphones on and the harp's scream still in his ears like tinnitus, and at a quarter past two the line that had been creeping down all morning — thirty-four, thirty-three and a half, thirty-three — stopped, and hesitated, and came back up. Thirty-four. Thirty-five. Thirty-five and a half. And held.

On the other console Bettany made a small sound.

"It's holding," she said. "Ambrose. It's holding. It's *up*."

He did not answer. He was looking at the clock.

A quarter past two. He had not given Hesper Vane the date of the next pause; he had walked out of her office and down the spindle and sat in the Lectern for six days refusing to think about it. But it was not hard to find. Anyone who could read a trace could find it, from his own short-period figures, which were on the Lectern's own system, in its own logs, in the files he had not thought to lock because they were only the ordinary daily numbers. Anyone he had trained. He looked at Bettany. She was staring at the trace with her face lit up, and she had not yet understood what he was looking at her for, and then she did, and the light went out of her face very slowly, and she looked down at her console.

"She asked me for the short-term projections," Bettany said, very low. "On the twenty-seventh. After you'd been up there. She said you'd said I could do them. She said you were tired."

"Yes," said Ambrose.

"I didn't — she just asked when the next pause was. That's all. I didn't know what it was for. I thought it was for the — for safety. So he'd go in when it was easiest." Her voice cracked. "Was it? Ambrose? Was it for that?"

He could not answer her.

The screens over the consoles flicked to the sunburst. It was not twenty hundred, and it was not the evening broadcast; it was an interruption, all channels, all screens on Anchorage and Hollin, and then Hesper Vane was there, in the Gallery, with the mouth in the window behind her, its bruised centre dark again, and its rim of fire very slightly — you could see it if you knew how to look — less tight than it had been that morning.

She told them the trial had succeeded.

She told them in numbers, as she told them everything. The waist at thirty-three at the moment of release. Thirty-five and a half now. Holding. She said that the Concession's readers had observed, following the release of brine-glass at the waist, a measurable recovery of the throat. She said that this confirmed the Concession's analysis, and that the Keeping would therefore proceed as planned at the deepest point of the Narrowing, on or about the first of May, with as many vessels as could be fitted and crewed. She said that the cost of the trial had been one vessel and one pilot. She said his name.

"Corrigan Dace," said Hesper Vane, "of the Brannock, gave his life this morning so that the door would stay open. His debts, and his daughter's, and his father's, have been struck from the books of the Concern. I have done it myself. I will see his name honoured in the Reach for as long as there is a Reach." She paused. "He is the first Keeper. He will not be the last. But he went first, alone, into the dark, not knowing whether it would answer. It answered. Remember him."

The screens went back to the sunburst.

Ambrose took his headphones off and put them down on the console very carefully, as if they might break.

"Ambrose," said Bettany.

"Go home, Bettany."

"I didn't know. I swear I didn't — "

"I know you didn't. Go home." He did not look at her. "Please."

She went. He heard her crying in the passage outside, and then the lift, and then nothing but the harp. It had stopped screaming. It was singing again, the thin high strained chord of the Narrowing, a hair less strained than that morning, as the waist held at its pause, exactly as the short-period figures had said it would — exactly as it would have held if the Brannock had never left the dock.

He put the headphones back on, and listened to the buoy.

He had been listening to it all morning, under the harp: the buoy at the inner markers, two million kilometres closer, hearing what the harp smoothed away. And when the glass went in, the buoy had heard something the harp had not. He played it back now. The release — the flare, the shockwave, a great white roar in the headphones. And then, after the roar, when the throat's chord came back: a new note in it. Very small. Very high. Hard and bright and steady, like a pin struck against a glass and left ringing. It had not been there before the release. It was there still, six hours later, in the live feed from the buoy, not fading. A small hard ringing at the waist, where nothing had rung before.

*Something stayed,* he thought. *She put glass into it, and something stayed.*

He did not know what it meant. He wrote it down. Then he took a clean sheet of paper from his drawer and wrote at the top of it, carefully, in his small neat hand, *Brannock, at the waist, 2.4.91, last transmission*, and under it, from memory, every word of what Corrigan Dace had said in the throat. *It's all folded light. Like the inside of a shell. It's drawing in. Like an iris. Like an eye shutting. And there's a light in the middle of it. In the middle where it shuts. A light. I've got no name for it.* He read it over. Then he folded it and put it between the pages of the logbook he kept in his drawer, the private one, so that somebody would have it, so that it would be written down somewhere that it had been a man who said it, and not a Keeper.

* * *

She was sitting on the dock floor with her back against the Mercy's landing strut and a bottle of Fat Diz's worst between her knees when Ambrose found her, a little after midnight.

Lulu was asleep in the Mercy's cockpit above them, curled up in the pilot's seat under Teo's flight jacket with her bag of bolts in her arms. Mother Salome had brought her down from the gallery that afternoon, after, and stood with her on the dock while the Mercy limped in on one attitude jet with her tow-hook hanging off, and Lulu had walked across the dock to Teo before the engines had even spun down and said, *Where's the Brannock?* — just that, with her bolts in her arms — and Teo had knelt down on the dock and told her. Lulu had not cried. She had listened, and nodded, and asked if she could sit in the Mercy, and had climbed up into the pilot's seat and sat there looking out of the canopy at the empty cradle where the Brannock should have been, until she fell asleep.

Ambrose sat down on the dock floor beside Teo without asking. He did it stiffly, an old man getting down onto steel, and when he was down he sat for a while with his long arms round his knees, looking at the empty cradle.

"I'm sorry," he said.

"Everyone's sorry." She drank. "She's sorry. She said so. On all the screens. *Remember him.*" She laughed, a bad sound. "They struck his debts. Lulu's free. She's nine and she's free. Isn't that wonderful."

He did not say anything.

"It worked, though." She turned the bottle in her hands. "Didn't it. I heard the broadcast. The waist came up. He went in and he did it and it came up. He didn't die for nothing. That's something. Isn't it? Tell me that's something."

"It didn't help," said Ambrose.

She went still.

"It didn't help," he said again, looking at the cradle. "The waist would have come up at a quarter past two if he had stayed in the Lantern. There's a short rhythm in it — a pause every few days. I told her about it. She found out when the next one was. She timed the trial to it." He was quiet for a moment. "And there's something else. The buoy heard it. When the glass went in — something stayed. At the waist. A sort of ringing. It's still there. It wasn't there before. I don't know what it is. But the glass didn't — feed anything, Teo. It didn't splint anything. It went in, and it burned, and something stayed, and that's all."

Teo put the bottle down on the dock floor, very carefully, so it would not fall over.

"Then say so," she said.

"To whom?"

"To *everyone*. Get on the screens. Get in the Assembly Hall and stand on the platform and tell them what you just told me. Tell them about the pause. Tell them she timed it. Tell them about your ringing." She was on her knees now, facing him, and she did not remember getting there. "You're the harp man. They'll believe you. They believe your charts, every pilot on the station flies by your charts. Tell them."

"I can't prove it."

"You just — "

"I can't *prove* it, Teo." For the first time his voice cracked. "I have a hypothesis. I have four paper traces and a sum on an envelope and a ringing I can't explain. If I stand up in the Assembly Hall and say that the Director is lying, she will stand up after me and say, in numbers, that the waist came up after the glass went in, and every person in that hall saw it come up. And they'll believe her. Of course they will. She has the numbers. All I've got is *why*." He pressed the heels of his hands against his eyes. "And I sail in twenty-six days. I have a berth. Cabin twelve. I have a daughter I haven't seen since she was four. If I stand up in that hall — "

"You'll lose your berth."

"I'll lose my berth," he said, behind his hands. "At least."

Teo looked at him for a long time: an old man in a cardigan, sitting on the floor of a dock in the middle of the night with his hands over his face.

Then she spat. On the dock, between them. She had not known she was going to do it.

"He went in for twenty-six days of your life," she said. "That's all he cost. Twenty-six days and a cabin." She stood up. She was not quite steady. "Go home, harp man. Go home to your daughter. I hope she's worth it."

She climbed up the Mercy's strut into the cockpit, and lifted Lulu out of the pilot's seat in her flight jacket, bolts and all — the girl did not wake, only muttered something and put her arms round Teo's neck — and climbed down again with her, and walked away across the dock toward the Lantern Row lift without looking back.

When she reached the lift and turned round to press the button, he was still sitting where she had left him, on the floor, under the Mercy's broken tow-hook, in the dark.

* * *

The Keeping glass shipment was due up from Shaft Nine at six in the morning on the fourth, and at five minutes to six every cutter in the shaft put down their tools and sat down.

Nico had been afraid it would be a few. It was not a few. It was all of them, all three shifts, two hundred people sitting down in the galleries and the cage-head and the canteen in the dust, with their cutters lying beside them on the salt, not saying anything. The overseers went up and down shouting. Nobody got up. At a quarter past six the overseers stopped shouting and went up in the cage to report, and at seven Marshal Brandt came down the access road from Tallow with forty Security men in grey and stopped at the shaft-head gate and looked at it.

Nico stood on top of the cage-head housing, where everyone in the yard could see him, with a borrowed megaphone he did not need.

"We'll cut for the Inward," he told Brandt, and the yard, and the forty men in grey. "We'll cut quota. We'll cut what pays the debts. We're not cutting for the dark." He held up his right hand. "Not one more hand for the dark. You heard what happened two days ago. A man flew into the Needle with our glass and didn't come back. That's what it's for. That's what it costs. We're not paying it."

"The Director says the trial worked," said Brandt mildly, from the gate.

"Then let the Director fly the next one."

There was a roar from the yard behind him that went up and up and echoed off the salt walls of the shaft-head, and Brandt waited for it to finish, the way his Director waited for silences, and then nodded, as if Nico had given him a perfectly reasonable answer, and said something to the man beside him, and the forty men in grey spread out along the fence and stood there. They did not come in. They did not go away.

Mother Salome came up the access road at noon, past the men in grey, who let her through, because nobody ever stopped Mother Salome going anywhere. She had the Tally under her arm in its blanket. She had Teo with her, grey-faced, and between them, holding Salome's hand, a small girl in an oversized flight jacket with a bag of bolts over her shoulder.

They came into the yard. Salome set the Tally down on the cage-head step, and unwrapped it, and the cutters who were sitting nearest got up without being asked and came and stood round it.

"He wasn't a shaft man," Salome said. "Corrigan Dace. He was a pilot. He never cut a gram in his life." She looked round at them. "But he died of the glass. Same as the thirty-one at the top of this plate. Same as all of them. The glass killed him and the Concern sent him to it. That makes him ours." She held out the stylus to the girl. "Go on, love."

Lulu took the stylus. She knelt down on the step in front of the steel plate, below the rows of names, and the yard went silent, two hundred cutters and forty men in grey at the fence, and a small girl's hand scratched letters into the steel, slowly, carefully, the way she had been taught at the shaft school: C. DACE. When she had finished she looked at it for a while. Then, as an afterthought, she scratched underneath it, smaller, in a hand that wavered: PILOT.

Not Keeper. Nico noticed. Everyone noticed. Nobody said anything.

Teo, standing behind the girl, looked up across the yard at her brother on the cage-head housing, and their eyes met. Hers were red. She did not look away. After a moment she lifted her hand, and put it flat over the bead at her throat, the way she always did, and held it there; and Nico understood that she had come to tell him something, and that she would tell him later; and that whatever it was, she was not going to tell him to keep his head down any more.

# Chapter 11: Fire on the Anchorhold

*The Severance · 2608*

The Shoals came for the glass at four in the morning, Anchorhold time, out of the shadow of the mouth itself, which nobody had thought possible.

"Contacts," said Marsh, and then, in a different voice, "Contacts, Captain, *contacts* — eleven, twelve, fourteen, bearing zero-zero-zero, they're coming out of the *shear*, how are they flying in the shear — "

Juno was on the bridge in her shirt, with her boots unlaced. She did not remember getting there. On the main display the mouth filled the forward quarter, and against its grey-gold rim fourteen small dark shapes were sliding out of the fire, one after another, in a long loose line, riding the shear-waves down toward the Anchorhold the way surfers ride a swell.

"They've got readers," said Juno. "They've got a reader aboard calling the waves. Somebody's taught them." She heard her own voice, flat and calm, the command voice, and was grateful for it. "Battle stations. Wake up the forward battery. Hollis, I want everything. Signal the Anchorhold: enemy strike inbound from the mouth, fourteen vessels, they're going for the stores. Signal Squadron. Where's the *Constant*?"

"Refitting at Tallow, Captain. It's us and the *Ardour* and the station guns."

"Then it's us and the *Ardour* and the station guns."

The *Vigil* had been on station at the Anchorhold for a week. Squadron had brought her forward from the twentieth after the Shoals hit the glass convoys off Dorcas twice in a fortnight; Juno had not complained. She had spent the week walking the Anchorhold's empty passages at night, and sitting in the harp hall, once, at the back, watching her sister sit in front of the harp with her eyes closed, and leaving before Pell saw her. The glass for the Thirtieth Keeping — eighteen tonnes of it, the best that Hollin had cut in a decade — was stowed in the holds of the Anchorhold's dead wheel, the one that had not turned since the Concession, behind armoured doors. That was what the Shoals had come for. Everybody knew that was what they had come for. You could not fly a Keeping without glass.

The fourteen came on. They were cutters, mostly, Shoals-built, fast and lightly armed — and two heavier ships behind them, frigates, old Compact hulls with new paint.

"The big one," said Marsh. "Captain. Look at her flag."

Juno looked. On the display the lead frigate's identification beacon had lit, which no Shoals ship ever did in action; and the name on it was *CALLUM*.

"Open channel from the *Callum*, Captain. All frequencies."

"Put it on."

"*Anchorhold, this is Rhee Calder of the Free League.*" His voice. Older, rougher, but his. "*We are here for the glass. We are not here for you. We are not here for the Keepers. Get your people out of the dead wheel and off the second wheel. Get the Keepers out. You have six minutes. After that we fire on the stores.*" A pause. "*Juno, if you're out there — I'm sorry. Get your sister off the second wheel.*"

The channel closed.

The *Ardour*'s captain was on the command link before Juno could speak, a fat, frightened, decent man called Pryce whom she had known for years. "Asante. Asante, did you — "

"I heard."

"The Keepers' dormitory's on the second wheel. Asante, if they fire on the dead wheel the second's right beside it — "

"I know where it is." She was looking at the display. Fourteen ships. Six minutes. "The station guns will engage the cutters. You take the second frigate. The *Callum* is mine." She switched channels. "Anchorhold, *Vigil*. Evacuate the second wheel. Evacuate the archive section and the Keepers. Get them into the spindle. Do it now."

The Anchorhold's answer came back a long ten seconds later, in the voice of a Warden guard-captain who sounded as though he had been woken by it. "*Vigil, the High Warden orders that the Keepers remain at their stations. The Keepers are under the protection of the order. The Shoals will not fire on — *"

"The Shoals have just told you they're going to fire on the dead wheel," Juno said. "The dead wheel is two hundred metres from your Keepers. Get them out."

"*The High Warden's orders, Commander — *"

She cut him off. "Helm. Take us in. Between the *Callum* and the dead wheel."

"Captain?"

"You heard me, Linnet. Put us in the way."

They went in. The *Vigil* came round the Anchorhold's spindle on full burn and put herself across the *Callum*'s approach, between the frigate and the dead wheel, close enough to see the new paint on the frigate's flank and the scorched place on her bow where some Compact gunner had once nearly done for her. The forward battery fired. The *Callum* fired back. The *Vigil* shook like a struck bell, and somewhere aft something tore, and the bridge lights went red, and Hollis in engineering began to swear in a long steady monotone that was, Juno knew from six years of it, the sound of the Chief having fun.

It went on for nine minutes. Afterward, nobody on the bridge could agree about the order things happened in.

The cutters went for the dead wheel. The station guns got four of them. The *Ardour* took the second frigate in the engines and it went spinning off into the shear and broke up there, silently, in long bright pieces. The *Vigil* and the *Callum* traded fire at a range where you could not miss, and the *Vigil* lost her number two battery and a third of her starboard plating and seven crew, and the *Callum* lost her forward guns. And at the fifth or sixth minute — before the six were up, before anyone had been evacuated from anywhere — two of the Shoals cutters came round the dead wheel's far side, where nobody was watching, and fired everything they had at the armoured doors of the glass stores.

The doors held. The wheel did not.

The shots went high, into the wheel's spokes, into the old Concession structure that had not turned in three hundred years; and the spokes, which were very old, let go. The dead wheel tore free of the spindle on one side and swung — slowly, horribly, like a door on a broken hinge — and hit the second wheel.

Juno saw it on the display. She saw the second wheel's rim crumple where the dead wheel struck it. She saw the second wheel's windows go dark in a long running ripple, section by section. She saw, from one of the dark sections, a sudden white plume of air and debris and small tumbling shapes venting into the grey-gold light of the mouth.

The Keepers' dormitory was on the second wheel.

"Danny," she said. "You have the ship."

"Captain — "

"You have the ship. Pull back. Cover the station. Let the *Callum* go if she runs. Hollis, I need a damage-control party at the forward lock in two minutes, suited, with cutting gear. Get me as close to the spindle as you can and let me off."

"Juno," said Marsh, not using her rank, for the first time in two years. "Juno, the *Callum*'s still — "

"*Callum*'s breaking off," said the helm. "Captain, she's breaking off. They're all breaking off. They're running for the mouth."

On the display the Shoals ships were turning away, all of them at once, the surviving cutters falling in behind the crippled *Callum* in a long loose line, back toward the shear and the fire at the rim of the mouth, the way they had come. Nobody pursued them. Nobody had anything left to pursue them with.

The channel from the *Callum* opened once more, on all frequencies, for a few seconds.

"*That wasn't* — " Rhee's voice, raw. "*That wasn't us. That wasn't the plan. The wheel — Juno, I didn't — *"

It closed.

* * *

The archive was on fire.

Adaeze did not know how. The archive was in the spindle, not the wheels; it should have been the safest place in the Anchorhold. But the dead wheel's spokes ran into the spindle's heart, and when the wheel tore loose it had wrenched something deep in the structure, and somewhere behind the racks an old Concession power conduit had split and was spitting fire into three hundred years of paper. She had been at her desk, as she was most nights, with her private notebook open. She had smelled the smoke before the alarms. She had gone out into the archive and seen the far end of it lit up orange.

She did not leave. She did not consider leaving. She ran the other way, toward the oldest racks, toward the fire, with her sleeve over her face.

The oldest spools were crystal and would survive heat, a while. The paper would not. She pulled the paper first — the tuning logs, the daybooks, the bound volumes of the first readers after the Blank Year — and threw them into an armful and ran them out into the passage and dumped them on the floor and went back. The smoke got thicker. On the third trip she could not see the racks at all, and found them by touch, and her eyes were streaming and her lungs had begun to feel like something torn. On the fourth trip she remembered the Fork.

The reliquary was off the harp hall, not the archive. It was not burning. But the harp hall was on the far side of the archive from the passage, and the archive was a corridor of fire between them, and she stood in the smoke with her arms full of daybooks and thought, quite clearly, *If the hall goes, the Fork goes,* and dropped the daybooks, and went through.

She would not remember most of it. Heat on the side of her face. Her robe catching at the hem and her beating it out with her bare hands. The iron door of the harp hall, hot to the touch, and then the hall itself, clear, cool, enormous, the harp singing under the dome as if nothing in the world were wrong, and the mouth hanging over it, and — sitting on the floor in front of the harp in her nightshirt, with a body across her knees — Pell Asante.

Adaeze stopped.

It was Ezra Lind. His face was blue. There was frost on his lashes and in his hair. Pell had his head in her lap and was holding it, with both hands, very carefully, the way you would hold a bowl of water, and looking down into his face, and not crying.

"He came to find me," said Pell. She said it to Adaeze as if continuing a conversation. "When the wheel went. I was here. I'm always here at night. He knew. He came through the dormitory to find me and the section went while he was in it, and he got the door shut behind him, and he got as far as the spindle lock, and somebody pulled him in." She looked up. "He walked here. On his own. He walked all the way here from the lock. He wanted to tell me he'd found me." She looked down again. "And then he sat down."

"Pell," said Adaeze.

"He failed his sled licence twice," said Pell. "He told me. He said the Needle had made a clerical error." And then she did cry, all at once, bending over the dead boy's face with her whole body shaking and no sound coming out of her at all.

Adaeze went to the reliquary. She did that first. She would think about that afterward, often — that she had gone to the grille before she went to the girl — and she would never be sure whether it had been faith or something less. She unlocked the grille with her archivist's key, and took the Fork from its grey cushion, and put it inside her robe, against her skin, where it lay cold and heavy. Then she went and knelt beside Pell on the floor in front of the harp and put her arms round her, and the dead boy too, and held them both.
