That was how Juno Asante found them, eleven minutes later, coming through the iron door from the burning archive in a damage-control suit with a cutting torch in one hand and three of her crew behind her, with her faceplate up and her face black with smoke.

She stopped in the doorway, as Adaeze had.

Then she came across the hall and knelt down in front of her sister.

"Pell."

Pell lifted her head. She looked at Juno as if she did not know her. Then she said, quite clearly, "Juno, he's dead," and Juno said, "I know, I know he is, I'm here," and took her sister's face in her two gloved hands.

They got everyone out through the harp hall's far door, the one that led up the spindle away from the fire: Adaeze, and Pell, and two of Juno's crew carrying Ezra Lind between them on a stretcher made of a Warden's cloak. On the way past the passage door Adaeze stopped, and Juno, behind her, saw what she was looking at: the heap of daybooks and bound volumes and tuning logs on the passage floor, where she had dumped them, singed, smoke-blackened, lying in a spreading pool of the foam the station's suppressors had finally begun to spray.

"Those," said Adaeze. "Please. Those."

Juno looked at her, and at the books, and did not ask. "Marsh. Get those. All of them."

* * *

The *Vigil*'s wardroom was the size of a large cupboard and smelled of burned insulation, and at two in the morning, twenty-two hours after the raid, it held the captain, the archivist, a heap of singed books, and a brass tuning fork lying on the table between them on a folded napkin.

Pell was asleep in Juno's cabin, sedated by the ship's medic. The other surviving Keepers were in the Anchorhold's spindle, in the High Warden's own guest rooms, under guard. Four of the nine had died when the second wheel was struck: Ezra Lind, and Tavish Orme, and the baker, and the woman who had once been a novice. The Wardens had issued a statement within the hour. *The Shoals have murdered the Keepers of the Thirtieth. The Draw will be held again, for four, at the Compact Hall, in five days.* The Keeping would go on. The glass had survived, behind its armoured doors. The glass always survived.

Adaeze had not let go of the Fork until Juno's medic tried to take her robe off to dress the burns on her hands, and then she had taken it out and laid it on the table and sat looking at it, with her bandaged hands in her lap,.

"I struck it," she said. "Ten days ago. Did I tell you?"

"No."

"I'm the archivist. I clean it once a month. I've cleaned it two hundred times." She looked at it on its napkin. "Ten days ago I struck it. Against my hand. It's a tuning fork. You're supposed to strike them." She laughed, a little, without much breath. "Your sister asked me once what it was for, a tuning fork you don't tune with. I said it was the sort of question she would ask."

Juno waited.

Adaeze told her. The First Keeping's records. The spool for the second of May that was the first spool in the rack, and the nothing before it. The catalogue: *transferred to the keeping of the First Warden. Not returned.* The forced rack, the sheared clamps. The Blank Year. And the Fork, struck in the alcove, its note going out into the hall and meeting the harp's drone and fighting it. *Wah. Wah. Wah.* The Hunger Tone and the Fork, not the same note.

"I don't know what it means," she said. "I've been trying to think what it means for ten days. Somebody took forty-four years of records out of the archive on the day after the First Keeping and never brought them back. Somebody tuned the harp to a note that isn't the note of the instrument it's supposed to have been tuned by. And the order has been calling the gap a joke for three hundred years." She looked up. "I keep thinking that if it were a mistake, someone would have noticed. Someone would have struck the Fork before me. In three hundred years. Someone." Her voice went very quiet. "Unless they did. And then they did what I've done. Which is nothing."

Juno looked at her for a while. Then she said, "Danny. Would you get it, please."

Marsh, who had been standing at the door, went out. He came back four minutes later with flour on his sleeves and a blank Navy slate in his hand, and put it on the table beside the Fork.

"The galley flour bin," he said, to Adaeze's look. "The Captain said somewhere stupid."

Juno turned the slate round and pushed it across the table, and told her about Mara Teague.

The archivist read the list. She read it slowly, the way she read everything, with her bandaged finger moving down the lines. The half-thickness foils. Nineteen of thirty-six. The sleeve names: four Draws, four names, four careful lines in a dead clerk's hand. She stopped at the last one and read it twice. Then she sat back.

"Teague," she said. "Mara Teague. I knew her. I didn't know she — " She stopped. "She died in custody. I saw the notice. A heart condition."

"Yes."

"She didn't have a heart condition. She used to run the stairs at the Hall at lunchtime. All eight hundred metres. Every day for nineteen years." Adaeze closed her eyes. "Oh, Mara."

"Is it true?" said Juno. "You'd know. You keep the Draw records. Is it true?"

Adaeze did not answer at once. She opened her eyes and looked, not at the slate, but at the singed heap of books on the floor in the corner of the wardroom. Then she got up, stiffly, and knelt down by the heap and went through it with her bandaged hands, slowly, until she found what she was looking for: a thin black ledger with a scorched spine. She brought it back to the table and opened it.

"The Draw ledger," she said. "The archive's copy. The clerks send one up after every Draw; I file it. Every foil, every name, in the order they come out of the Wheel." She turned the pages. "I've always noticed — I thought it was a clerk's mark. A tick. One name in every Draw has a little mark beside it. A small *s*, in the margin. I asked about it once, when I was new. They said it meant the foil had been *sealed*. Checked. I never — " Her finger stopped. "Here. The Twenty-Ninth."

Juno leaned over. The ledger's page, in a clerk's neat hand: nine names. And beside the third, in the margin, very small, an *s*.

*Callum Calder.*

Adaeze turned to the end, to the newest pages, to the Thirtieth, in the same hand, dated the eighth of October. Nine names. She did not have to look for it. Juno could see it from across the table.

*7. Pell Asante, of the Speaker's house.* And in the margin, small and neat and ordinary: *s*.

Neither of them said anything for a long time.

"He chose her," said Adaeze at last. Her voice was level and her face was grey. "The High Warden. He put her name in his sleeve and drew it out and read it to the Hall. To make your mother — " She stopped. "And he declined your petition. To your face. He told you the Draw was the only fair thing in the Reach."

"Yes," said Juno.

The archivist looked down at the Fork on its napkin. She reached out one bandaged hand and touched it, very lightly, the way you would touch the face of someone sleeping.

"I took the grey," she said, "because somebody has to keep the records honestly." She was quiet. "I've been keeping them honestly for twenty years. All of them. Every name." She laid her hand flat over the Fork. "I just never read the margins."

* * *

Her mother was at the window when she came in, as if she had not moved from it in a month.

The *Vigil* was in the yards at Tallow for nine days of patching, and Juno had come straight up from the yards in her stained uniform without calling ahead. Efua Asante did not turn round. In the glass Juno could see her reflection laid over the well of lights below — the black and silver, the composed civic face. On the table behind her a slate glowed with the evening's Assembly business. Juno did not have to read it. Everyone in Tallow Under knew. The appropriation for the Shoals campaign had passed two weeks ago, by four votes, with the Speaker's own vote among them. After the raid on the Anchorhold it would have passed by forty. Nobody remembered any more that it had been close.

"You voted for it," Juno said.

"Yes."

"Two weeks after he drew her name."

"Eighteen days," said Efua. "Yes."

"Did he come to you?"

Her mother was silent for a moment. Then she said, to the glass, "Sit down, Juno."

"I don't want to sit down."

"Then stand." Efua turned round. Her face was perfectly composed, and her eyes were not. "He came to me on the ninth of October. The day after the Draw. In this room. He sat where you're standing and drank a cup of tea and told me how sorry he was, and how extraordinary Pell was, and how he had prayed for her. And then he said that the Thirtieth was the deepest Keeping in two hundred years, and that the Shoals had sworn to stop it, and that without the appropriation the Navy could not protect the Keeping, and that Pell's chances of coming back out of the throat would be very much better with a strong escort than a weak one." She paused. "And that he would see to it personally that the best ship in the Home Squadron was given the escort. Commanded by the best captain." Another pause. "He did, didn't he."

Juno felt the floor of the room go out from under her, very slowly, like a lift.

"The *Vigil*," she said. "Our posting. He — "

"Tobiah Greaves didn't choose you for the Keeping, Juno. Cassius Mor did. He asked for you by name. As a favour to me." Her mother's mouth moved in something that was not a smile. "He was very kind about it."

"Did you know?" Juno said. "About the sleeve. Did you know?"

And her mother, the Speaker of the Assembly of the Compact of the Reach, looked at her across the room for a long time, and then nodded once.

"Every Speaker knows," she said. "It's not written anywhere. It's told to you, by the Speaker before you, the night you're elected. That the Wheel is fair except for one foil. That the High Warden is given one name in every Draw, for the good of the Reach. That it's the price of the Keeping, and the Keeping is the price of the door, and the door is the price of everything." Her voice did not change. "I was told on the night of my election, eight years ago. By old Hennessy, at his own dining table, over the cheese. I went home and sat on the edge of the bath and was sick. And then I went to work the next morning, Juno, because what else does one do?" She drew a breath. "I have known for eight years that the High Warden chooses one Keeper in every Draw. I didn't know he'd choose mine. I suppose I thought — " She stopped. "I suppose I thought I'd been careful."

"You voted for a war," said Juno, "to buy my sister an escort."

"I voted for a war that was going to pass with or without me," said her mother, "to keep the one person who might bring my daughter home standing between her and the guns." For the first time, her voice shook. "And it was you. And you did. You brought her out of the harp hall with a dead boy in her lap. I watched the footage. Do not stand in my house and tell me what I bought."

They stood on either side of the room. Below the window the light-well was full of the small lamps of the houses, a well of stars, and from somewhere far down it a voice was singing the Hunger Tone, held and held.

"He's going to draw four more names on Thursday," said Juno. "To replace the dead. Four more sleeves."

"Probably."

"And she's still going in. Pell. She's still going in on the fifth of December, because he chose her, with a foil up his sleeve, to buy your vote. And you've known since the night it happened."

"Yes," said Efua Asante. "What are you going to do about it?"

Juno did not answer. She did not have an answer. She stood in her mother's room in her stained uniform with the list of the dead in her head — Ezra Lind, Tavish Orme, the baker, the novice, Callum Calder, Mara Teague — and found that her hand had gone, by itself, to the breast pocket of her uniform, where a small folded square of paper lay over her heart with a frequency written on it in pencil.

"I don't know yet," she said.

Her mother looked at her hand, and at the pocket,. Then she turned back to the window.

"When you do," said the Speaker of the Assembly, to the glass, very quietly, "don't tell me."

# Chapter 12: Ashes in the Gutter

*The Ebb · 2937*

The Lamplighter called it the Cleansing, and it began at midnight on the twenty-sixth of May, twenty-eight days before the Sixtieth Narrowing, with a sermon.

Isra heard it from the Pans. She was on the bone-pickers' dock with a torch in her teeth, under the Gristle's belly, refitting the deck plates the Lantern had torn up the week before, and the speakers on the dock came on all at once the way they did, and that warm low voice filled the cold hangar like water filling a tank.

"Children," said the Lamplighter. "There are those among us who hoard.

"I have asked for the glass. I have asked gently. I have sent my Deacon to your doors with a lamp and a kind word. Most of you have given. I bless you. But there are those among us — in the Gutter, in the Pans, in the old Shoals holds — who have hidden what the Needle needs. Who have kept back glass in their walls and their floors and their mouths, while the door dies. Who have gone, some of them, in secret, to the Anchor, to whisper with the Wardens about things the Wardens have no business saying." A pause. "I know who you are. I have always known.

"Tonight my Lantern will go into the Gutter and cleanse it. Every room. Every wall. Every floor. What is hidden will be found, and what cannot be found will be burned out. Do not stand in their way. If you have nothing hidden, you have nothing to fear.

"What the Needle keeps, it keeps for us."

Isra dropped the torch.

It took her twenty minutes to get from the Pans to Rope Street. She ran the whole way, up through the cargo tunnels and the old shuttle ramp and the long dark gallery under the Third Terrace where the light-wells had fallen in, and the whole way she could smell it coming: smoke. Not salt-smoke, the dry grey breath of a cutting torch on rock; real smoke, black and greasy, the smoke of rags and oil and wood and plastic, of everything people had carried down into the Gutter because it was all they had. By the time she came out at the head of Rope Street the tunnel was full of it to the roof, rolling and orange-lit, and people were coming out of it the other way, crawling, coughing, carrying children, carrying nothing.

The Lantern had lit it from both ends.

They had not meant to, perhaps. Isra would think that later, much later, when she could think anything. They had come down Rope Street with their oil lamps on poles, room by room, tearing out walls and floors, and somewhere somebody had fought them, or dropped a lamp, or thrown one; and the Gutter was a hundred years of rags and bedding and dried salt-weed and paper packed into tunnels with no air, and it had gone up the way paper goes up. She could see them at the far end of the tunnel through the smoke, the black coats, lamps swinging, falling back. Coughing. Shouting at each other. Not coming forward.

Hanne's room was at the end of a dead-end off Rope Street. In the middle. Where the smoke was thickest.

Isra pulled her shirt up over her mouth and nose and went in.

She did not remember most of it. Heat, low down, along the floor. The walls invisible. Somebody's hand catching at her ankle — a child, a boy, she dragged him back to the street and went in again. The dead-end. The ship-door, which had been part of a ship, which was steel, which was standing open. And in the doorway, on his knees, a huge dark shape coughing so hard he could not stand, with something in his arms.

"Pike."

"Got her," Pike said. It came out of him in pieces. "Got her. Ribs. Can't — lift — "

Hanne was in his arms. Small, folded, wrapped in her shawl. Her face was black with soot and her eyes were shut and she was holding, against her chest, with both arms, the cittern.

Isra took her. She weighed nothing. Isra took her and put her over her shoulder the way you carry a sack of glass, and got her other arm under Pike's arm, and the three of them went back up Rope Street through the smoke on their knees, and their hands, and Pike's breath whistling and whistling, until the air got thinner and cooler and they came out into the junction under the old shuttle ramp and lay down on the salt.

Deacon Rusk was standing at the junction with his hands clasped behind his back, watching the smoke come out of the tunnel.

He looked down at them. At Pike, flat on his back with his strapped ribs heaving. At Isra on her knees. At Hanne, in her shawl, with the cittern in her arms.

"Old Hanne," he said. He sounded, for once, almost uncertain. "Is she — "

"Get away from her," said Isra.

He looked at her. His face, under the soot and the lamplight, was unreadable. Then he stepped back, one step, and turned his head and called to the black coats behind him: "Leave these. They've nothing. Go on." And he went away up the ramp without looking back.

Hanne opened her eyes.

"Girl," she said. Her voice was a thread.

"I'm here. Gran. I'm here. Don't talk."

"Don't be stupid. I've been talking for seventy-three years, I'm not stopping now." Her bird's eyes moved, found Isra's face, held it. "The cittern."

"You've got it. It's here. You've got it."

"Listen." The old woman's hand moved on the strings. "The low string. Do you hear me? The low string. Never retune it. It's never been retuned. Not by me. Not by my mother. My mother's gran set it, and her father before her, back and back. Every teller. We're told: you can tune the others how you like, but the low one stays. Always. It's the old note." She coughed. It went on a long time, and when it stopped there was blood on her lips. "I never knew why. Nobody knew why. We just kept it." She looked at Isra. "That's why your glass sings at it. Isn't it. That's what you found."

Isra could not speak. She nodded.

"Hah," said Hanne. Something like satisfaction moved in her face. "Wrong is better than nothing. Because you can fix wrong. Didn't I tell you." Her fingers found the low string and plucked it, very softly. It sounded under the roar of the burning tunnel: one deep note, old, true. Against Isra's breastbone, inside her shirt, the shard woke and hummed.

"Take it to your Warden," said Hanne. "The boy. Take it to him."

"Gran — "

"*The door grew narrow in the dark,*" sang Hanne Venn, in a voice so thin it was hardly there, plucking the low string, "*the Reach was cold and small — *" and stopped, and did not go on.

Isra knelt on the salt with her grandmother in her arms, and the cittern between them, and the low string still faintly humming. Beside her Pike lay with his face turned away. Behind them, all down Rope Street, the Gutter burned.

* * *

She came in to Dock Four at noon the next day with Pike strapped into the jump-seat and her grandmother's body wrapped in a bone-picker's shroud in the Gristle's hold, and Lucan was on the dock, and when she climbed down out of the hatch and saw him she found that she could not let go of the ladder.

He came and took her hands off it, one finger at a time, and held them.

They laid Hanne out in the harp hall. That was Lucan's idea; Isra would not have thought of it. The Wardens had a rite for the dead, he said, an old one, older than the Quiet: the body lay for a night under the dome, in the sound of the harp, so that the Needle would know who was coming. He did not believe the last part. He said so. But he thought Hanne might have liked the music. So they carried her up through the dark spindle, the two of them, with Pike walking behind, slowly, one hand on the wall, and laid her on a pallet on the worn floor under the mouth with her shawl folded round her and the cittern beside her, and Lucan lit the little lamps that the rite required, nine of them, round the pallet, and they sat down on the floor.

The harp was singing the Hunger Tone again. Lucan had retuned it, as the Prior ordered, the night the Prior ordered it. He had sat up all night doing it, he told Isra, without once looking at the screen.

"I've been playing it like this for nine days," he said. "Listening to it lie to me." He looked at the harp, gold in the lamplight. "I've been thinking about your grandmother's cittern the whole time."

Isra said nothing. After a while she reached over and picked up the cittern from beside Hanne's body, and set it on her knee, the way Hanne had always set it, and held it out to him.

"The low string," she said. "She said it's never been retuned. Never. Every teller back and back. They're told the low one stays." Her voice did not quite hold. "She said it's the old note."

Lucan looked at the cittern. Then he got up without a word and went to the reliquary grille.

The hinge-pins were back in. The Prior had seen to it. Lucan did not bother with them. He took out of his sleeve, from somewhere inside his robe, a small brass object, and turned round, and Isra saw what it was.

"You — "

"I took it back the night after he took it," Lucan said. "Off his bedside table. He sleeps very deeply. He hasn't noticed yet. Or he has, and he hasn't said." He came back and knelt beside her. "I'm going to hell, I think. If there is one. I've stopped being sure there's anything to go to."

He struck the Fork against his palm. Its note went up, thin and pure, into the dome, and met the harp's drone and fought it, *wah, wah, wah*. And then, while it was still sounding, Isra plucked the low string of her grandmother's cittern.

The two notes met.

They did not fight. They lay down together in the air of the hall like two people who had been apart a long time, one inside the other, so exactly the same that for a moment Isra could not tell which she was hearing — the Fork or the string or both — only one note, round and clear, going up into the dome under the bruised dark mouth. Inside her shirt the shard rang against her breastbone like a struck bell.

Lucan let the Fork fall to his lap. His face was wet.

"The tellers," he said. "The tellers kept it. All through the Quiet. When the order lost the records, and the harp went to the Hunger Tone, and nobody remembered — they kept the old note. In a string. Not knowing why." He gave a short, cracked laugh. "Three hundred years. In a cittern in the Gutter."

Isra put the cittern back down beside Hanne, carefully, the low string still faintly humming against the old woman's shoulder. Then she lay down on the floor of the harp hall next to her grandmother's body with her face against the shawl, the way she had lain against Hanne's knee when she was small, and cried the way she had not cried on the salt at the junction or in the Gristle or on the dock — all of it at once, out loud, with nothing held back, while Lucan sat beside her with the Fork in his lap and his hand on her back, and Pike, against the wall, with his eyes closed, coughed softly into his rag.

* * *

The Lamplighter came to take the Anchor the next morning, and found a dead woman in her chapel.

Lucan heard the ships before he saw them: the deep shudder through the station's frame as the black ore-hauler docked, and then another, and another. Eight ships. More than she had brought the first time. More than the Anchor had seen in a century. He was on his feet in the harp hall with the Fork still in his sleeve when the iron door opened and the black coats came in, two by two, with lamps, and spread along the walls in their silent double rank as they had before. Then the Prior, on his two sticks, his face like old paper. Then the Deacon. Then her.

She came into the middle of the hall and stopped.

The nine little lamps were still burning round the pallet. Hanne lay in her shawl under the mouth with the cittern at her side. Isra was sitting on the floor at her head, cross-legged, with her back straight and her face swollen from crying and her eyes perfectly dry; and she looked up at Mercy Oduya as the Lamplighter came in, and did not get up, and did not look away.

The Lamplighter looked at the body for a long moment.

"Who is this?" she said.

Nobody answered. Then the Deacon, behind her, said quietly, "Old Hanne Venn. The teller. From Rope Street."

Lucan saw it go into her. He saw it the way he had seen her face change the first time she stood under the dome: something going through the broad plain face and leaving it different. She stood very still.

"Hanne," she said. "From Rope Street." She took a step toward the pallet. "In the Cleansing?"

"In the smoke," said Isra from the floor. "Your smoke."

The Deacon moved. The Lamplighter lifted one hand without turning, and he stopped.

"She sang at my son's naming," said Mercy Oduya. She was looking at Hanne's face. "Did you know that? I was twenty-two. I lived on Rope Street then, four doors down from her. I had nothing. I had a baby and a room with no heat. And she came to his naming and sat on my floor with that — " she looked at the cittern — "with that thing on her knee, and sang the Long Night for him, all forty verses, every one of them different. She said he should hear all of them. Because they were all wrong, and he should know." Her voice was very low. "Tobin. She sang all forty for Tobin."

She knelt down. The black coats along the walls shifted, uneasily. She knelt on the worn floor beside the pallet in her black coat, across the body from Isra, and put out one cracked brown hand and laid it flat on the old woman's shawl, over her heart.

"I'm sorry," she said. To Hanne. "I'm so sorry."

Isra stared at her.

"You burned her," Isra said.

"Yes," said the Lamplighter. She did not take her hand away. "I did. I said cleanse the Gutter, and they cleansed it, and she was in it. I'll carry her." She looked up, across the body, into Isra's face. "I carry a great many. I'll carry her too. And I will go on, because the door is dying, girl, and if it dies every one of them was for nothing. Every one. Hanne and Tobin and all of them." Her dark eyes were wet and perfectly steady. "Do you understand that? It's the only way they mean anything. If it works."

Isra did not answer. Lucan, standing by the harp with the Fork heavy in his sleeve, watched the two of them kneeling on either side of the dead woman, the prophet and the bone-picker, and thought that he had never in his life seen two people understand each other so completely and hate each other so much.

The Lamplighter stood up.

"The old woman stays here tonight," she said. "With her lamps. Nobody touches her. Tomorrow the Wardens will give her the rite, in the grey, properly." She turned to the Prior. "And then, Prior, this hall is mine. My Keepers will come here to be blessed. My readers will sit at that harp. The Great Keeping will go from this station, from this dock, under this dome. Your Wardens will keep their cells and their greenhouse and their grey, and they will stand on the dock when my Keepers fly, and sing." She paused. "That is how it will be."

Prior Saar leaned on his two sticks.

"That is how it will be," he said.

She turned to go. At the iron door she stopped, and looked back once — at the harp, singing its long false drone; at the old woman on the floor in her ring of lamps; at Lucan beside the frame.

"Brother," she said. "You told me the truth, the first time. That it was dying. You were right." She looked at the mouth through the dome. "I mean to save it. I'd like you on my ship when I do. Giving me the line. Will you?"

Lucan felt the Fork in his sleeve, cold against his arm. He felt Isra's eyes on him from the floor.

"Yes, Lamplighter," he said.

She nodded, as if that were the answer she had expected, and went out, and her black coats went after her, and the Deacon last, who looked back from the door at Isra on the floor — a long look, Lucan thought, not pleasant and not unpleasant, the look of a man reading a list — and closed it.

Isra got up off the floor.

"*Yes, Lamplighter,*" she said.

"Isra — "

"You said *yes*."

"I said yes," said Lucan, "because she is going to fly a hundred skiffs into the throat in twenty-six days, and she wants somebody to give her the line, and if it isn't me it'll be one of her own, who'll give her whatever line the harp tells him with the harp tuned to a lie." He took the Fork out of his sleeve and held it, so she could see it, and put it back. "I'd rather it was me. I'd rather be on the ship. I'd rather be in the room when it happens, with this in my pocket, than anywhere else in the Reach. Wouldn't you?"

Isra looked at him for a long moment, there in the ring of lamps over her grandmother's body, with the harp droning its false note behind him and the mouth leaning over them both.

"Yes," she said at last. "I would."

She sat back down at Hanne's head. After a while Lucan sat down beside her, and they kept the vigil together, all that night, the two of them and the nine little lamps and the old woman, under the dome, while the harp sang the wrong note over and over and over into the dark.

# Chapter 13: Proof

*The Concession · 2291*

The swell slowed on the eleventh of April at nine minutes past three in the morning, exactly where the sum on the back of the envelope had said it would.

Ambrose had been waiting for it for ten nights. He had stopped going to his bunk. He slept in the Lectern, in his chair, in two-hour snatches between readings, with the headphones round his neck and the buoy's feed running in one ear and the harp's in the other; and every night he logged the swell's period, the slow rise and fall under the throat's chord, to the second, in pencil, in the little column in his notebook that had begun on the night of the first of March with the word *probably*. Four minutes and eleven seconds. Four minutes and nineteen. Four twenty-six. Lengthening, night by night, as the long tide approached its turn, exactly as a pendulum lengthens its swing when you let the string out — exactly as the sum said. He had told himself every night that it was not proof. That a curve can be made to fit anything, if you want it badly enough. That he wanted it very badly.

At nine minutes past three on the eleventh the period lengthened by eleven seconds in a single swing, which it had never done; and then by eleven again; and he sat bolt upright in his chair with the headphones pressed to his ears and listened to the long tide under the Needle slow, and slow, and hang — he could hear it hanging, a vast slow pause at the bottom of something, like a breath held at the end of an exhalation — and then, at nineteen minutes past four, begin, very faintly, to come back.

Not the waist. The waist would go on narrowing for another eighteen days; the short breath, the Narrowing itself, still had eighteen days of falling in it. But the long tide underneath it had turned. He could hear it. Anyone could have heard it, with the headphones on, who knew what to listen for.

He sat until it was light — what the station called light — with the headphones on, and wept, quietly, for no reason he could have explained to anybody, and then he got up and washed his face in the little sink at the back of the dome and went to work.

He made it into a proof. It took him two days. He knew by then exactly what a proof had to be, for this: not a hypothesis, not a curve on an envelope, but a thing that could be put in front of somebody who did not want to believe it and could not be argued with. So he made three. He made the numbers: the old paper traces, corrected against the fork, the four Narrowings laid side by side with the swell drawn through them, and the swell's turn at three in the morning on the eleventh plotted on the same axes, sitting exactly on the line. He made the prediction: the date and the hour the waist would bottom out, at a little above twenty-one per cent, on the first of May; the date and the hour it would begin to recover, on the second, with nothing done to it; the curve of its recovery, day by day, out to the end of May. Something that could be checked. Something that would be true or false in front of everyone.

And he made the sound.

That was the one he cared about. He took the buoy's feed and the harp's, the raw sound of the throat, and ran it through a filter he built that night from the harp's own spare resonators, tuned — he did it by hand, with the fork, string by string — to pass only the long low swell and nothing else. Then he sped it up. A thousand times. So that a month of the long tide went by in forty minutes, and three hours in eleven seconds. And he sat in the Lectern at dawn on the twelfth with the headphones on and played it.

It was a breath. That was all. A vast slow breath, in and out, in and out, very deep, like someone sleeping in the next room. And at the end of the forty minutes, the last few seconds — the eleventh of April — you could hear it reach the bottom of an exhalation, and hang there, and begin to draw in again.

Anyone could hear it. A child could hear it. You did not need a theory. You only needed ears.

He copied all three onto a data spool, and put the spool in his breast pocket with the unsent letters to his daughter, and the fork in his jacket pocket, and sat for a moment longer in the grey light under the dome.

Then he went to find Teo Kesh.

* * *

The strike was nine days old and the shaft-head looked like a siege, because it was one.

Brandt's men had not come in. They had not gone away either. There were sixty of them now, in a ring along the fence, in shifts, day and night, and on the fifth day they had shut off the water main to the shaft-head and on the seventh the power to the canteen, and the cutters were living on what could be carried in at night over the back of the slag heaps from the Pans. Teo had been carrying it. She had flown the Mercy round to the Pans after every run and loaded her cargo bay with whatever the Lantern Row kitchens would give, and walked it in over the slag in the dark with her helmet lamp off, three or four times a night. Brandt knew. She was certain he knew. His men had watched her go past, twice, and said nothing. She did not understand it and did not like it.

She was sitting on the canteen step at noon on the thirteenth with a bowl of Fat Diz's salt-eel stew going cold in her lap when Ambrose Lyle came up the access road from Tallow in his cardigan, alone, past the men at the gate — who looked at him and let him through, the way they let Mother Salome through — and across the yard to her, with a portable reader under his arm and his face grey with no sleep.

She did not get up. She had not seen him since the dock, since she spat.

He stopped in front of her.

"I need you to listen to something," he said. "And your brother. And Mother Salome. And as many of the others as will come." He hesitated. "And then you can spit again, if you want. I'd understand."

She looked at him. Then she put the bowl down on the step and went to get Nico.

They played it in the canteen, in the dark, because the power was off. Ambrose had brought batteries. He set the reader up on a table at the front with a pair of old speakers from the Lectern, and the cutters came in from the yard and the galleries and the cage-head, two hundred of them, filthy, hungry, sitting on the tables and the benches and the floor with the dust of nine days on them, and Mother Salome at the front with the Tally across her knees in its blanket, and Nico beside her. Teo stood at the back against the wall. She did not know why. She thought perhaps she wanted to be able to leave.

Ambrose told them who he was. He did not need to; half of them knew his name from the charts, and the other half had heard it in the Lantern. He told them what the harp was, and the buoy, in a few sentences. He told them about the swell. He did not use the word *beat*. He did not use any word they would have to ask about. He said, *The Needle breathes. Twice. A short breath every four thousand days, and a long one, much slower, underneath. When the short one comes at the bottom of the long one, it goes deep. That's what's happening now. That's all that's happening.*

Then he said, "I'm going to play you the long one. Forty days of it, in forty minutes. You don't have to understand anything. Just listen."

He played it.

Teo had expected — she did not know what she had expected. Numbers. A noise. What came out of the old Lectern speakers in the dark canteen was a breath. A vast, slow, sleeping breath, in and out, deep and soft and patient, filling the room the way the harp filled the dome, so that after a minute or two you stopped hearing it as a sound at all and started feeling it, in your chest, in time with your own. In and out. In and out. Two hundred people sat in the dark and breathed with the Needle, and nobody moved, and nobody spoke. Somebody near the front was crying. Teo found, after a while, that she was holding the bead at her throat so hard that its edge had cut into her palm.

At the end — the last few seconds — the breath went out, and out, and out, longer than any before, until it seemed it must stop; and hung there, at the bottom, for one long, terrible, silent moment.

And began to come back.

Ambrose switched the reader off. The canteen was perfectly silent.

"That was two days ago," he said into the dark. "The eleventh of April. The long breath turned. It's coming back now. The short one will go on down until the first of May, because it has to, and then it'll come back too, on its own, the way it always has — because the long one's carrying it. Whatever we do. Whatever anybody puts into it or doesn't." He took a breath of his own. "The Needle doesn't need us. It never did. It isn't starving. It isn't closing. It's breathing. And on the second of May it will start to open, and by the end of May it'll be as open as it was in January, and that'll be true whether nine pilots fly into it on the first or none."

Nobody said anything for a long time.

"And Corrigan," said Teo from the back.

Two hundred heads turned.

Ambrose found her in the dark. "Corrigan went in at the start of a pause that was coming anyway," he said. "The Director knew when the pause was. She asked my assistant. I didn't tell her. I didn't stop her either." His voice did not change. "He died for nothing, Teo. I'm sorry. I should have said so on the dock, the way I'm saying it now. I didn't, because I wanted to go home." He was quiet for a moment. "I still want to go home."

Nico was on his feet.

"Then we tell them," he said. His voice was shaking. "Now. Tonight. We break out the canteen's broadcast set — we've got batteries, he's got batteries — we put that on every channel in Tallow. Every screen. We let everybody hear it breathing. Let them hear that and then let her stand up and tell them it's starving — "

"She will," said Ambrose. "Stand up. And tell them it's starving."

"Let her try."

"She'll stand up," Ambrose said, "and say that the waist is still narrowing, which it is, and will go on narrowing for eighteen days, which it will. And that a noise from a man who wanted to go home is not a reason to let the door close. And that the trial worked. Every one of them saw the trial work." He spread his hands. "And then, on the second of May, when the waist begins to recover, she'll say the Keeping did it. And they'll believe her, Nico. Because they'll have seen nine pilots go in on the first."

"Then nobody goes in on the first," said Nico.

"Somebody will," said Mother Salome.

She had not moved. She sat at the front with the Tally across her knees and one hand flat on its blanket, the way she always did, and she was looking not at Ambrose or at Nico but at the dark speakers on the table, as if the breath were still coming out of them.

"Somebody will," she said again. "She's offering to strike their debts. All of it. Their families'. There isn't a pilot on that station who hasn't lain awake since the fifteenth of March doing the sum. She'll find nine. She'd find ninety." She turned her head and looked at Ambrose. "You're right, harp man. And it's not enough to be right. Not here. Not with her holding the paper." She looked down at the Tally. "Who's it for, your proof? Who do you think is going to hear it, that she can't talk over?"

"Ardent," said Ambrose.

The canteen stirred.

"The Board," he said. "The Inward. The Concern's licensed through the Ardent courts; the Charter's a public instrument. There are inspectors. There are newspapers. There are people on the other side of the door who have never heard of Hesper Vane and don't owe her anything. If I put this in front of them — the proof, the prediction, the sound — they can't say I'm a frightened old man who wants to go home. They can watch it come true. From there. The second of May." He touched his breast pocket. "I sail on the twenty-eighth. On the *Providence*. I'll carry it. I'll put it in front of everyone I can find in Ardent. And when the waist turns on the second, exactly when I said it would, they'll know."

"And the nine who go in on the first?" said Teo.

He did not look away from her. "I'll broadcast it here too," he said. "On the twenty-eighth. From the *Providence*, as she goes into the throat, when she's too far for anyone to stop it. On every channel. The sound, the prediction, all of it. Three days before the Keeping. It's the best I can do, Teo. If I do it sooner she'll take the spool and the berth both, and then it never gets out at all."

Nobody spoke. In the dark at the front, Nico sat down slowly on his bench.

"Three days," said Teo.

"Three days," said Ambrose. "And then it's theirs. The pilots'. Every one of them will know, before they fly. What they do with it — " He stopped. "I can't fly it for them. I can only tell them."

Teo pushed herself off the wall. She walked down through the dark canteen between the benches, between the cutters, to the front, and stopped in front of the old man at his table, and looked at him.

Then she held out her hand.

He looked at it, startled. Then he took it. His grip was dry and thin and surprisingly strong.

"Twenty-eighth," she said.

"Twenty-eighth."

* * *

Hesper Vane sent for him the next morning, which he had known she would.

She was standing at the window when he came in, with her back to him and her hands folded behind her, looking out at the mouth. The rim of fire was tighter than it had been a week ago; the dark at the centre smaller and deeper. It looked like an eye squinting against pain. She did not turn round.

"You went to the shaft-head," she said.

"Yes."

"And played them your swell. In the canteen. In the dark." She sounded, if anything, interested. "Marshal Brandt's men listened from the yard. They tell me it was very moving. One of them asked me afterwards whether it was true." She turned. "I told him it was a recording of the Needle made by a very distinguished scientist, and that it was entirely accurate, and that it had no bearing whatever on the Keeping. He was quite satisfied."

Ambrose did not answer. He stood on her red Inward rug in his cardigan with the spool in his breast pocket and waited.

"Sit down, Ambrose."

He sat. So did she, behind the desk, with her hands folded on it, the way she always did.

"I'm not going to cancel your berth," she said. "I expect you thought I would. I thought about it. I'm not going to." She tilted her head slightly. "You'll sail on the twenty-eighth, as planned, in cabin twelve. You may take with you whatever you like. Your proof. Your sound. Your prediction. Every paper trace in the Lectern, if you can fit them in your luggage. You can play it to the Board, and to the Ardent courts, and to every newspaper in the Inward. I won't stop you."

He stared at her.

"Do you know why?" she said.

He did not.

She unfolded her hands, and reached to the side of the desk, and touched — just touched, with one fingertip — the black wooden cabinet that stood there with its lock. "Because the Board already knows," she said. "Not your swell. Your swell is new and very beautiful and I'm sure it will be written up in all the journals. But the Board knows that the Keeping has nothing to do with whether the Needle closes. I told them so. In writing. On the twentieth of March, in a memorandum, which they approved on the first of April, the day before the trial. It's in there." She tapped the cabinet once. "I'll show it to you, if you like."

"You — told them."

"Of course I told them. They're my employers. I don't lie to my employers, Ambrose, I find it inefficient." She leaned back. "I told them that the Narrowing was very probably a natural event, and very probably temporary, and that the Concession's own throat-reader could find no evidence that anything done to it would affect it either way. And then I told them what to do with it."

"Which is?"

"Govern," said Hesper Vane.

She said it quite simply, the way you would name a tool.

"The Concern is leaving," she said. "You know that. It's leaving because the Reach costs more to hold than it pays — the salaries, the ships, the Security, the doctors. But the glass still pays. The glass will always pay. The Board would like the glass to go on coming through the Needle after the Concern has gone, at a fraction of the cost, without anyone here to make it come. That was the problem I was sent to solve. How do you keep forty thousand people cutting glass and shipping it through a door to people they will never see, who own their debts, after you have taken away everyone who might make them?" She spread her hands. "You give them a reason. You give them a door that needs keeping. A door that only you know how to keep. A covenant: *give, and the door stays open; stop, and you are alone in the dark forever.* You make the giving sacred and the keeper indispensable. And then you don't need Security, Ambrose. You don't need the *Providence*. You don't need anyone at all but a reader who knows when the door will open, and someone to tell the story."

He sat very still.

"Every civilization," said Hesper Vane, "is a story about what will happen if you stop. That's all a civilization is. The story is never true. It doesn't have to be. It only has to be believed, by enough people, for long enough, and then it's the world." She looked at him almost kindly. "I'm giving the Reach a story. It's a better one than most. It asks a great deal, once every eleven years, and in return it gives them a door, and a purpose, and an order, and each other. The Board approved it in an afternoon. It will outlast the Board. It will very probably outlast the Concern. That's what I'm for."

"And the pilots," said Ambrose. His voice did not sound like his own. "The ones who fly in."

"Are volunteers. Who will be paid more than anyone in the Reach has ever been paid for anything. And whose names will be remembered." She paused. "And some of them will come back. Most, if they're good. Mr Dace was unlucky."

"He wasn't unlucky. You sent him in to make a pause look like a miracle."

"Yes," she said. "I did. And it worked. Shaft Nine is the only shaft on strike, Ambrose. The other eleven are cutting Keeping glass on triple shifts, singing. Did you know they'd started singing? Somebody made up a song about him. I don't know who." She looked at the window. "In a year they'll have forgotten it was a trial. In ten they'll have forgotten it was a choice."

He stood up. He did not decide to; he found himself standing.

"I'll tell them," he said. "Here. Not in Ardent. Here. On every channel. Before the first."

"You can try," said Hesper Vane. "And I'll stand up after you and say what I said to Marshal Brandt's man: that it's accurate and beautiful and has no bearing on the Keeping. And they'll believe me, because the waist will still be narrowing, and they'll be frightened, and frightened people don't want a breath, Ambrose, they want something to do." She did not raise her voice. She never raised her voice. "And then, on the second of May, the door will open, and they'll have done it, and nobody in the Reach will ever listen to your swell again."

She stood too, and came round the desk, and stood in front of him on the red rug, a slight straight woman a head shorter than him, and looked up at him.

"Go home," she said. "Sail on the twenty-eighth. Give your daughter her fork. Write your paper. You'll be famous, I should think. You've earned it. It's very good work." Her mouth moved slightly. "It's only that it doesn't matter."

He looked at her for a long time. Then he looked past her, at the desk, and the black cabinet beside it with its lock, where a memorandum lay that said everything she had just said, in writing, approved by the Board of the Ostrander Concern on the first of April, the day before Corrigan Dace flew into the dark.

"Thank you, Director," said Ambrose Lyle, and went out.

# Chapter 14: True Pitch

*The Severance · 2608*

She retuned the harp on the night of the seventeenth of November, eighteen days before the Thirtieth Keeping, because she could not think of a reason good enough not to.

She had thought about it for nine days. She had thought about it in the infirmary with her hands in dressings, and in the archive, among the black ruin of the racks, sifting what the fire had left; and in her little office with the singed daybooks stacked on her desk and the Draw ledger open at its last page, at a small neat *s* in a margin. She had thought about it in chapel, singing the Hunger Tone with the others at the morning office, and found that she could no longer sing it without hearing, underneath it, the Fork's note fighting it in the reliquary alcove — *wah, wah, wah*. She had prayed. She had not been answered. She was not sure any longer that she had expected to be.

What decided her, in the end, was a line in a daybook.

It was one of the ones she had carried out of the fire: a slim volume bound in black, its spine scorched, its first pages brown and brittle at the edges. The daybook of the Lectern for May and June of 2291. The first daybook of the order, written in the weeks after the First Keeping by whatever readers had been left to read. She had catalogued it fifteen years ago and never opened it since, because it was all numbers. She opened it now. And on its first page, at the head of the first entry, dated the second of May, in a small upright hand that was not the hand of the rest of the book, she read:

*Henceforth the harp is to be tuned each morning to the H.T. and not otherwise. By order of the F.W.*

She sat with that for an hour.

*Henceforth.* So there had been a before. A way the harp was tuned before the First Keeping, which the First Warden had then forbidden. On the day after. On the same day, very nearly, that forty-four years of records had been taken from the archive and never returned.

At midnight she took the Fork from the reliquary with her archivist's key, and went out into the empty harp hall under the dome, and began.

It took her until four. Nine courses. She did it the way she had been taught to tune a harp as a novice, before she ever learned the Hunger Tone — with a reference and her ears — only the reference was the Fork, struck against her bandaged palm and held to the frame, and her ears were twenty years older and very tired. Her hands hurt. Twice she had to stop and sit on the floor and breathe until the burns stopped throbbing. Somewhere in the fourth course she started to hear the difference. By the seventh she was weeping, steadily, without noticing, the tears running into her collar.

The harp did not drone when she had finished. It sang.

She would think afterward that there had been no other word. The long low ache of the Hunger Tone was gone, and what had come up out of the strings in its place was wider and deeper and more alive: a great slow chord with something moving in it, a rise and fall under the surface, so slow you felt it more than heard it, like the swell under a ship. She stood in front of the harp in the dark with the Fork in her hand and listened to it, and after a long time she understood what she was listening to. It was breathing.

She went to the reader's console and woke it.

She knew how to read a trace; she had been trained to it, before the archive. She watched the waist line draw itself across the old screen in pale green, the throat narrowing toward its point, the Narrowing eighteen days from its trough. And underneath it, where the Hunger-tuned harp had never shown anything but the throat's own steady noise, there was now a second line. Slow. Enormous. A long low wave running under the Narrowing like a tide under a boat. And it was not falling. It was at the bottom of its trough, or within a day or two of it — she could see it flattening — and on the far side of the trough, the line began, very gently, to rise.

The Narrowing was deep because the tide was low. And the tide was turning.

She did not trust it. She went down into the ruin of the archive with a lamp, to the oldest rack, where the crystal spools had come through the fire scorched but whole, and she brought them up one by one — the First, the Fifth, the Twelfth, the Twentieth, the Twenty-Ninth — and fed them through the old reader. They had all been recorded through a harp tuned to the Hunger Tone; but she knew the Hunger Tone's offset from the Fork's note now, to the hair, because she had just spent four hours taking it out. She could take it out of the records too. It was arithmetic. It was the kind of thing an archivist did all day.

She did it for the Twenty-Ninth first, because she had stood in this hall and read its numbers aloud eleven years ago with her voice shaking.

The tide was there. Under the Twenty-Ninth Narrowing, as under this one: the long slow wave. And the waist had begun to recover — the short breath, the Narrowing itself, turning up off its trough — at fourteen minutes past six on the morning of the Long Night. The Keepers had gone in at nine. Nearly three hours later. She remembered it. She had been here. Everyone had stood in this hall and sung while they went in, and the High Warden had struck the Fork, and an hour afterward the readers had called out that the waist was opening, and the whole Anchorhold had wept for joy.

It had been opening for three hours before they flew.

She did the Twentieth. The Twelfth. The Fifth. It was not always three hours; once it was eleven, once forty minutes. But it was always before. Every time. Every Keeping in the archive had been flown into a door that was already opening, timed by readers who could see the waist turn on a harp that could not see why, and called a miracle.

She did the First last. The spool for the second of May, the first spool in the rack, the morning after. It was all that survived; there was nothing from the Long Night itself. But she could see on it, at the very start, the tail of the long tide's turn — a little lower than now, a little earlier in its rise. The First Keeping, too, had been flown at the bottom of a tide that was already turning.

There was one other thing. She almost missed it, at the end, with her eyes burning. At the waist, at the very narrowest point, where the trace should have shown nothing but the passage, the retuned harp was showing something small and hard and bright. A knot. A ringing. As though something were lodged there. It had not been visible on any trace she had ever seen. She looked at it for a while, and did not know what it was, and made a note of it in her private notebook, and let it go, because she had no more room in her for anything else.

At five in the morning, Anchorhold time, Adaeze Quill sat on the floor of the harp hall with her back against the reader's console and the Fork in her lap, under the dome, with the harp singing its true chord above her and the mouth leaning in, and she put her face in her bandaged hands and wept for a hundred and twelve people whose names she had read aloud on the anniversaries for twenty years.

* * *

Corrie Rock was a lump of dirty ice the size of a city block, with a smugglers' hab dug into it like a tick into a dog, and it had been neutral ground in the Severance for six years because both sides needed somewhere to buy things they were not supposed to have.

Juno came in on a Navy jump-shuttle with its transponder off and Marsh's voice in her ear the whole way, quietly furious. *If Squadron asks, Captain, I'm going to tell them you've got food poisoning. I'm going to tell them it's very bad. I'm going to describe it.* She had told him to describe it as much as he liked. She landed in a hangar that smelled of rust and ammonia, and a Shoals girl with a clan tattoo along her jaw met her at the hatch and looked at her uniform and spat on the deck and led her through a maze of ice tunnels to a door.

He was sitting at a table in a room with no windows, with a pot of something hot and two cups, and his right hand wrapped in a dressing to the wrist.

She had not seen his face in eleven years except in grainy pictures. It was older. Thinner. There was a scar along his jaw she did not know and grey at his temples he was too young for. It was still his face. He looked at her standing in the doorway in her greys and did not get up, and for a moment neither of them said anything at all.

"You came," he said.

"You sent a message on the cadet band."

"Eleven years ago I'd have said that was romantic." He nodded at the other chair. "Sit down, Juno. I'm not going to shoot you. I can't hold a gun." He lifted the bandaged hand. "The *Callum*'s bridge took a hit from your forward battery. Very good shooting. Your gunner should get a medal."

"He did."

"Good."

She sat. He poured. Neither of them drank.

"I didn't mean the wheel," he said. "I need you to know that. Whatever else. I told my cutters the stores, the doors, nothing above them. Two of the boys came round the far side and — they're nineteen, Juno. They were frightened. They fired everything they had at whatever they could see. Nobody meant the wheel." He looked at his tea. "Four Keepers. I heard. And seven of yours."

"Ezra Lind," said Juno. "He was twenty-two. He walked a hundred metres through a vented section to tell my sister he'd found her, and then he sat down and died in her lap."

Rhee closed his eyes.

"Callum was eighteen," he said after a while. "He went in at the Twenty-Ninth and didn't come out. You were there. You were at the Hall."

"I know."

"No. You don't." He opened his eyes. "You don't know, Juno. You've never known. You came to the Hall and stood on your mother's terrace and sang the Hunger Tone and went home. You didn't — " He stopped. "Sorry. That's not fair. You're here."

She took out her slate. Not a Navy slate; a cheap civilian thing she had bought in Tallow with cash. On it was a single photograph: a page of a black ledger with a scorched spine, nine names in a clerk's hand, and beside the third, in the margin, a small neat *s*.

She pushed it across the table.

He looked at it for a long time. His face did not change. Then he put his good hand flat on the table beside the slate, very carefully, as if the table might move.

"I knew," he said. "I didn't know. I knew." He was very quiet. "I filed that complaint in our third year. About the Shoals cadets. You signed it."

"I know."

"My name was on top." He looked up at her. "I used to lie awake. In the League, in the first year. Thinking: it was the complaint. It must have been the complaint. And then thinking, no, that's mad, that's grief, nobody would do that. The Wheel is the Wheel." He laughed, without any sound. "How many?"

"One every Draw. That we know of. A sleeve name. The High Warden carries it in."

"And Pell."

"And Pell."

He pushed the slate back across the table to her. "Then you know why we're going to stop it," he said. "The League voted. Three days ago. At the Thirtieth Narrowing, when the skiffs go out to the mouth, we'll be there. Everything we've got. We're not letting nine more people go into that thing so that a man in a grey robe can tell the Assembly how to vote."

"If you fire on the Keepers you kill my sister."

"We won't fire on the Keepers. We'll fire on their escort." He held her eyes. "Which is you."

"And then the Keepers fly in anyway, because the Wardens will send them in whether the escort's there or not, into a throat full of crossfire. Do you think Mor will call it off because the Shoals are shooting? He'll call it a martyrdom. He'll have nine more names in the liturgy by Christmas." She leaned forward. "Rhee. Listen to me. What if it doesn't need doing?"

He frowned. "What?"

"What if the Keeping does nothing?" She said it very low, though the room had no windows and no one else in it. "There's a Warden. The archivist at the Anchorhold. Adaeze Quill. She found — " She stopped, and began again, and told him: the Blank Year; the Fork and the harp not agreeing; the daybook from the fire. *Henceforth the harp is to be tuned to the H.T. and not otherwise.* She had heard the last of it only that morning, by tight-beam, in the archivist's voice, flat and exhausted: *I tuned it to the Fork. It breathes, Commander. The door breathes. It opens by itself. It always has.*

Rhee listened without moving. When she had finished he sat looking at the wall.

"Can she prove it," he said at last. Not a question.

"She thinks so. She thinks she can show anyone who can read a trace. She thinks — she says she can make it so anybody can *hear* it. On the harp. If it's tuned true."

"And who's going to listen? The Assembly? The Hall? They'll burn her for heresy before she gets the first word out."

"Not if everybody hears it at once." Juno had been thinking about it for two days. "On the Long Night. The harp hall broadcasts on every channel in the Reach while the Keepers fly. It always has. Every house in Tallow Under has the Long Night on. Every ship. Every hab in the Shoals that has a receiver — you know they do, Rhee, you used to listen to it in the dormitory with the sound down. Everyone in the Reach is listening to that harp on the night of the fifth." She held his eyes. "If it's tuned true, on the night, and somebody's in the hall to tell them what they're hearing — "

"Then the Wardens kill whoever it is and switch it off."

"Then somebody has to hold the hall long enough."

He looked at her across the little table, in the windowless room, over the two cold cups of tea.

"You're asking me," he said slowly, "not to shoot at you."

"I'm asking you to hold fire on the night. All of it. And to be ready, if I call, to come in and stand between the Anchorhold and the Home Squadron for as long as it takes to play the Reach a sound." She heard her own voice and did not recognise it. "And if it doesn't work — if she's wrong, if nobody listens — then you can do whatever the League voted, and I won't be in a position to stop you, because I'll be in a cell. Or dead."

"And if she's right?"

"Then nobody goes in," said Juno. "Not Pell. Not anybody. Ever again."

Rhee Calder looked down at his bandaged hand on the table. Then, slowly, he turned it over, palm up, and left it there.

After a moment she put hers in it. His fingers closed, carefully, over the dressing.

"Eleven years," he said.

"I know."

"I'll put it to the League," he said. "I can't promise. Some of them will want to fire anyway. Some of them have lost more than I have." He held her hand. "But I'll be there, Juno. On the night. On the cadet band. Call, and I'll come."

* * *

He came into the harp hall at dawn on the nineteenth, on the second morning, while she was still at the console.

She had not retuned it. She had meant to. She had told herself, on the first morning, that she would sit for one more night and copy everything — every trace, every spool, every correction — onto crystal, three times, and hide the crystals in three places, and then put it back to the Hunger Tone before the novices came in for the morning office. She had sent the novices away, the first morning, at the door. *A fault in the harp. I'm working on it.* They had gone. She had not thought it would hold for two.

She heard the door and did not turn round. She knew his step. She had known it for twenty years.

He did not say anything at first. He came and stood in the middle of the hall, under the dome, a few metres behind her, and stood there, listening. She could see his reflection faintly in the console glass, laid over the green trace: the tall straight figure in grey, the white cropped head lifted. The harp sang its true chord above them both, wide and deep and breathing.

"Well," said Cassius Mor softly. "Well, well."

She turned round.

He was not looking at her. He was looking at the harp, with an expression she had never seen on him, and could not at first name. Then she could. It was the face of a man hearing a piece of music he had loved as a child and had not heard in a very long time.

"It's beautiful, isn't it," he said.

She said nothing.

"I've heard it once before," said the High Warden. "Like this. Many years ago." He came forward, slowly, and laid his palm against the harp's gold frame, the way all the readers did. "What have you found, Sister?"

She had decided, in the two nights, what she would say if he came. She had decided to tell him everything, all of it, plainly, and watch his face. So she did. The daybook. *Henceforth.* The retuning. The tide under the Narrowing. The twenty-nine Keepings, corrected; the waist turning, every time, before the Keepers flew. The First, at the bottom of its tide like this one. She told it in order, carefully, the way she would have catalogued it, and she watched him the whole time.

He did not argue. He did not ask a single question. He stood with his hand on the harp and listened to her as if she were telling him something he had heard before, a long time ago, and was glad to hear again from someone who told it well.

When she had finished there was a long silence.

"You have done remarkable work," Cassius Mor said. "Truly. I have never in my life seen a piece of scholarship like it. The First Warden would be very proud of you."

"Would the First Warden," said Adaeze, very quietly.

He looked at her then. His eyes were grey and warm and steady, and very sad.

"Retune it, Sister," he said. "Before the novices come. They're waiting in the passage. I sent them away a second time, but I can't do it a third." He held out his hand, palm up. "And give me the Fork. It belongs on its cushion."

She looked at his open hand for a long time. Then she put the Fork in it. She did not know why. She would think about that afterward, often, and never decide — whether it was the habit of twenty years, or the gentleness of his voice, or simply that her hands were burned and tired and the Fork was heavy.

He closed his fingers round it. He went to the reliquary alcove and laid it on its grey cushion, carefully, the way she would have, and closed the iron grille, and locked it with the key on the chain round his neck. Then he stood for a moment with his back to her, his hand on the bars.

"You'll have copied it," he said, without turning. "Three times, I'd guess. You're very thorough."

She did not answer.

"Yes," he said. "Of course you have." He turned round. "Retune the harp, Adaeze. Then go to bed. You haven't slept in two nights. We'll talk about all of this after the Keeping, when there's time." He smiled at her, the warm kind tired smile that he had given the Commander across his desk, the smile the whole Reach knew. "There's always more time after the Keeping."

He went out. She heard him in the passage, speaking gently to the novices, sending them to breakfast.

She stood alone in the hall with the harp breathing above her. Then she went to the console and, with her bandaged hands, began to take down the true chord, course by course, peg by peg, back to the Hunger Tone; and as she worked she found that she was not afraid, which surprised her, and that what she felt instead was something very cold and very clear, like the light at the rim of the mouth. *I've heard it once before,* he had said. *Like this. Many years ago.*

He had known. Not suspected; known. He had come into the hall and heard the true chord and recognised it, the way you recognise a voice. And he had not been surprised by a single thing she said.

# Chapter 15: Strata

*The Ebb · 2937*

The Lamplighter's readers slept from midnight to four, and in those four hours, every night for a week, the harp hall was Lucan's.

They were not bad readers, the two boys the Lantern had sent up. They were earnest and quick and they had learned the Hunger Tone at the Lantern House from a man who had learned it from a Warden who had left the order, and they could read a trace well enough to call a line. But they were frightened of the harp. They sat at the console all day in their black coats with their backs very straight, and called out the waist every hour in voices that cracked, and at midnight they went gratefully to their bunks in the old dormitory on the near wheel and did not come back until morning. And Prior Saar, who had given the night watch to Lucan for twelve years, had said nothing about changing it.

Lucan did not retune the whole harp. He had learned that much. He retuned one course — the ninth, the lowest, the one that had been spliced worst and that nobody would notice was singing differently under the others — to the Fork, every night at midnight, and every night at four he put it back. One course was enough. One course tuned true, if you filtered the others out, would show you the mass at the waist the way a single lamp shows you a cave.

He had spent the first two nights just looking at it.

On the third, Isra came. She came up from the greenhouse on the near wheel, where Lucan had got her taken on as a hand — the Lantern let the Wardens keep their greenhouse, and a bone-picker girl watering beans was beneath anyone's notice — and sat on the floor beside his console in the dark with her knees drawn up and the shard on its cord in her fist, and watched. She did not ask questions. She had stopped asking questions since her grandmother died. She watched the way Pike worked the grapple arm: patiently, completely, as if nothing else existed.

It was she who saw the bands.

"Can you make it bigger," she said, on the fourth night, at about two. "The edge. There. Where it's ridged."

He made it bigger. The single true course sang into the console, and the edge of the mass drew itself across the screen in pale green, and grew, and grew, until the ridges on its surface became lines, and the lines became layers, packed one on another, thin and thick and thin, like the side of a cliff where the sea has cut it. Like the root of her shard.

"There," said Isra. "Those."

"I see them."

"Count them."

He looked at her.

"Count them," she said. "I've been counting the ones on the Returns for ten years. On the roots. You can count the years on a ship sometimes, my gran said, by the bands, like rings in old wood. I never knew if it was true." She did not look away from the screen. "Count them."

It took him the rest of that night and most of the next. The layers were very fine near the bottom of the mass, so fine that the single course could barely separate them, and he had to sit with his ear pressed to the headphones and the filter narrowed to a thread, listening to each band ring its own tiny note as the throat's noise went over it, and mark each one with a pencil on the screen's glass. Isra sat beside him and kept the count. She did it on her fingers, and then on a scrap of paper, and then, when they lost their place, on the floor beside her with a piece of charcoal from one of the nine little lamps that had burned round Hanne.

At ten past three on the fifth of June, with the Lamplighter's boys due back in fifty minutes, Lucan took off the headphones and sat back.

"Fifty-nine," said Isra.

He did not answer.

"Fifty-nine. Lucan. And the Sixtieth's in eighteen days."

"I know."

He was looking at the screen. At the bands, marked in pencil. They were not all the same. That was the thing he could not stop looking at. Some were fine as a hair. Some were thick. And the thick ones were not anywhere. They were at the top, the newest, the outermost — two of them, very thick, laid down on everything else like two coats of paint — and in the middle, a long run of medium bands, steady, one after another, thirty or so of them; and then, below those, nearer the bottom, a stretch where the bands thinned out again and became irregular and faint, some barely there at all.

He got up and went to the shelf at the side of the hall where the order kept its books — the few that had survived the Quiet, the daybooks from 2741 onward — and took down the Book of Keepings, the order's own record, which listed every Keeping it knew of with the number of skiffs that flew it, as far back as the books went. He brought it back and opened it on the floor beside the charcoal marks.

*Fifty-Ninth: seven skiffs.* The outermost band. Thick.

*Fifty-Eighth: nine.* The band beneath. Thick.

*Fifty-Seventh: two. Fifty-Sixth: one. Fifty-Fifth: none flown.* Three bands — and the third, when he looked, so faint he had almost not marked it at all.

He went down the book, band by band, with Isra watching, and it was there all the way down. Where the order's records said many skiffs, the band was thick. Where they said few, it was thin. Where they said none, it was a ghost: barely a line, as if the throat had laid down only what little glass had drifted in by itself. Below the Forty-Second, where the order's books stopped, he could not check it any more; but the long run of medium bands went on down, steady, thirty-odd of them, one after another, exactly as even as you would expect if, for three hundred years before the Quiet, somebody had been flying the same number of skiffs into the throat every four thousand days.

And at the very bottom of the mass, against the throat's own wall, there was one more band. Very thin. Almost nothing.

"The first," said Isra.

"The first," said Lucan.

He sat down on the floor beside the Book of Keepings. He found that his hands were shaking and put them flat on his knees.

"It's not starving," he said. "It never was. It's not even sick. It's — " He stopped. "We did this. Every Keeping. Every skiff. Every gram of glass we've ever flown in there to feed it. It didn't feed anything. It *stayed*. It stuck to the waist and grew, layer on layer, Keeping after Keeping, three hundred years, six hundred years, until there was no room left for the door." He looked at the screen. "We've been choking it. The Keeping is what's killing it."

Isra did not say anything. She was looking at the two thick bands at the top. The Lamplighter's.

"And she's going to give it a hundred," she said.

"Yes."

"Every gram in the Reach."

"Yes."

"How thick does that make the band, Lucan?" Her voice was very quiet. "A hundred skiffs. How thick."

He did not have to work it out. He had already worked it out, in the back of his mind, while she counted.

"Thick enough," he said.

* * *

"It rings," said Isra.

It was the next night. They had not slept. Lucan had spent the day calling numbers beside the Lamplighter's two boys with his face composed and his mind somewhere else entirely, and Isra had spent it watering beans and thinking, and at midnight she had come up to the hall and sat down beside him on the floor and said it before he had even finished retuning the ninth course.

"What does?"

"The thing. The glass. In the throat." She held up her shard. "It rings. You heard it. The first night you tuned to the Fork. Like a bell. The whole station could hear it." She turned the shard in her fingers. "And this sings at the cittern. At the Fork. It's the same note. It's all the same note. The glass in the salt, the glass in the throat, the Fork, the low string — it's one note, Lucan. Everything's tuned to it."

"Yes."

"So what happens if you ring it?"

He looked at her.

"Really ring it," she said. "Not listen to it. Ring it. Hit it with its own note, as hard as you can, at the right time." She was speaking faster now. "The Returns come out when the throat squeezes. That's what you said. Every Narrowing, it squeezes, and things that are stuck get pushed loose, and come out with the glass grown on them backwards. So it's already trying. Every four thousand days it tries to push the stuff out and it can't, it only gets a few. What if you helped it? What if, at the Narrowing, when it's squeezing hardest, you rang the whole mass at its own note — " She brought her two fists together, hard, in front of her. "Glass breaks, Lucan. You hit glass at its own note, it breaks. Every cutter knows that. That's what a burst is. That's what took Fennick's hand, in the old song. You go too fast at the wrong note and it lets go."

Lucan sat very still on the floor.

"The harp's a listener," he said slowly. "Not a — it picks up. It doesn't put out. And it's two million kilometres from the waist. You couldn't ring a teacup from here."

"Then you need something closer."

They both turned their heads at the same moment and looked at the telescope.

It took him an hour to find the Bell again. It had not moved. It never moved. A small grey cylinder hanging in the glow at the very edge of the mouth, where the pull held it, nose toward the dark, its lattice of wires at the tail bent and furred with frost. He had looked at it a hundred times and thought of it as a ruin, a curiosity, a thing the old Wardens had made a story about. He looked at it now through the brass eyepiece.

"It's a little harp," he said. "That's what the old books say. A listening buoy. Built to sit right at the mouth and hear the throat close. Built by — " He stopped. "Built by whoever built *this*." He put his hand on the gold frame beside him. "Same maker. It must be. Nobody else could have."

"Can it ring? Instead of listen?"

"I don't know." He was still looking through the eyepiece. "The harp can. Not much. There's a — there's a driver in the frame. Old. I found it years ago when I was restringing and I never knew what it was for. A coil, under the base, that can set the wires going instead of only hearing them. If the buoy was built the same way — " He stopped again, and his breath went out of him slowly, and he stepped back from the telescope.

"Look at the end of it," he said. "The tail. Where the wires come out of the end-plate. In the middle of the plate. Look."

She looked. It took her a while. The frost, the bent lattice, the grey glow. And then she saw it: a small dark hole in the centre of the end-plate, round, neat, deliberate, about the width of a finger. Like a keyhole.

"What's that?"

"I don't know," said Lucan. "But I know how wide it is." He took the Fork out of his sleeve and held it up, stem first, in the lamplight. "I've been staring at that socket for twelve years. I always thought it was a mounting bolt." He turned the Fork's stem slowly. "It's not a bolt. It's a cradle. It's the right size, Isra. It's exactly the right size."

She took her eye from the telescope and looked at him, and at the Fork in his hand, and at the harp behind him, and at the shard in her own fist. All one note.

"He built it to take that," she said. "Whoever it was. He built the buoy with a place for the Fork in it. To tune it."

"Or to wake it." Lucan was very pale. "If the Bell's been sitting out there dead for six hundred years because nobody's put the Fork back in it — "

"Then somebody has to go and put it back."

They looked at each other.

"At the Narrowing," Lucan said. "It has to be at the Narrowing. When the throat's squeezing. When the Returns come. That's the only time the mass is loose enough to — " He was thinking aloud. "The Bell's right at the mouth.
