Somebody would have to fly to it in the Long Night, in the shear, with the Keeping fleet going past, and put the Fork in it, and give it power — it'll have none, after six hundred years, it'll need a ship's power, a cable — and then somebody here would have to drive the harp at the same time, tuned true, through the coil, at the Bell. So they ring together. The harp to wake it and the Bell to throw it. And keep it up. For as long as it takes."

"How long does it take?"

"I have no idea," said Lucan. "Nobody's ever done it. Nobody's ever *thought* of it." He laughed suddenly, a short cracked laugh. "And I'm going to be on her ship. I said yes. I'm going to be on the Lamplighter's flagship at the head of a hundred skiffs, giving her the line. I can't be here at the harp."

"Then you'll give her a bad line," said Isra. "Slow. You'll hold them at the markers. Tell her the shear's too heavy, tell her to wait for a better wave. You're her reader. She trusts you. She said so. Hold her as long as you can." She was not looking at him. She was looking at the telescope. "And I'll take the Gristle to the Bell."

"Isra — "

"I've been flying the Ossuary for ten years. I know the shear at the mouth better than anyone alive. Better than you. Better than her boys." She held out her hand. "Give me the Fork."

"Not yet," said Lucan. He put it back in his sleeve. "Not yet. I have to get the coil working first. And someone has to be at the harp." He looked at her. "Someone who can tune it true and hold it. For as long as it takes. While I'm on her ship lying to her."

"Who?"

He did not answer. He looked at the iron door, toward the passage, toward the cells, where twelve old men in grey were sleeping in a station built for six thousand.

* * *

Pike told her the next morning, in the greenhouse, among the beans.

He had been in the Wardens' infirmary for ten days with his ribs strapped, and the Wardens' old doctor had given him something for the cough that worked a little and something for the pain that worked more, and he had been sitting up every day in the greenhouse in a chair by the lamps, because he said the green smelled like nothing he could remember. She came in at seven with her watering can and he was in his chair. He had a black coat over his knees. He had a bead on a cord round his neck.

She stopped in the doorway.

"Sit down," said Pike.

"What's that."

"Sit down, Isra."

She did not sit down. She stood in the doorway with the watering can in her hand and looked at the bead at his throat, a nub of cloudy glass on a black cord, lying very slightly to the left. Toward the mouth.

"The Deacon came up last night," Pike said. "With the list. For the Keeping. They've got eighty-one. They want a hundred. He asked the infirmary if any of us wanted to give ourselves." He looked down at the coat on his knees. "I said yes."

"No."

"I've said it, girl."

"*No.*" She put the watering can down, very carefully, because otherwise she would have thrown it. "You can't. Pike. You can't. You don't know — I didn't tell you, I didn't tell you what we found, Lucan and me, I should have, I didn't want you to — " She was across the greenhouse, she was on her knees beside his chair, she had his big ruined hand in both hers. "It's choking. The Needle. It's not hungry, it's *full*. It's full of glass. Every Keeping makes it worse. Every skiff. We counted, Pike, we counted the layers, there's one for every Keeping, there's fifty-nine of them and they're killing it — " She was crying. She did not care. "If you go in with a cradle of glass you're not saving it, you're making it worse. You're one more layer. You'll be one more layer, Pike, you'll be — "

"I know," said Pike.

She stopped.

"I heard you," he said. "In the hall. At night. You two think you're quiet." He turned his hand over in hers, and held hers back, very gently, the way he held the grapple controls. "I've been lying in that infirmary listening through the vents to the two of you counting. Fifty-nine." He was quiet for a while. "I believe you. For what it's worth. I believe you."

"Then *why*."

He looked out at the beans, at the green, at the lamps.

"Couple of reasons," he said. "My sister's got three kids in the Fourth Terrace. The Lantern's promised them rations till they're grown, if I go. They'll keep that. They keep that sort of thing; it's how they get the next hundred." He coughed, and waited for it to stop. "And I'm dying, Isra. You know I am. The doctor here's an honest old man. He gives me till the autumn, if I'm lucky. I'd rather not do the autumn. I've seen the autumn. My father did the autumn."

"That's not a reason."

"No," he agreed. "It's not." He was quiet. "The boy," he said.

She went still.

"At the Fifty-Ninth. The one who turned round. *I don't want to. I'm sorry. I don't want to.*" He said it in a flat soft voice, exactly as he had said it in Hanne's room. "I was on the second gun. I've been thinking for eleven years that somebody ought to go in for him. Into the place he didn't want to go. So he's not there on his own." He looked at her. "That's stupid. I know it's stupid. I know it'll make the glass thicker and I know it won't help him and I know he's not anywhere, he's just dead. I know. It's not a reason. It's just what I've got."

Isra knelt on the greenhouse floor with his hand in hers and could not speak.

"Do your bell," said Pike. "You and the Warden boy. Hanne's note. Do it. I heard that too." Something moved in his beard that might have been a smile. "I'll be out there in a skiff with a cradle of glass and a bead on. If you ring the thing, and it works, and I'm not in yet — " He shrugged, carefully, with his strapped ribs. "Then maybe I don't go in. Maybe I turn round. Maybe I'm the one who turns round, this time." He looked down at her. "And there's nobody on the second gun."

"And if it doesn't work?"

"Then I'm one more layer," said Ostap Pike. "I've been one more layer all my life, girl. I'm used to it."

He let go of her hand and put the black coat round his shoulders, slowly, with his ribs, and got up out of the chair, and stood for a moment among the beans in the green light, huge and stooped and grey, with the bead at his throat.

"They want us at the Lantern House tonight," he said. "For the blessing. All the Keepers. On the shuttle. I'll see you on the night, Isra." He touched her hair, once, the way Hanne used to. "Mind the shear at the mouth. It comes from behind."

# Chapter 16: The Last Ship

*The Concession · 2291*

Bettany brought him the memorandum on the twenty-second, at two in the morning, in the Lectern, with her hands shaking so badly that she dropped the crystal twice before she got it into his palm.

"She gave me her key," she said. "On the twenty-seventh. To file the short-term projections to her channel. She said it would save time. She never asked for it back." She was white to the lips. "I've been sitting on it for three weeks. I didn't — I thought if I didn't look I wouldn't have to know. And then he died. And then you went to the shaft-head and I heard — the guards were talking about it, in the canteen, about the breath — and I thought, I looked her in the face and told her when the pause was, and Corrigan Dace — " She stopped. "So I looked. Tonight. It's all there. All her files. That's the one you want. I think that's the one."

He fed it into his reader. It was the one.

*To the Board of the Ostrander Concern, from H. Vane, Director, Reach Concession. 20 March 2291. Confidential. Subject: Continuity of brine-glass revenue following the Withdrawal: a proposal for governance by covenant.*

He read it through twice. It was nine pages long, and it was very good, and it said exactly what she had told him across her desk, and more, in numbered paragraphs with headings: that the Narrowing was almost certainly natural; that the Concession's senior reader could find no mechanism by which brine-glass could affect it; that this was immaterial. That a population which believes its survival depends on a periodic sacrifice, administered by an authority which alone can read the conditions for it, will maintain that authority indefinitely and at negligible cost. That the sacrifice should be voluntary, honoured, and rewarded, so as to be sought rather than resented. That the authority should be local, permanent, and quasi-religious in character, so as to survive changes of personnel. *Paragraph 14: It is recommended that the undersigned remain in the Reach as its first head.* And at the bottom, in a different typeface, a single line: *Approved by the Board, 1 April 2291. Proceed.*

He took the crystal out of the reader and held it for a while.

"Go to bed, Bettany," he said. "Don't come in tomorrow. Tell them you're ill. Tell them anything." He looked at her. "And thank you."

She went. He sat alone under the dome with the memorandum on its crystal in one hand and the fork in the other, and the buoy's feed in his ears, the small hard ringing at the waist that had begun on the second of April when the Brannock's glass went in and had not stopped since.

He had spent a week on the ringing. He knew what it was now. He had built it, in the end, out of nothing but listening: the note of the thing, its pitch, the way it answered the throat's noise when the noise went over it, how big a thing would have to be to ring like that and where it would have to sit. It was glass. Corrigan's glass. Two tonnes of it, released at the waist on the second of April. It had not burned. It had not dispersed. It had not braced or fed or splinted anything. It had gone, on release, into the one place in the universe where everything was already bent toward it — and it had *stuck*. Settled onto the throat's wall at the narrowest point, the way frost settles on the coldest pane, and stayed there, a little hard crystalline scar, ringing.

So he had done the other sum. The one he had not wanted to do.

Nine ships at every Narrowing, the memorandum said. Two tonnes a ship. Every four thousand days, for as long as the covenant lasted, which was meant to be forever. Each Keeping laying down its scar on the last. He did it carefully, conservatively, three times. It came out the same each time, give or take a few Keepings either way. At the Concession's proposed rate, the throat would begin to fail — truly fail, narrow and not recover — at around the fiftieth Keeping. It would close, for good, at around the sixtieth.

Six hundred and fifty years.

He copied the sum onto the spool beside the swell and the prediction and the sound. He copied the memorandum onto it. Then, at nine in the morning, because he was a fool, because he could not help believing that somewhere under the steady grey eyes there was a person who did arithmetic and would care about this one, he went up to the Gallery for the last time.

She heard him out. She always did.

He told her about the scar. He did not tell her about the memorandum; he was not quite that much of a fool. He laid out the ringing, the pitch, the mass, and then the sum, and he watched her follow it, step by step, as she had followed the swell; and he saw her reach the end of it, and saw her understand it exactly.

"Sixty Keepings," she said.

"Give or take."

"Six hundred and fifty years."

"And then the door shuts, Director. For good. Not because it was starving. Because you choked it. With the glass you said would feed it." He leaned on her desk. "Your Board wants the glass to come through that door forever. That's what this is for. All of it. Well — it won't. Not if you do this. You'll be closing the door you're selling. Slowly. But you'll be closing it."

Hesper Vane was quiet for a long moment.

"Six hundred years, Ambrose," she said at last.

He stared at her.

"Six hundred and fifty, you said. Give or take." She folded her hands. "I'm forty-seven. The Board's average age is seventy-one. The Concern has existed for two hundred and twelve years and has had four names. The Reach has had people in it for fifty." She looked at him almost gently. "In six hundred years there will be no Concern, and no Board, and very possibly no Ardent, and certainly nobody alive who has ever heard of either of us. There will be no one to blame. There will barely be anyone to remember." She shrugged. "Whoever is sitting in this room in six hundred years will have to find their own answer. I expect they'll find the same one I have. People usually do."

"They'll be dying," he said. "The people in six hundred years. Because of this. Because of you."

"Everyone in six hundred years will be dying," said Hesper Vane. "That isn't a moral argument, Ambrose. It's a description of time." She held out her hand across the desk, palm up. "Give me the spool."

He did not move.

"You have it on a spool," she said. "Of course you do. The swell, the prediction, the sound. And now this. Give it to me. You can sail on the twenty-eighth with your berth and your fork and your daughter's letters, all of it, I've said so. But the spool stays here."

"No," said Ambrose.

She looked at him with her hand still out. Then she drew it back and laid it on the desk, folded over the other, exactly as before.

"Very well," she said. "Thank you for coming up. Go back to the Lectern, Ambrose. Read the throat. Give me the numbers."

He went. At the door he looked back. She had not moved. She was sitting behind the desk with her hands folded, looking at the mouth through the window, and he thought — he would think it all that day, and he would think it at the end — that she looked, for the first time in eleven years, tired.

He did not hear her call Marshal Brandt. But he knew, walking down the long curve of the spindle with the spool in his pocket, that she would.

* * *

He sent for her on the twenty-fourth, at midnight, with a note under the door of her bunk-cube in Ambrose's small neat hand: *Lectern. Now. Come the back way, through the archive. Please.* She did not know there was a back way. She found it: a service crawl from the pilots' dorm level up the inside of the spindle that came out behind the steel drawers of the Lectern's archive, among the old paper rolls. He was waiting there. He had not slept; she could see that. He had a spool in his hand.

"Take this," he said.

"What is it?"

"Everything." He put it into her palm and closed her fingers round it with both of his hands. "The swell. The prediction. The sound — you heard the sound. And two other things. Listen to me, Teo. I don't have long."

He told her. The ringing at the waist, that had begun when the Brannock's glass went in. What it was. The sum. *Sixty Keepings. Six hundred and fifty years. And then it closes.* And then, in a lower voice, the memorandum: *governance by covenant*. *Paragraph fourteen.* *Approved by the Board, first of April. Proceed.* The day before Corrigan flew.

She stood among the paper rolls with the spool in her fist and listened, and she did not interrupt, and when he had finished she did not say anything for a long time.

"The Board knew," she said at last.

"The Board approved it."

"So Ardent's no good. Your plan. The *Providence*, the newspapers, the inspectors — "

"Some of them might listen." He did not sound as though he believed it. "Some. The courts, perhaps. But the Board knows, Teo. The Board wrote *proceed*. Whatever I put in front of anyone in Ardent, the Board will have been there first, saying it's a scientist's hobby and has no bearing on policy." He let go of her hand. "So it has to be here. It has to be the Reach. Not Ardent. The pilots. The cutters. The people who'll be asked to fly and cut and give, every eleven years, forever. They have to hear it. All of it. The breath and the scar and the paper with *proceed* on it. And they have to hear it from somewhere she can't talk over."

"Then broadcast it. Tonight. From here. You've got the harp's transmitter, you've got every channel — "

"She's cut it," said Ambrose. "This afternoon. The Lectern's transmitter goes to the Director's channel and nowhere else, as of three o'clock." He smiled slightly. "She's very thorough."

"Then I'll do it. The Mercy's got a set. I'll take it out to the lanes and — "

"The Mercy's impounded. Since this evening. There are four of Brandt's men on Dock Four." He saw her face and put a hand on her arm. "I'm sorry. I checked before I sent for you. It's why I sent for you. Because they'll be coming here next, and I wanted it in somebody's hands who isn't me before they do."

She stared at him. "Coming for you."

"Yes."

"Then *come with me*. Now. The crawl. We'll go down to the Pans, we'll get you to the shaft-head, there's two hundred cutters there who'll — "

"Who'll be broken tomorrow morning," said Ambrose gently. "She told me. Not in so many words. Marshal Brandt has orders to clear the shaft-head at dawn. If I'm there, they'll have a reason to be rough about it." He shook his head. "No. You go. You go now, with that, and you keep it, and you find a way. I don't know what way. I'm a harp man, Teo, I've never in my life known what to *do*." He laughed, a little, and it cracked in the middle. "But you have. I saw you, at the inner markers. You turned into the waves before I'd finished calling them."

Somewhere out in the dome, beyond the archive's steel drawers, the door slid open. They heard it. Footsteps, heavy, several. A voice, mild, unhurried: "Mr Lyle? Marshal Brandt. The Director would like a word."

Ambrose looked at her. He put his hand in his jacket pocket. For a moment she thought he was going to give her the fork as well, and she saw him think it, too — saw his hand close round it in the pocket, and his face change, and the decision go through it.

He did not. He left his hand where it was, round the fork, in his pocket.

"For Clare," he said, very low. "If you ever — no. Never mind. It doesn't matter." He pushed her toward the crawl. "Go. *Go*, Teo."

She went. She went into the crawl on her hands and knees with the spool in her fist, and the panel slid shut behind her, and she lay in the dark in the narrow tube with her face against cold steel and listened.

She heard Ambrose go out of the archive into the dome. She heard him say, quite calmly, "Good evening, Marshal." She heard Brandt say something she could not catch. She heard Ambrose say, "No, I don't think I will," and then a silence; and then, faintly, the sound of the harp. He had struck something, or touched it. The whole dome rang with it, the thin high strained chord of the Narrowing, and under it, so faint she might have imagined it, a single clear note, pure and thin, laid across the chord like a ruler.

Then voices, raised. Then something heavy. Then the harp again, all its strings at once, a long discord, as though somebody had fallen against it.

Then nothing.

She lay in the crawl until her arms stopped shaking. Then she went down through the spindle in the dark, the way she had come, with the spool inside her coveralls against her skin, next to the bead.

* * *

The notice went out on every screen at six in the morning on the twenty-fifth, between the throat report and the quota boards, in the same plain type as both.

*The Concession regrets to announce the death of Senior Throat-Reader Ambrose Lyle, who fell from the maintenance gallery of the Lectern late last night while making adjustments to the instrument. Mr Lyle served the Concession for twenty-three years. His work will be continued by Miss B. Shore.*

Teo read it on a screen on the Pans shuttle, halfway down to Hollin, with her helmet on her knees and the spool inside her coveralls. She read it three times. *Fell.* *Making adjustments.* She thought of the harp's long discord in the dark, all its strings at once. She put her hand flat over the place on her chest where the spool lay and did not take it away until the shuttle landed.

She went to the shaft-head because there was nowhere else to go, and because Ambrose had said *at dawn*, and it was dawn.

It was over by the time she got there.

She came up the access road from the Pans at a run and stopped at the top of it, where you could see down into the yard, and stood. The fence was down. The gate was off its hinges. There were Security men everywhere, sixty, eighty, in grey, moving through the yard in twos with their batons, unhurried, the way they did everything; and the cutters were sitting on the salt in long rows with their hands on their heads. The canteen door was broken in. There was smoke coming out of it, not much. On the cage-head step, where Lulu had scratched Corrigan's name, three shapes lay under sheets.

She found Mother Salome by the cage-head, kneeling beside the sheets with the Tally across her knees.

"They came at five," said Salome, without looking up. Her voice was flat. "Through the fence. A hundred of them. They said we'd killed a Security man in the night. We hadn't. Nobody had been near the fence all night. They said we had." She lifted the edge of the nearest sheet and looked under it and put it down again. "Fennick tried to stop them coming into the canteen. With his one hand. They hit him. He didn't get up. And then the others tried to help him and they hit them." She closed her eyes. "Three. Fennick and the Okonjo boy and old Ruth from the cage."

"Nico," said Teo.

"They took him." Salome opened her eyes. "First. Before anyone. They knew exactly which one he was. They put him in the wagon in chains and Brandt read him a charge off a slate. The murder of a Security officer." She looked at Teo at last. "He'll hang, Teodora. That's what Brandt said. To his face. Under the Concession's articles, a cutter who kills a Security man in a strike hangs. On the Pans. In front of everybody."

Teo sat down on the salt beside her. She did not decide to. Her legs went.

"Ambrose is dead," she said.

"I saw."

"He didn't fall."

"No," said Mother Salome. "I didn't suppose he did."

She held out the stylus. It took Teo a long moment to understand. Then she took it, and Salome tilted the Tally on her knees, and Teo looked down at the plate of old steel, the thirty-one names at the top in Salome's careful capitals, the rows below in a dozen hands, and at the bottom, small and wavering, C. DACE — PILOT.

"He wasn't a shaft man either," said Salome. "Lyle. He was a Concern man. He read their harp for twenty-three years." She looked at the sheets on the cage-head step. "But he came here in the dark and played us the breath. And they killed him for it. That makes him ours."

Teo scratched it. A. LYLE. Her hand was not steady. When she had finished she looked at it for a while, and then, under it, smaller, she scratched: HARP.

Then she sat beside Mother Salome on the salt while the men in grey moved through the yard, and Fennick and the Okonjo boy and old Ruth from the cage lay under their sheets, and somewhere in Tallow her brother sat in chains; and she held the spool through her coveralls with one hand and the bead with the other, and did not cry, and did not know what to do.

* * *

Brandt came for her on the twenty-seventh. Not with men; alone. He found her in the pilots' dorm, in her bunk-cube, where she had been for two days with the door locked, and he knocked politely and waited, and when she opened the door he said, "The Director would like to see you, Miss Kesh. I'm to bring you up. Will you come, or shall I fetch help?"

She went.

Hesper Vane was standing at the window when Teo came in, and this time she turned round at once.

Teo had never been in the Gallery. She had seen it on the screens behind the Director's head a hundred times — the curved window, the mouth hanging in it like a painting — and it was smaller than she had thought, and emptier, and the rug on the floor was the colour of old blood. Hesper Vane came across it to her and stopped and looked at her, a slight straight woman with grey cropped hair, a head shorter than Teo; and she was holding something in her right hand, turning it slowly in her fingers, the way you might turn a pen while you think.

It was a tuning fork. Brass. Fifteen centimetres long. Its tines worn bright.

Teo looked at it, and something inside her went very cold and very still.

"Mr Lyle's effects," said Hesper Vane, following her look. "He had no family here. They came to me." She turned the fork once more and set it down on the desk, carefully, on a sheet of paper, as if it might mark the wood. "Sit down, Miss Kesh."

"I'll stand."

"As you like." The Director went back behind the desk and sat, and folded her hands. "I'll be brief. Your brother is charged with the murder of a Security officer during an illegal strike. The penalty under the Concession's articles is death. The Marshal has a witness."

"He didn't do it."

"I know he didn't," said Hesper Vane. "The Marshal's witness is lying, and the Marshal knows he is lying, and so do I. That's rather the point." She did not look away. "Your brother will hang on the Pans on the fourth of May. Unless."

Teo waited.

"The Keeping flies on the first," said the Director. "I have eight pilots. I need nine. I would like the ninth to be the pilot who flew the trial escort to the inner markers and held there three hours under fire. The best tug pilot on the station, Mr Lyle said. He was usually right about people." She paused. "Fly the Keeping. Go to the waist with a cradle of glass and release it. And the charge against your brother is withdrawn on the morning of the second, and he walks out of the cells, and his debts are struck from the books, and yours. Every mark. Today. I'll do it in front of you."

"And if I don't come back?"

"Then he walks out anyway, free and clear, and you'll be remembered." A small pause. "The Keepers of the First Keeping will be remembered for a very long time. I've seen to that."

Teo looked at the fork on the desk.

"Did Mr Lyle give you anything?" said Hesper Vane. "Before he died?"

"No."

The grey eyes rested on her. Then the Director nodded, as she had nodded at Ambrose, as if Teo had given her an answer.

"No," she said. "Well. If he had, it wouldn't matter. I want you to understand that, Miss Kesh. Whatever he might have given anyone — it wouldn't matter at all." She unfolded her hands. "Yes or no."

Teo stood on the blood-coloured rug and thought of Nico in chains on the Pans. She thought of Corrigan going away from her into the fire, getting smaller wrong. She thought of an old man in a cardigan on the dock floor with his hands over his face, and of a single clear note laid across the harp's chord in the dark, and then the discord. She thought of the spool, which was not on her; which was, at this moment, sealed inside the casing of the Small Mercy's flight recorder on Dock Four, under the noses of four of Brandt's men, where she had put it at three in the morning by going in through the tug's belly hatch from the maintenance trench underneath, where no one had thought to look. She thought of the throat. Nine hours to Ardent, in an ordinary week.

"Yes," she said.

"Thank you," said Hesper Vane. She picked up a slate and touched it and turned it round so that Teo could see. *KESH, T. — 40,712 M. — CANCELLED. KESH, N. — 18,900 M. — CANCELLED.* She touched it again and the figures were gone. "There."

Teo looked at the empty screen. Forty thousand seven hundred and twelve marks. She had carried them for twenty years. She felt nothing at all.

The Director held out her hand. In it, on a black cord, there was a bead. A nub of cloudy brine-glass the size of a pea.

"We're giving one to each of the Keepers," she said. "So that you'll carry a piece of it in with you. So that you'll be part of it." Her mouth moved. "It was my idea. I thought it would be remembered."

Teo looked at the bead in the Director's palm. Then she put her own hand up, slowly, and touched the one at her throat. Nico's. Cut from his first seam when he was sixteen. Lying, as it had lain all her life, a little to the left. A little toward the mouth.

"I've got one," she said.

She took the Director's anyway, because Brandt was standing at the door, and put it in her pocket. Then she turned and walked out across the red rug, past Brandt, and the door slid shut behind her, and she did not look back.

The *Ostrander Providence* sailed at noon on the twenty-eighth.

Teo watched her go from the window of the pilots' dorm: three hundred metres of hull with the company sunburst on her flank, backing out of her dock at the pace a big ship did everything, turning, lining up on the green in-lane, the last of the beacon strings that would be lit for a week. She went into the mouth an hour later. The throat was so narrow by then that the readers had given her a single window of forty minutes, and a throat-hauler's reader aboard, and she went in on it. Teo watched her get smaller. Get smaller wrong. Stretched, pulled long, thinned to a line of light, and gone.

Cabin twelve was empty. Senior grade. Teo did not know how she knew that, but she did.

She stood at the window for a long time after the *Providence* had gone, looking at the mouth: the rim of grey-gold fire wound tight, tighter than she had ever seen it; the bruise at the centre shrunk to a dark pin. Three days. Three days to the bottom. She thought of the spool in the Mercy's flight recorder. She thought, *Nine hours, in an ordinary week.* She thought, *Nobody has ever flown it in a Narrowing. Nobody has ever been to the waist and come back. Nobody has ever gone through.*

She put her hand over Nico's bead, and held it, and felt it lean, very gently, insistently, against her palm. Toward the mouth.

# Chapter 17: The Inheritance

*The Severance · 2608*

The message came to the *Vigil* folded inside a ration wrapper, in the hand of a novice of sixteen who had run the length of the Anchorhold's spindle to bring it and stood in the airlock afterwards shaking so hard he could not speak.

*Sister Quill taken to the penitents' cells on the 25th by order of the H.W. Charge: heresy. No visitors. They took her notebooks. Please.*

There was no signature. The novice would not give his name. He went back up the spindle at a run before anyone could stop him, and Juno stood in the airlock with the ration wrapper in her hand and read it twice, and then went to find Nan Hollis.

The Chief was in the engine room with her arms in a coolant manifold to the elbow. She heard Juno out without taking them out.

"The penitents' cells," Hollis said. "The old Concession lock-up. Bottom of the spindle, Needle-ward end, under the docks. Six cells and a guardroom. Built for drunks and strikers." She pulled her arms out and wiped them on a rag. "I did a refit on the life support down there when I was a rating. Twenty years ago. The ventilation's on the same bus as the dock lighting. You kill the dock lighting, you kill the air in the cells, the doors open on a failsafe so nobody suffocates. They've never fixed it. Nobody fixes anything on that station that the Wardens can't see from the chapel."

Juno looked at her.

"You're asking me if I'll do it," said Hollis. "I'm fifty. I've been in this ship six years. I pulled seven of our people out of the starboard plating after the raid in pieces." She threw the rag down. "I'll do it."

They did it on the evening of the twenty-seventh, eight days before the Narrowing, while the High Warden was in Tallow Under at the Compact Hall for the blessing of the four new Keepers — four new names, Juno did not doubt, and one of them from a sleeve. There were five of them: Juno, Hollis, Marsh, and two ratings from the engine room whom Hollis vouched for and who did not ask what they were doing. They went down to the docks in damage-control suits with a work order for a coolant leak that Hollis had written herself. At nineteen hundred Hollis opened a panel under Dock Two and did something inside it with a pair of insulated pliers, and every light on the Anchorhold's docking level went out at once.

In the dark the cell doors sighed open, one after another, down the corridor: six soft pneumatic breaths.

There were two guards. They were Warden lay-brothers, not soldiers, and they had no idea what was happening, and Marsh put one of them on the floor with a hand over his mouth while the rating sat on the other, and Juno went down the dark corridor with her suit lamp on, looking into each open door.

Adaeze was in the fourth.

She was sitting on the bunk with her back against the wall and her knees drawn up, in her grey, with no spectacles. Her face was swollen down one side. Her bandaged hands were in her lap, and the bandages were dirty, and one of them had been unwrapped and wrapped again badly. She looked up into Juno's lamp and blinked, and did not seem surprised.

"Commander," she said. "They told me you'd come. I said you wouldn't be so stupid."

"I'm very stupid," said Juno. "Can you walk?"

"I can walk." She got up, slowly. "They didn't hurt me much. One of the lay-brothers hit me, on the first night. The High Warden was very angry with him afterwards. He doesn't like that sort of thing." She swayed, and Juno caught her. "He came every evening. Sat on the end of the bunk. Talked. He wanted me to understand, he said. He was very — patient."

"Did they find the crystals?"

Something moved in the archivist's swollen face that might have been a smile. "Two," she said. "Not the third."

"Where's the third?"

"Under the Fork," said Adaeze. "Inside the cushion. I unpicked a seam on the night I gave it back to him." She leaned on Juno's arm. "He'd never think to look there. Nobody would. It's the one thing in the Anchorhold nobody touches."

They got her out into the dark corridor. Marsh had the guards tied with their own belts. Hollis was at the far end with the panel open, waiting.

"Commander," said Adaeze. She had stopped, with her hand on the cell door. "There's something else. He told me, in there. On the third night. I think he wanted somebody to know. I think he's wanted somebody to know for a very long time." She was breathing hard. "There's a book. In his cabinet, in the gallery. The black one with the lock. They call it the Inheritance. Every High Warden is given it on the night of investiture, by the one before, and reads it alone, and keeps it. The First Warden wrote it." She held Juno's arm tightly. "He said that when he read it, at forty-one, he went out on the gallery afterwards and stood at the window for the whole night and thought about stepping off. And then in the morning he didn't. And then he was High Warden for twenty-five years." She looked up at Juno in the dark. "He said everyone who's read it has stood at that window."

"Where is he now?"

"At the Hall. Till midnight. The blessing." Adaeze's grip did not loosen. "Commander. If we're going to do this — whatever we're doing — I want to read it. I want it in my hands when I stand in that harp hall. Not his word. Not my traces. *Theirs.*"

Juno looked at the dark corridor, the open cells, the two lay-brothers tied on the floor, Hollis at the panel, Marsh with his sidearm drawn and his face very white. Five hours till midnight. The High Warden's gallery was at the top of the spindle, the far end of the station.

"Hollis," she said. "How long can you keep the docks dark?"

* * *

The cabinet was black wood, old beyond anything in the Anchorhold but the harp, and its lock was brass, and Nan Hollis looked at it for about four seconds and then opened it with a cutter's bar and a word Adaeze had not heard since she was a child on the Dockside.

Inside, on a shelf, there was a book.

That was all. Not a reliquary, not a casket. A book, bound in black leather gone brown at the corners, the size of a daybook, tied with a cord. Adaeze took it out with her bandaged hands and held it, and the leather was soft with handling, the way the Founders' Tally was soft, the way anything is that has been held by a great many hands for a very long time.

She had already been to the reliquary. They had gone that way, through the harp hall, in the dark, with the harp singing its false drone above them; and she had unlocked the grille with her archivist's key, which they had not thought to take from her, and lifted the Fork from its cushion, and felt inside the unpicked seam, and the third crystal had been there. She had put both in her robe. The Fork lay against her ribs now, cold and heavy. She had not quite meant to take it. She had found that she could not leave it.

"Read it," said Juno. "Quickly. We've got an hour, maybe less."

Adaeze sat down in the High Warden's chair, at the High Warden's desk, in the long low gallery with the mouth hanging outside the window like a painting of nothing, and untied the cord, and opened the book.

The first page said, in a small upright hand that she knew — she had seen it ten days ago at the head of a scorched daybook, *henceforth the harp is to be tuned to the H.T. and not otherwise* — the first page said: *To my successor.*

She read.

She read aloud, because Juno asked her to, and because she found she could not read it silently; her lips moved anyway. Juno stood behind her. Marsh was at the door with his sidearm. Hollis was at the window, watching the docks below, where the lights were still out.

*To my successor. You will have been told by now that the Narrowing of the Needle is a hunger, and that the Keeping feeds it, and that the door stays open because the Reach gives. None of this is true. I want you to know it from me, in my own hand, so that you need never doubt it, and never hope otherwise.*

*The Narrowing is a natural event. The Needle breathes. It has two breaths, a short and a long, and when they fall together it goes deep, and then it comes back. It has always come back. It will always come back. Nothing that is done to it alters this in the least. I had this from the Concession's own reader, who heard it first, before the First Keeping, and proved it, and whom I could not persuade to keep it to himself.*

Adaeze stopped. Behind her, Juno had gone very still.

"Go on," said Juno.

*I instituted the Keeping not to keep the door but to keep the Reach. A people who believe that their survival depends upon a sacrifice, which only one authority knows how to make, will keep that authority. They will keep it through any hardship and any change. They will keep it after everyone who began it is dead. That is what I have made, and what I leave to you: not a rite, but a government. You are its head. You will find it heavy. Carry it.*

*To that end I have taken the Concession's harp records into my own keeping and destroyed them, so that no reader after me can lay the old traces beside the new and see the breath in them. To that end I have ordered the harp tuned to a note of my own choosing, which I have called the Hunger Tone, and which is so chosen that a harp tuned to it can hear the waist narrow and the waist widen, but cannot hear why. To that end I have had the Keepers flown at the hour the waist begins to turn, and not before; and the readers will tell you the hour, and you will tell them it is the hour of the Keeping, and they will believe that it is because it is.*

"Oh," said Adaeze, very quietly, to the page.

*You will be tempted, as I was, to think the Keepers a price too high. Consider what else would hold the Reach together, with the Inward a door away and nobody on this side of it who owes anyone anything. Consider what the alternative is. I considered it for a long time. I believe it is fire. You may believe otherwise. If you do, you may break the covenant, and you will find out.*

*And let the High Warden hold one name in every giving. Not by lot. So that the Reach is reminded, though it never knows it, who keeps it.*

Juno made a sound. Adaeze did not look round.

There was more. Pages of it, in the same small upright hand. Instructions. How to choose readers who would not ask. How to keep the order's books so that the gap at the beginning would look like nothing but a gap. How to manage an Assembly, if there came to be one. How to speak to the families of Keepers who did not come back. It was all very clear and very calm and very well written, and none of it was cruel, exactly; it was simply perfectly certain, all the way through, the way a proof is certain. And at the end of it, on a last leaf folded over and sealed with a disc of grey wax the size of a coin, there was a line on the outside of the fold, in the same hand:

*My name, for my successor's eyes. It is not to be spoken. The order has none but the one I gave it.*

Adaeze put her bandaged thumb on the grey wax.

"Don't," said a voice from the door.

Cassius Mor stood in it. He was in his grey with the silver at the cuffs, and the four lay-brothers behind him in the passage had sidearms, which Adaeze had never in her life seen a Warden carry, and Marsh's own sidearm was up and pointing at the High Warden's chest, and his hand was quite steady.

"Don't break the seal, Sister," said the High Warden. "Not like this. Not in a hurry, in the dark, with a gun at the door." He looked at her, sitting in his chair with the book open in front of her, and his face was very tired and very kind. "I came back early. I had a feeling. When the docks went dark." He looked at the book. "You've read it, then."

"Yes."

"All of it?"

"All but the name."

He nodded slowly. "I stood at that window," he said, "for nine hours, the night I read it. Did you know that? I expect I told you, in the cell. I'm sorry. I talk too much to people I've locked up." He came one step into the room. Marsh's gun followed him. "Adaeze. Listen to me. You think the book is a confession. It isn't. It's a gift. It's the hardest gift anyone has ever been given, and every one of us who has carried it has carried it because the alternative was worse. You've read what the alternative is."

"Fire," said Adaeze.

"Fire." He held out his hand. "Give it to me. And the Fork. And go back to your cell, and on the sixth of December, when the door has opened and the Keepers have come home, we'll talk about what you've read, the two of us, as long as you like. I'll answer every question. I promise you that. I've wanted to, for twenty-five years."

"And Pell?" said Juno.

He turned his head and looked at her for the first time.

"Your sister," said Cassius Mor, "will fly into a door that is already opening, and turn, and come home. As most of them do. As she is very likely to, with the best escort in the Navy." His voice was gentle. "I chose her because your mother needed choosing, Commander. I'm sorry. I'm sorrier than you will ever believe. I chose her also because she is the best of the nine, and because I knew you would be there, and because I thought — I truly thought — that of all of them she was the most likely to come back. I've done that every Draw. Chosen someone who'd come back." He looked at the book. "That's not in there. That's mine."

Juno did not answer. Her face was like stone.

"Give me the book, Sister," said the High Warden, and took one more step.

"Sir — " said Marsh. "Sir, don't — "

One of the lay-brothers in the passage, a boy, nineteen, frightened, who had never held a sidearm before that night and had been told the people in the High Warden's gallery were Shoals agents, saw the High Warden move toward the gun and fired.

He missed the High Warden. He hit Danny Marsh in the chest.

Afterward Adaeze would remember it in pieces. Marsh sitting down suddenly against the door frame with a surprised look. Mor turning, crying out *No* — a real cry, an old man's cry, anguished. The boy in the passage dropping his gun as if it had burned him. Hollis coming across the room from the window like a ship under full burn and taking the High Warden by the collar and the arm and putting him face first onto his own red carpet. Juno on her knees by Marsh with her hands on his chest and his blood coming up between her fingers.

"Captain," said Marsh. He looked puzzled. "Captain, I think — "

"I know. Don't talk. Hollis, *medical* — "

"Galley flour bin," said Marsh, and smiled, a little, as if it were a joke between them, and stopped breathing.

Juno knelt there with her hands on him. Then she took them away and stood up. Her face had not changed at all.

"Bring the book," she said to Adaeze. "Bring the Fork. Hollis. Leave him."

Hollis let go of the High Warden. Mor did not get up. He lay on his face on the red carpet in his grey, where the path was worn to the weft, and he did not get up, and he did not say anything, and as Adaeze went past him with the Inheritance against her chest she saw that his shoulders were shaking.

* * *

Pell's door was on the way.

It was not, really. It was two passages out of the way, on the guest level of the spindle, where the five surviving Keepers and the four new ones were sequestered in the High Warden's own rooms; and the docks were still dark, and the station's alarm had begun, a long slow pulse through every bulkhead, and there were lay-brothers with sidearms somewhere behind them. Juno went anyway. Nobody tried to stop her. Hollis took Adaeze on toward Dock Four with the book and the Fork, and Juno ran.

Her sister opened the door before she knocked. She had heard the alarm. She was dressed, in the plain white of the Keepers, with the bead at her throat, and her hair cut short, and her face very calm.

She looked at Juno's hands.

"Whose is that?"

"Danny's." Juno heard her own voice from a long way off. "Danny Marsh. My XO. A Warden shot him. Ten minutes ago. In the High Warden's gallery." She took a breath. "Pell. Get your coat. You're coming with me. Now."

Pell did not move.

"Pell, listen to me. It's a lie. All of it. The Keeping. It doesn't do anything. It never has. The archivist — Sister Quill — she proved it, she tuned the harp to the Fork and it *breathes*, Pell, it opens by itself, it always has. And the Draw's rigged. Mor chose you. He told me so. To my face. He had your name in his sleeve, he picked you to make Mum vote for the war, and he's got a book in his cabinet that the First Warden wrote, that says all of it, every word — we've got it, Adaeze has it, she's on the way to the *Vigil* with it now. It's a *lie*. You don't have to go. Nobody has to go. Get your coat."

Her sister looked at her. Behind Pell, in the common room, the other Keepers had come to their doors and were standing in them in their white, listening. Abbe Sorrell. The lift-mechanic. Four new faces Juno did not know.

"Is that true?" said Pell.

"Yes."

"All of it?"

"Every word."

Pell was quiet. Then she said, very gently, "Then say it where everyone can hear it."

"What?"

"Say it in the hall. On the Long Night. On the harp. When everyone's listening." Pell's eyes were wet and steady. "Not to me, in a corridor, with blood on your hands, in the middle of an alarm. You're my sister. I'd believe anything you told me in a corridor. That's why I can't." She put her hand on Juno's arm, on the dirty sleeve of the damage-control suit. "If it's true, Juno, it's true for all of us. For Abbe. For Ezra. For every one of the hundred and twelve. Not just for me because I'm the Speaker's daughter and my sister came to get me." She swallowed. "I won't run. If it's a lie, I want to hear it said out loud, to everyone, at once. And then I'll know what to do."

"Pell — "

"Go," said her sister. "They're coming. I can hear them. Go."

Juno went.

The *Vigil* left the Anchorhold at forty minutes past midnight with her docking clamps cut instead of released, which tore four metres of plating off the Anchorhold's Dock Four and was entered in the Compact Navy's log, the next morning, as an act of mutiny. She went out on full burn with her running lights dark, past the *Ardour*, whose captain was a fat frightened decent man called Pryce who had been ordered to stop her and who, for reasons he never afterwards explained to the board of inquiry, did not.

Juno stood on her bridge with Marsh's blood dried brown on her hands and her crew at their stations. She had told them, in the airlock, all of it, in four sentences, and said that the shuttle was there for anyone who wanted it. Six had taken it. Forty-one had not. Hollis was in the engine room. Adaeze was in the wardroom with the book open on the table in front of her and the Fork beside it on a folded napkin, not reading, only sitting with her bandaged hands flat on the pages.

The command channel was screaming. Squadron. Admiral Greaves, his square grey face on the screen, red with fury, ordering her to heave to and surrender her ship. She let it scream for a while. Then she switched it off.

She took from the breast pocket of her uniform a small square of folded paper, soft with handling, and unfolded it, and looked at the frequency written on it in pencil. She did not need to; she had known it by heart for a month. She dialled it in.

"*Callum*," she said, on the old cadet band. "This is the *Vigil*. Juno Asante." She found that her voice was quite steady. "I'm coming over. I've got the archivist, and I've got something for her to read. Seven days, Rhee. On the night of the fifth. I'm going to need you."

There was a long silence on the band. Static. The hiss of the Shoals.

Then his voice, very close, as if he had been sitting with his hand on the set for a month waiting for it. "*Vigil, Callum. I hear you.*" A pause. "*Come home, Juno.*"

# Chapter 18: Returns

*The Ebb · 2937*

Lucan asked the Prior on the fourteenth, because there was nobody else to ask.

He had been building the coil for a week. It was under the harp's base, where it had always been — a ring of old dark copper wound round a core of something he did not recognise, wired into the frame by whoever had built the frame, six hundred years ago, for reasons nobody had written down. He had cleaned it and traced its wires and found the place where they had been cut, long ago, neatly, with a tool, and he had spliced them again with the last of Isra's resonator wire and run a lead from it to the reader's console, hidden under the floor-plates. It would take very little now. Retune the harp to the Fork. Close one switch. And the harp, which had only ever listened, would sing *out* — at the Bell, at the waist, at the thing in the throat — with every course it had.

But someone had to be standing at it when it did. And Lucan would be on the Lamplighter's ship.

So on the night of the fourteenth he went to the Prior's cell and knocked, and the old man let him in, and Lucan sat on the end of his narrow bed under the one lamp and told him everything. The strata. The fifty-nine bands. The Book of Keepings, laid alongside them. The Bell, and the socket, and the Fork. The coil. He told it plainly and in order, and the Prior sat in his chair with his two sticks across his knees and listened without moving, the way he had listened four weeks ago in the harp hall, his face like old paper.

"I need someone at the harp," said Lucan at the end. "On the night. When the Long Night begins and the skiffs go out. I need someone to tune it true and close the switch and hold it. For as long as it takes. I can't. She'll have me on her ship." He looked at the old man. "You taught me to tune it. You're the only other one who can."

Prior Saar was silent for a long time.

"Twelve," he said at last.

"I know."

"Twelve of us, Lucan. Eleven, if they take you." He looked at the lamp. "Do you know what she'll do, if you ring her throat, in front of her hundred Keepers, on the night of her Great Keeping? Whether it works or not? Do you know what she'll do to the station that did it?"

"Yes."

"And you still ask."

"I still ask," said Lucan. "Prior. Her son's in there. A hundred more are going. Ostap Pike is going, with a bead on, because he shot a boy eleven years ago and thinks he owes it. And it's choking. We're choking it. Every one of them who goes in makes it worse. You've seen the bands. You know."

"I know," said the old man. He closed his eyes. "Go to bed, Brother. I'll think on it."

Lucan went to bed.

They came for him at three in the morning, in the harp hall, where he had gone, as he always went, to retune the ninth course and read the night. There were six of them, black coats, with lamps; and the Deacon behind them, his hands clasped behind his back; and behind the Deacon, on his two sticks, at the iron door, not coming in, the Prior.

Lucan stood up from the console. He did not try to run. There was nowhere to run to.

"Brother Lucan," said the Deacon pleasantly. "Good morning. I'm told you've been tuning the First Warden's harp to the First Warden's Fork, at night, in secret, against your Prior's express orders. And that you mean to do something worse on the Long Night. Something to do with — " he glanced at the Prior — "a bell."

Lucan looked past him at the old man in the doorway.

Saar did not look away. His face was grey and perfectly still.

"Twelve," said the Prior. "I'm sorry, Lucan. Twelve."

The black coats took Lucan's arms. One of them went to the reliquary grille and lifted out its hinge-pins — they knew about the hinge-pins; the Prior had told them that too — and found the grey cushion empty, and turned, and the Deacon held out his hand, and Lucan, after a long moment, took the Fork out of his sleeve and gave it to him.

Rusk turned it over in his thin fingers, curiously, and held it up to the lamp.

"*For Cl,*" he read. "Is that all it says?"

"It's worn," said Lucan.

"Hm." The Deacon slipped it into his own coat. "The Lamplighter would still like you as her reader, Brother. She was very clear. She says you're the only one who's ever told her the truth. You'll give her the line from her ship on the night of the twenty-third, as you promised. In the meantime you'll stay in the penitents' cells, under the docks." He smiled. "I'm told they're very old. The Concession built them. For strikers."

They took him down. As he went past the Prior at the door the old man put out one hand, as if to touch his arm; and then did not; and let it fall.

* * *

The Return that came out of the mouth on the fifteenth of June was the first one Isra had ever seen come out.

She was alone in the Ossuary in the Gristle. She had been going out every day since Pike left, because the Lantern let bone-pickers work again now — every Return was tithe, and somebody had to cut it, and the Lantern's own boys were afraid of the mouth — and because if she sat in the greenhouse among the beans she would go mad. She cut what she was told to cut and gave it to the Lantern cutter at the edge of the drift, and was given rations for it, and went back to the Anchor at night and lay awake. She had not seen Lucan in two days. She did not know why. Nobody would tell her.

She was a kilometre from the mouth, in the lee of an old ore-hauler, when the mouth flickered.

She had seen it flicker all her life. Not like this. The bruised dark at the centre swelled, and went pale, and something came out of it the way a fish comes up out of dark water: slowly, and then all at once, tumbling, end over end, with a long trail of glittering dust behind it that was glass, shattering off its hull as it came, a wake of glass, sparkling in the grey light like spray.

A skiff. Small. Twelve metres. A Keeper's skiff.

She went after it before she had decided to.

It was not like the others. That was the first thing. The others came out crusted, furred, their glass grown back along their hulls like hair in a wind, centuries of it. This one had hardly any. A skin of it, thin, milky, a few years' worth — no more. Under it she could see the hull's paint. Black. And on the nose, done in yellow, with a little red flame in it, a lamp.

A Lantern skiff.

She matched spin. She put the grapple on it herself, without Pike, badly, and it took her four tries. Then she went over in her suit and stood on its hull in the grey light at the edge of the mouth and looked at it.

The engine housing at the stern was gone. Not torn; holed. Three neat round holes punched through the plating, one after another, in a line, with the metal curled inward at their edges like the petals of a flower. She knew what made holes like that. She had seen them on the hulls of Shoals wrecks from the old wars, in the deep Ossuary. Forward guns. A cutter's forward guns, at close range.

She went to the canopy. The glass on it was thin enough to see through without cutting. She put her helmet lamp to it and looked in.

He was in his seat. They always were. A boy. Seventeen at the most; a soft round face, a lot of dark curly hair, frozen. His hands were not on the yoke. They were on the cradle release, the white lever, both of them, and the lever had not been pulled. At his throat, a bead. And on the inside of the canopy, above his head, where he would have seen it every time he looked up, someone had painted, carefully, in the same yellow as the lamp on the nose, a name.

TOBIN.

Isra stood on the hull of the skiff for a long time. She did not put a rag over his face. She found she could not.

*I don't want to. I'm sorry. I don't want to.*

*And Rusk said, Fire.*

*I was on the second gun.*

She went back to the Gristle and sat in the pilot's seat with her helmet on her lap, and then she did a thing she had never done in ten years of picking bones. She did not cut. She took the skiff in tow, whole, glass and all, the boy in his seat with his hands on the lever he had not pulled, and turned the Gristle not toward the Lantern cutter at the edge of the drift, but toward the Anchor.

* * *

She brought him in to Dock Four and tied him up next to the Lamplighter's black ore-hauler, and climbed out, and stood on the dock beside him, and waited.

The black coats on the dock looked at the skiff, and at the lamp on its nose, and at her, and nobody stopped her. Somebody ran. Ten minutes later the Lamplighter came.

She came alone, at first. Across the huge dark hangar from the lift, in her black coat, fast, faster than Isra had ever seen her move, and then slower, and slower, as she came close enough to see the skiff, and the lamp, and the name through the thin milky glass of the canopy; and then she stopped.

Isra did not say anything.

Mercy Oduya stood on the dock and looked at her son.

After a while she went to him. She climbed the skiff's side the way you would climb a wall in a dream, slowly, not quite there, and knelt on its hull beside the canopy and put her two bare cracked hands flat on the glass over his face. She did not make a sound. She knelt there with her hands on the glass for a long time, while the black coats gathered at the edges of the hangar and did not come closer, and Isra stood on the dock below with her helmet under her arm.

Then the Lamplighter's hands moved on the glass, and stopped, and she leaned forward and looked down along the hull toward the stern. At the engine housing. At three neat round holes in a row, with the metal curled inward like petals.

She looked at them.

"He turned round," said Isra.

The Lamplighter did not move.

"At the Fifty-Ninth. At the mouth. At the start of the Long Night. He turned round. He pulled up and turned and came back out toward the escort." Isra's voice was steady; she did not know how. "And the Deacon told him to go back. And he didn't. He said something, on the open channel. Everyone on the escort heard it. He said, *I don't want to. I'm sorry. I don't want to.*" She swallowed. "And the Deacon said *fire*. And they fired. The *Patience*'s forward guns. And his engine went, and he drifted into the mouth with the pull. And the Deacon said, *Well, he's a Keeper now.*"

The Lamplighter turned her head and looked down at Isra from the skiff's hull.

"Who told you that?"

"Somebody who was on the second gun."

Deacon Rusk had come across the hangar while Isra was speaking. He stood at the foot of the skiff now, a few metres from her, with his hands clasped behind his back, and his pleasant face had gone very still.

"Mercy," he said.

She looked at him.

"He'd been blessed," said the Deacon. "He'd been sung off from the steps. He was carrying a cradle of glass the Needle needed and he was turning *back* with it, in front of six other Keepers, in front of the whole escort, on the open channel. If he'd come home — if they'd seen him come home — " He spread his hands. "You'd have had nobody at the Sixtieth. Nobody. You know you would. You'd have wanted me to. You *would* have, Mercy. You'd have given the order yourself."

The Lamplighter looked at him for a long, long time.

"Yes," she said at last. Very quietly. "I expect I would."

The Deacon let out a breath.

"Get out," said Mercy Oduya.

He stared at her.

"Get off this station. Get on the *Patience* and go back to Tallow and do not come back to me. Do not come to the steps on the night. Do not come to the Lantern House. Go anywhere you like. Go." She did not raise her voice. It did not need raising. "If I see you again, Ezekiel Rusk, I will do what you did. And I will be right to. And I will carry it." She looked at him. "Go."

He went. He walked away across the hangar toward the *Patience* with his hands clasped behind his back, at his ordinary pace, and the black coats at the edges of the hangar moved aside for him, and nobody spoke; and only Isra, watching, saw his right hand come out from behind his back as he went and touch, very briefly, the breast of his coat, where something small and heavy was.

The Lamplighter knelt on her son's hull with her hands on the glass.

"Go away, girl," she said, not looking down.

Isra did not go away. "It's choking," she said.

The Lamplighter's hands went still.

"The Needle. It's not starving. It never was. Lucan tuned the harp to the Fork and saw it. There's a thing in the throat — a mass, glass, as big as a station — at the waist. We counted it. It's in layers. One for every Keeping. Fifty-nine. The two thick ones on top are yours. The Fifty-Eighth and the Fifty-Ninth." Isra's voice cracked at last. "He's in it. Tobin. His glass is in it. That's where it went. It didn't feed anything. It's still there. It's what's killing the door." She took a breath. "And you're going to give it a hundred more."

The Lamplighter turned her head and looked down at her. Her face was wet and still.

"Get out of my sight," she said.

Isra went. At the lift she looked back. The Lamplighter was still kneeling on the hull of the little black skiff in the grey light of the hangar, with her forehead against the canopy now, against the glass, over her son's face, and her shoulders were not moving at all.

* * *

The Prior found her in the greenhouse at two in the morning, among the beans, sitting on the floor with Hanne's cittern across her knees, not playing it.

She had heard about Lucan by then. Everyone had. The Lamplighter's boys had stopped being afraid of saying things, the day the Deacon left; they had begun to be afraid of other things instead. She had heard that the Warden reader was in the penitents' cells under the docks, and that the Prior had given him up, and that the Deacon had taken the First Warden's Fork from him in the harp hall and put it in his own coat. She had sat on the greenhouse floor since midnight working out how to get onto the *Patience*, which had left for Tallow at six, and how to find the Deacon in Tallow, and what she would do when she did, and she had not got very far.

The old man stood in the doorway on his two sticks, breathing hard from the walk.

"He didn't take it," said Prior Saar.

She looked up.

"Rusk." The old man came in, slowly, and lowered himself onto an upturned seed-tray, which took some time. "He didn't take it to Tallow. He meant to. He took it from Lucan and put it in his coat. And this afternoon, when she sent him off the station, he went to his cabin to pack, and he took it out of his coat, and he stood there looking at it, and then he went down to the harp hall and put it back on its cushion and went." Saar's voice was dry. "One of the novices saw him. He didn't say anything. He just put it back."

"Why?"

"I have no idea," said the Prior. "His mother sang flat." He reached into the breast of his robe and took out something small and heavy and held it out to her across the beans, on his old shaking palm. "I went and got it. An hour ago. Before anyone thought to."

It was the Fork.

Isra did not take it. She looked at it lying in his palm, and then at his face.

"You gave him to them," she said.

"Yes."

"Lucan. You gave him to the Lantern. For twelve."

"For eleven," said the Prior. "Yes. I did." He did not look away. "I've given a great many things to a great many people, girl, to keep eleven old men breathing in a station built for six thousand. I've been doing it for thirty years. I gave her my grey on her steps. I gave her the readings. I gave her Lucan." He held out the Fork. "I find I can't give her this as well. I've been sitting in my cell since the boy came in on your tow-line trying to, and I can't."

She took it. It was cold. It was heavier than it looked, as Lucan had said.

"He's in the fourth cell," said the Prior. "The lay-brothers who guard it are mine. They're asleep. They're old men; they sleep very deeply." He leaned on his sticks and got up off the seed-tray, slowly. "Go and see him. He'll want to tell you about the Bell. He's been telling the wall about it all day."

"And you?" said Isra. "On the night? Will you be at the harp?"

The old man stood among the beans in the green light, eighty-one, on two sticks, in a frayed robe.

"I don't know," said Prior Saar. "I've never in my life known what I would do until I'd done it. That's the trouble with being old. You think you'll have found out." He turned toward the door. "Go and see him, girl."

The penitents' cells were at the bottom of the spindle under the docks, six of them and a guardroom, very old, built of a grey steel that had been painted many times. The two lay-brothers in the guardroom were asleep in their chairs with their mouths open. The fourth cell had a small barred grille in its door at the height of a face.

Lucan's face was in it before she had finished saying his name.

"You've got it," he said. He was looking at her hand. "He gave it to you. The Prior. He — "

"Rusk put it back. The Prior took it. I've got it." She held it up to the grille. "Tell me what to do."

He told her. He talked very fast, with his face pressed to the bars, his spectacles askew, and she listened and did not interrupt, the way she had listened to Hanne. The Bell's end-plate: seat the Fork stem-first in the socket, all the way down, until it locked — he thought it would lock, there would be a catch. The power: the Gristle's main bus, a cable from her external port to the Bell's frame; the Bell would have a port somewhere, it had been built to be serviced, he had seen something like a hatch on its flank through the telescope. The lattice at the tail had to face the waist. If it had drifted, she would have to turn it — use the grapple, gently, Pike's way. And then wait. For the harp. For the Long Night. For someone to close the switch.

"If nobody closes it," said Isra.

"Then the Bell's just a buoy with a fork in it," said Lucan. "And you'll be sitting at the mouth in the shear in the middle of the Great Keeping with a hundred skiffs going past you into the throat." He pressed his forehead against the bars. "Isra. I don't know if anyone will close it."

"Then I'll close it," she said.

"You can't. You'll be at the Bell. You can't be in two places — "

"I'll ring it," said Isra Venn. "Somehow. From the Bell. From the Gristle. I don't know how. I've got the Fork. I've got the shard. I've got Gran's low string." She held up the cittern, which she had brought down with her without thinking, the way you bring a thing you cannot put down. "You said it yourself. It's all one note. The salt and the throat and the Fork and the string. It's all one note, Lucan. Somebody's going to play it on the night. If it's not the harp, it'll be me."

He looked at her through the bars.

"I'll be on her ship," he said. "At the head of the line. Holding them at the markers as long as I can. Telling her the shear's too heavy. Lying to her." He swallowed. "She trusts me."

"I know."

"She'll know. When it starts. She'll know it was me."

"I know."

"Pike's on the list," he said. "Number forty. I heard the boys reading it out."

"I know that too."

They looked at each other through the little barred grille in the old grey door, with the lay-brothers snoring in the guardroom behind her, eight days before the Sixtieth Narrowing. Then Lucan put his hand through the bars, as far as it would go, and she took it, and held it, with the Fork in her other hand and the cittern against her hip, until the old men in the guardroom began to stir.

# Chapter 19: The Long Night

*The Concession · 2291*

They let her see him on the last night, because Hesper Vane had said they should.

The Concession's cells were at the bottom of Anchorage's spindle, under the docks, six of them and a guardroom, built of a grey steel that had been painted once and never again. They had been made for drunks and for men who hit overseers; they smelled of disinfectant and old fear. Nico was in the fourth. He was sitting on the bunk with his back against the wall and a chain from his ankle to a ring in the floor, and when the guard let her in he looked up and saw her flight coveralls and went white.

"No," he said.

"Hello, Nic."

"No. Teo. No. They told me. The guard told me, this morning, I said he was lying, I said she'd never — " He was on his feet, as far as the chain would let him. "For *me*? You're flying into that thing for *me*?"

"Sit down."

"I'm not — "

"Sit *down*, Nico." She said it the way their mother used to, and he sat, because he always had, and she sat down on the bunk beside him, close, with her shoulder against his, the way they had sat on the shaft-school steps when he was six and she was fourteen and their father had just been brought up from the Lower Nine under a sheet.

The guard was in the doorway. He was listening. She did not look at him.

"The debts are struck," she said. "Both of ours. She did it in front of me. I watched the numbers go." She found she could almost smile. "Forty thousand seven hundred and twelve marks, Nic. Gone. Like they were never there. And yours. You're free. You walk out of here on the morning of the second whatever happens. She gave her word. She keeps her word, that woman. That's the worst thing about her."

"I don't care about the debts." His voice cracked. "I don't — Teo, you heard him. In the canteen. The harp man. It doesn't *need* it. It's *breathing*. You heard it breathe, you were there — it's going to open on the second whether anybody goes in or not, it doesn't need you, it doesn't need anyone — "

"I know."

"Then *why* — "

"Because she needs me," said Teo quietly. "And because you're in a cell." She felt him go still against her shoulder. "I know what I heard, Nic. I'm not going in to feed anything. I want you to know that. Whatever they say afterwards. Whatever she says. I'm not going in to feed it."

He turned his head and looked at her. She looked back. She did not say anything else, because the guard was in the doorway; but she held his eyes, and after a long moment she saw something come into them, slowly — not understanding, quite; the beginning of it.

"Teo," he said, very low. "What are you going to do?"

"Turn into the wave," she said. "Like I always do."

She put her hand in the pocket of her coveralls and took out the bead the Director had given her — the Keeper's bead, cloudy glass on a black cord — and held it out to him.

"She gave me this. For the Keeping. So I'd be *part of it*." She put it in his palm and closed his fingers over it. "You keep it. I don't want it. I've got yours." She touched her throat, where his bead lay, the one he had cut from his first seam at sixteen. "I've always got yours."

He held the Director's bead in his fist and looked at it, and then at her, and his face crumpled, and she put her arms round him on the bunk with the chain on his ankle and the guard in the door and held him, the way she had held him on the shaft-school steps, until the guard cleared his throat and said it was time.

"Remember the breath," she said into his hair, very quietly, too quietly for the doorway. "Remember Ambrose. Remember what he played you. Whatever she says, afterwards. Whatever they sing. *Remember.*"

"I will," said Nico.

"Promise."

"I promise," said her brother. "Teo. I promise."

* * *

There were nine tugs on Dock Four at four in the morning on the first of May, each with a cradle welded under its belly, each cradle loaded with two tonnes of the best glass the shafts of Tallow had ever cut, and there were nine pilots standing in front of them in a line, in their flight coveralls, with their helmets under their arms and the Director's beads at their throats.

Teo knew them all. You did, on the lanes. Haldane, who had flown the Pans run for twenty years and had six children and a seventh coming. The Moss brothers, both of them, who had signed together, because neither would let the other go alone. Ida Okonjo, whose cousin had died under a sheet at the shaft-head six days ago. Jory Ferris, nineteen, who had got her licence in January. Big Sven, who could not read. Marguerite, who had flown a tug called the *Ardent Rose* for thirty-one years and said she was tired of looking at the door from outside. And Corrigan's empty place, which nobody stood in, and then Teo.

Nine. Eight debts, and Nico.

The Concession had sent its chaplain to bless them. He stood at the end of the line in his white with the sunburst on it, a thin nervous man from the Inward whom nobody had ever seen before the trial, holding a book; and before he could open it Mother Salome came across the dock from the lift, tall and stooped, with the Tally under her arm in its blanket and Lulu Dace beside her holding her hand, and walked straight past him as if he were not there, and stopped in front of the pilots.

"I'll do this," she said. To the chaplain; not looking at him. "They're ours."

He did not argue. Nobody ever argued with Mother Salome.

She did not have a book. She did not say anything about the Needle, or hunger, or doors. She went down the line of pilots, one by one, and stopped in front of each of them, and put her hand on their face — just her hand, flat, on their cheek, the way you would touch a child with a fever — and said their name. That was all. *Haldane.* *Tomas Moss.* *Ewan Moss.* *Ida.* *Jory.* *Sven.* *Marguerite.* She came to Teo last.

"Teodora," she said.

Her hand was dry and warm and smelled of salt. Teo closed her eyes.

"I know what you're going to do," said Mother Salome, very low, so that only Teo could hear. "Your brother told me. Not in words. He doesn't know it in words. But I know your face. I've known it since you were born." Her hand did not move. "Turn into the wave, girl."

Teo opened her eyes.

Lulu was standing beside Salome, holding her other hand, looking up at Teo with her father's eyes. She had her bag of bolts over her shoulder. She took one out — a bright steel bolt, a cradle bolt, the kind they had used to weld the Brannock's frame — and held it up.

"For luck," she said.

Teo took it. She put it in the breast pocket of her coveralls, over her heart, and buttoned the pocket shut.

They went to their tugs. Teo climbed the Small Mercy's strut into the cockpit, which smelled of coolant and oranges and forty years of other pilots, and of her, and sat down in the pilot's seat and put her helmet on the console and her hand, for a moment, flat on the casing of the flight recorder under the console, where the spool was. Sealed in. Waiting. The flight recorder was the one part of a tug that was built to survive anything; it had its own power, its own transmitter, its own hull. If the Mercy came apart, it would not.

She had been through it all a hundred times, lying awake in her bunk-cube for three nights. It was very simple. The nine would go in at the hour the readers called. They would fly the throat to the waist. At the waist, the eight would pull their white levers and release their glass and turn round and come home, if they could. She would not pull hers. She would not add her two tonnes to Corrigan's scar or to the eight others' on top of it, ringing at the waist forever. She would go on. Through the iris. Past the waist. Down the far throat, through the Narrowing, while it was at its narrowest, nine hours to Ardent in an ordinary week and nobody knew how many in this one; and if she came out at the other end, at Port Ardent, she would open the flight recorder's transmitter on every frequency the Inward had and play them the breath, and the sum, and nine pages in numbered paragraphs ending *Approved by the Board. Proceed.* To everyone. The courts and the newspapers and the inspectors and every ordinary person in Ardent with a receiver in their kitchen. And then let the Board explain.

And if she did not come out —

Then the flight recorder would keep it. Wherever she ended up. For whoever came.

Nobody had ever flown the throat in a Narrowing. Nobody had ever been past the waist and come back to say what was there. Corrigan had gone to the waist and said there was a light in the middle with no name.

She put her hand on Nico's bead at her throat. It was not leaning to the left any more. It was standing out from her skin on its cord, straight out, taut, toward the mouth, like a dog that has seen its master.

"Mercy, Lectern," said a voice in her ear. Not Ambrose. Bettany Shore, very young, very frightened, trying to sound like him. "Keepers, Lectern. Stand by. The Director will speak."

* * *

Hesper Vane spoke for ninety seconds. Teo did not hear much of it. She heard *door* and *covenant* and *the first Keeping* and *you will be remembered*. She heard the Director say that at the moment the Keepers entered the throat, an instrument would be struck in the Lectern — *the instrument by which the Narrowing was first heard* — so that all the Reach would know the hour. She thought of a brass fork turning in a slight woman's fingers on a blood-red rug. Then the Director said *Go*, and Bettany Shore's frightened voice said *Keepers, Lectern, you are clear for the mouth, the waist is at twenty-one point four and steady, God help you*, and Teo fed the Small Mercy's mains and took her off the dock.

They went in a line. Haldane first, because he had flown the longest; then the Moss brothers, side by side; then the others; and the Mercy last, because she had asked to be last. Out past the beacons, which were all amber now, every one; out past the outer markers; into the shear.

She did not remember the shear afterward, much. It was the worst it had ever been and she did not remember it. She remembered Bettany's voice, calling the waves, not as well as Ambrose had, late every time, so that the pilots had to read them themselves; and she remembered reading them, turning into them before Bettany finished speaking, the way she had at the inner markers with an old man in a cardigan behind her saying *Good. That was very good.* She remembered the Mercy screaming. She remembered the inner markers going past on either side, the two dead beacons, the place where she had held for three hours; and then she was past them, for the first time in her life, and there was nothing in front of her but the mouth.

Somewhere far behind her, two million kilometres behind, in the Lectern, somebody struck a fork.

She heard it. She would swear afterward that she heard it — that it came to her through the channel, thin and pure, laid across the roar of the shear like a ruler. Ambrose's note. Clare's.
