And the bead at her throat, which had been straining toward the mouth on its cord, gave a single sharp *tick* against her skin.

Then the throat took her.

It was folded light. Corrigan had been right. Light folded on itself like the inside of a shell, grey-gold and white and a colour that was neither, going down and down and in, and the Mercy going down into it, and the light going past on every side so close she could have put out her hand. There was no shear in here. There was no sound but the Mercy's engines and her own breath. The other tugs were ahead of her in a line, small, stretched, pulled long, like beads on a string, and the light went past them too.

She reached down under the console with one hand, without looking, and found the flight recorder's switch, and turned it to *record*.

"This is Teodora Kesh," she said. Her voice sounded strange to her in the helmet, very close. "Pilot. Small Mercy. Out of Dock Four, Anchorage. First of May, twenty-two ninety-one. I'm in the throat. I'm not a Keeper. I want that on the record before anything else. My name's on a list of Keepers and I'm not one. I've got two tonnes of glass in my cradle and I'm not going to release it." She took a breath. "There's a spool in this recorder. It's not mine. It's Ambrose Lyle's. He was the Senior Throat-Reader at the Lectern for twenty-three years and on the twenty-fourth of April he was murdered for what's on it — "

The waist was coming.

She could see it ahead. The folded light drawing in, and in, all of it, the whole throat, toward a single point far ahead of her where it tightened like an iris, like an eye squinting against pain; and at the centre of the iris, very small, the light. Corrigan's light. The one with no name. It was not white. It was every colour and none. It was the most beautiful thing she had ever seen.

And around the light, at the iris's edge, there was something else. Small and hard and bright, clinging to the folded wall like frost to a pane. She could hear it. Even in the helmet, over the engines. A thin high ringing.

*Something stayed.*

Ahead of her Haldane's tug reached the waist and slowed and hung there at the edge of the iris, small against the light. She saw his cradle open. She saw the glass go — a dull blue spill, tumbling out of the cradle into the light — and the light flare, white, then sickly blue, then dark; and the ringing at the iris's edge grow louder, a new note joining the old. Then the next tug. Then the Moss brothers together. One after another, at the waist, the cradles opening, the glass spilling, the flare, the ringing, louder each time, and the tugs turning, slowly, laboriously, in the narrow folded light, and starting back up the throat toward her, past her, going home.

Marguerite went by her on the way out so close that Teo could see her face through two canopies. She was crying and laughing at the same time. She raised one hand.

Teo raised hers back.

Then there was nobody ahead of her but the iris, and the light.

"I'm at the waist," she said into the recorder. Her voice was quite steady; she noticed that. "The others have released and turned. I'm not releasing. I'm going on. Through the waist. To the Inward. If I come out at Ardent I'll broadcast this on every channel they've got. If I don't — " She stopped. "If I don't, whoever finds this, listen to it. All of it. The breath and the sum and the paper. And tell them. Tell everyone. Tell them it doesn't need us. Tell them his name. Ambrose Lyle. Tell them Corrigan Dace's name, and say he was a pilot, not a Keeper. Tell them Nico Kesh didn't kill anybody." She swallowed. "And tell Nico — "

She did not finish. She did not know what to tell Nico. She found that she was smiling.

"Turning into the wave," she said, and fed the mains everything they had, and a little more, and the Small Mercy went forward into the iris.

The light was very close now. It filled the canopy. It filled the cockpit. It came in through the delta crack in the forward canopy where her mother had once taken a rock, forty years ago, and lay across Teo's hands on the yoke, every colour and none. The ringing was all round her, and under it something deeper, slower, vast and patient, rising and falling, like someone sleeping in the next room.

The bead at her throat stood out on its cord, straight out, toward the light.

"Oh," said Teo Kesh, to the recorder, very softly, the way Ambrose had said it at the inner markers with the headphones on. "Oh. It's beautiful. I didn't know."

And the iris closed around her, and the light went in.

* * *

The waist turned at fourteen minutes past six in the morning on the second of May, at twenty-one point one per cent of nominal, eleven minutes later than Ambrose Lyle had said it would on a sheet of paper twenty days before.

Bettany Shore watched it turn. She had been at the harp for twenty-six hours. She had called the line for nine tugs into the throat and eight out of it, the last of them limping back up through the shear at eleven at night with Marguerite at the controls weeping on the open channel. She had called the Long Night after that — the throat shut, the waist at twenty-one, the harp singing its thin high strained chord into the dark of the dome hour after hour with no change at all — and she had not slept, and she had not left the console, and at a little after five in the morning she had put on Ambrose's old cracked headphones, which were still on the hook beside his chair, and listened to the buoy.

So she heard it before the trace showed it. The long slow breath, under the throat. Turning. And then the short breath turning on top of it, at fourteen past six, and the trace on the console coming up off the bottom, twenty-one point two, twenty-one point four — and she sat in his chair with his headphones on and cried, because it was exactly as he had said, and he was not there to see it.

The Director came down to the Lectern at seven.

Bettany had not heard her come in. She looked up and Hesper Vane was standing beside the console, looking at the trace — twenty-three now, and rising — with her hands folded in front of her. She looked as she always looked. She did not look as though she had been up all night, though Bettany knew she had; she had been on the gallery above the dome, at the window, from the moment the Keepers went in.

"The *Small Mercy*," said the Director.

"She didn't come out." Bettany's voice was hoarse. "The other eight released and turned. Marguerite said — Marguerite said on the channel that the Mercy didn't release. That she went *on*. Past the waist. Into the — " She stopped. "Into the light, Marguerite said. And then the throat shut."

Hesper Vane was quiet for a moment.

"Did the *Mercy* transmit?" she said. "On the way in. At the waist. Anything at all."

"Nothing reached the Lectern after the mouth. Nothing ever does, in the throat. Only the buoy, and the buoy only hears — " Bettany stopped again. "No. Nothing."

"Good," said Hesper Vane, very quietly.

She stood looking at the rising trace. Then she went to the harp.

She walked round it once, slowly, the way she had walked across rooms in all the years Bettany had worked under her, as if she were looking at a painting. The frame, gone faintly gold in the dome's light. The nine courses of wire. Ambrose's console, with his chair pushed back and his headphones on the hook. She stopped in front of the frame and reached into the pocket of her jacket and took out something small and brass, and held it in her palm, looking at it. Bettany knew what it was. Everyone in the Lectern knew what it was. It had been struck at four in the morning on the first of May, from the gallery above the dome, by the Director's own hand, at the moment the Keepers went into the mouth — once, against the gallery rail, so that the note rang down through the dome into the harp and out over every channel in the Reach.

"This was Mr Lyle's," said Hesper Vane.

"I know."

"Everything here was tuned to it. He told me so, once. He said if he lost it, nothing would agree with anything." She turned it over. "Strike it for me, Miss Shore."

Bettany looked at her.

"Strike it. Against your hand. Here, in front of the harp."

Bettany took it. Her hands were shaking. She struck it against the heel of her palm, the way she had seen Ambrose do a thousand times, and the note went up into the dome, thin and pure, and met the harp's chord and lay down across it like a ruler; and under the chord, faint, so faint that you would not hear it unless you knew it was there, the long slow breath of the Needle, turning, coming back.

"You can hear it," said Hesper Vane. "Can't you. When it's tuned to that."

"Yes," whispered Bettany.

"Everyone could, with practice. That's what he found. Anyone at all, with ears." The Director held out her hand, and Bettany gave her the fork back, and she closed her fingers over it. "Henceforth, Miss Shore, the harp will not be tuned to this. It will be tuned to a note I will give you, this morning, and teach you, and you will teach to every reader who comes after you. A slightly different note. You will tune to it every morning and not otherwise, and the harp will hear the waist close and the waist open, as well as it ever did, and no one will ever hear that again." She nodded at the faint breath under the chord. "I will call it the Hunger Tone. I think that's a good name for it. Don't you?"

Bettany could not speak.

"And the old records," the Director went on, in the same level voice. "Every trace in the archive before today. Mr Lyle's paper rolls. His notes. The Concession's harp logs back to forty-seven. You'll box them, today, and Marshal Brandt's men will bring them up to the Gallery, and they will be in my keeping. And you'll begin a new daybook, this morning, with today's date at the top." She looked at the console. "Your readings from today onward are the first readings of the new order. The Concession ends today, Miss Shore. The *Providence* has sailed. There's nobody left in the Reach who works for the Ostrander Concern but me, and as of this morning I don't either." She paused. "I'm told the shafts are already calling this the First Keeping. The Keepers, the First Keepers. They'll want someone to keep it. Every four thousand days. Someone who knows how the door is kept. Someone who can read it." The grey eyes rested on Bettany for a moment. "The Wardenship of the Throat, I thought. A Warden keeps a door. I'll be the first."

She turned the fork over in her fingers once more, and Bettany saw the engraving on its stem catch the dome's grey light, the tidy capitals, worn a little bright at the edges where his thumb had gone for twenty-three years: A. L. — 440 — FOR CLARE.

"We'll keep this," said the First Warden. "In the Lectern. On a cushion, I think. The instrument by which the hunger was first heard." She closed her hand over it. "We'll strike it at every Keeping, once, at the hour, so the Reach knows. And otherwise nobody will touch it." Her mouth moved. "For clarity."

She went out. Bettany heard her go up the spindle, unhurried, toward the Gallery, and the door slide shut behind her.

Bettany sat at the console for a long time. The trace climbed. Twenty-four. Twenty-five. Twenty-six. The door was opening, all by itself, exactly as he had said, the long breath carrying the short; and in the Lectern's own log, on the console in front of her, there was nothing but the number. *Waist twenty-six. Holding.* After a while she opened the new daybook the Director had left on the console, black-bound, and found that it was not empty. At the head of the first page, above the date, in a small upright hand that was not hers, the First Warden had already written the instruction:

*Henceforth the harp is to be tuned each morning to the H.T. and not otherwise. By order of the F.W.*

Bettany sat and looked at it. Then, because she had been told to, and because she was twenty-two and frightened and alone in the dome with a dead man's headphones round her neck, she took up a pen and wrote under it, in her own round hand, the first reading of the new order: *Waist twenty-six. Holding.*

* * *

They let Nico out of the cells at eight in the morning on the second of May, as the Director had promised, and he walked out of the spindle into the docks and down to the Pans shuttle and out onto the salt flats of Hollin under a sky where, for the first time in two months, the Needle's rim was loosening.

You could see it from the Pans. You could see it if you knew where to look, and everyone on the Pans that morning knew: a faint smear of wrong stars low over the salt, a bruise with a rim of grey-gold fire round it. The fire was less tight than yesterday. Everyone said so. People were standing on the salt in their hundreds in the cold, in the thin bitter wind off the flats, looking up at it, pointing, crying. Somebody had a horn and was blowing it. Somebody was singing — a tune Nico did not know, low and droning, with words about little ships and glass. People had their hands on each other's shoulders. People were embracing strangers.

*It worked,* they were saying, all round him, all over the Pans. *It worked. They went in, and it opened. The Keeping worked.*

He walked through them with his hands in his pockets and his head down.

Mother Salome was at the shaft-head, on the cage-head step where the three sheets had been. The fence was still down. Nobody had cut glass for a week. She had the Tally on her knees, unwrapped, and Lulu Dace sitting beside her, and when Nico came up the access road she looked up and did not say anything, and moved over on the step to make room.

He sat down.

"Marguerite told me," said Salome after a while. "She came straight from the dock. She said the Mercy went on. Past the waist. Into the light. She didn't release." She looked down at the steel on her knees. "She said she was smiling."

Nico did not answer. He was holding something in his fist, in his pocket. He took it out. The Director's bead, the Keeper's bead, cloudy glass on a black cord. He looked at it.

"They're singing," he said. "On the Pans. They're singing about it. *They loaded glass in little ships.*" He laughed, a terrible sound. "*It worked.* That's what they're saying. All of them. It opened, so it worked. She'll be on the screens by noon telling them so. And they'll believe her. Because it opened." He closed his fist on the bead. "And nobody will ever know. That she didn't release. That she was trying to — "

"You know," said Mother Salome.

He looked at her.

"You know," she said. "I know. Marguerite knows. The child knows." She put her hand on Lulu's head. "That's four. It's not nothing. It's how everything starts, Nico. Somebody knowing, and saying it to somebody else." She held out the stylus. "Go on."

He took it. She tilted the Tally on her knees toward him, and he looked down at the old steel: the thirty-one at the top in her careful capitals, and the rows below in their dozen hands, and at the bottom the newest, in a nine-year-old's wavering letters, C. DACE — PILOT, and under it, in Teo's own hand, scratched a week ago on this step, A. LYLE — HARP.

He scratched it under those. His hand was steady. T. KESH.

He stopped there, with the stylus on the steel.

"Keeper," said Mother Salome softly. "That's what they'll call her. That's how they'll have it. Her name will be on a list of Keepers in the Director's book, and they'll sing it, and it'll be true for them."

"She wasn't one."

"I know."

"She said so. To me. In the cell. *I'm not going in to feed it.* She said to remember." He looked at the bead in his other fist, and then at the plate. "I promised."

He put the stylus back to the steel and scratched, under her name, carefully, the same word Lulu had scratched for Corrigan Dace: PILOT.

Then he sat on the cage-head step between the old woman and the child with the Tally across all three of their knees, in the bitter wind, with the shaft silent behind them and the Pans singing below, and looked up at the Needle opening over the salt, all by itself, exactly as an old man in a cardigan had said it would; and he held his sister's name under his fingers, the letters of it, cold and shallow and new, and he said it, once, aloud, so that somebody else would have heard it.

"Teo."

# Chapter 20: The Thirtieth Keeping

*The Severance · 2608*

They came out of the shear at three in the morning on the fifth of December, the *Vigil* and nineteen ships of the Free League, riding the waves down off the rim of the mouth the way the raiders had a month before, with a League reader in the *Callum* calling the line on the open band so that every ship could hear it.

Juno had never flown the shear. She flew it now. She stood on the *Vigil*'s bridge with her hands on the back of the helm's chair, and Linnet at the helm turned into each wave as the reader called it, and the corvette shuddered and bucked and came down off the fire into the grey-gold dark with the Anchorhold ahead of her and nineteen Shoals ships strung out behind; and Juno found that she was thinking, absurdly, of Pell at eight, in the harp hall, standing in front of the harp for an hour until they had to carry her out.

"Home Squadron," said the tactical rating who had Marsh's chair. His voice was steady. "Bearing one-eight-zero, coming up from Tallow. The *Constant*, the *Resolute*, the *Ardour*, six frigates. Admiral's flag on the *Constant*."

"They knew we'd come," said Juno.

"Everyone knew we'd come, Captain."

On the main display the Anchorhold hung against the mouth, its broken wheel still slewed against its spindle where the dead wheel had struck it, its docks lit. And beyond it, at the edge of the shear, at the mouth itself, a line of small lights. Nine. The Keepers' skiffs, in their cradles on the Keeping barge, waiting for the hour.

The command channel opened without her touching it. Greaves.

"*Asante.*" His square grey face, very tired. "*Asante, I'm going to say this once. You are a mutineer in time of war in company with the enemy. The Keeping flies in four hours. If you or any ship with you comes within ten kilometres of the Keeping barge I will fire on you. I don't want to. I've known you since you were a cadet. But I will.*"

"Understood, Admiral," said Juno. "We're not going near the barge."

He frowned. "*Then what the hell are you doing here?*"

"Holding a door," said Juno, and closed the channel.

The plan was very simple, the way the plans you make with nothing are always simple. The League's nineteen would come in between the Anchorhold and the Home Squadron and hold there, and not fire unless fired on, for as long as it took. The *Vigil* would dock at the Anchorhold — at Dock Four, which was still torn open where she had left it, and which nobody would be guarding, because nobody would think anyone would try to dock there — and put Adaeze aboard with Hollis and six volunteers. Adaeze would go up the spindle to the harp hall. And at the hour, when the Keepers went in and the whole Reach was listening to the harp on every channel, she would tune it true, and read.

"Thirty minutes," Juno had said to her, in the wardroom, the night before. "That's what Rhee thinks he can hold. Maybe less. Thirty minutes from when you start."

"It'll take me twenty to tune it," Adaeze had said. "If my hands hold." She had looked down at them, still bandaged. "I'll read while I tune. I'll start reading as soon as I'm in the hall. They'll hear it go true while I read. That's better, I think. They'll hear the difference." She had put her hand on the book, which had not left her for seven days. "And the seal. I'll break the seal at the end. On the air."

"You haven't read it."

"No." Adaeze had looked at the grey disc of wax. "I want to read it once. With everyone."

The Home Squadron came on. The League's nineteen spread out across its path in a long thin line, between it and the station, and stopped, and hung there. Juno watched it on the display. She saw the *Callum* at the line's centre, her forward guns still dark from the raid, her running lights lit. She saw the squadron's frigates slow. She saw, for a long held minute, nobody fire.

"*Juno*," said Rhee's voice on the cadet band. Very calm. "*I've got them. For now. Go.*"

"Linnet," she said. "Dock Four."

* * *

The harp hall was full when Adaeze came into it, and singing.

She had forgotten that it would be. She had thought of it, all the way up the spindle with Hollis and the six beside her, as she had always known it at night: empty, cold, the harp alone under the dome. But it was the Long Night. Every Warden in the Anchorhold was here, a hundred of them, in their grey, in rows on the worn floor before the harp, and the novices along the walls, and the readers at their consoles, and they were singing the Hunger Tone, all of them, held and held, so that the whole dome hummed with it; and over their heads the mouth hung huge and dark with its rim of fire wound tight, and in the middle of the floor, before the harp, stood the High Warden.

The broadcast lamp above the reader's console was lit. Red. Every channel in the Reach.

Nobody stopped her. That was the thing she would have remembered, if there had been time to remember anything. She came through the iron door in her grey with her bandaged hands and the black book against her chest, and the singing faltered, near the door, and then all the way along the rows, voice by voice, until it died; and a hundred Wardens turned and looked at her, the archivist, who had been taken for heresy, who everyone said had stolen the Inheritance and fled to the Shoals; and nobody moved.

Except the High Warden's guard-captain, who put his hand on his sidearm.

"No," said Cassius Mor.

He did not raise his voice. He did not need to. The guard-captain took his hand away.

Mor stood in the middle of the floor in his grey with the silver at the cuffs, very straight, and looked at her across the hall. He looked, she thought, as if he had not slept since the night in the gallery. He looked a hundred years old.

"Sister," he said.

"High Warden."

"You've come to read it."

"Yes."

He looked at the book against her chest. Then at the broadcast lamp, red, over the console. Then up through the dome at the mouth. For a long moment he did not say anything at all, and the hall was so silent that she could hear the harp's drone, the false one, the Hunger Tone, going out over every channel in the Reach into every house.

"She wrote that the alternative was fire," said Cassius Mor. "I've believed that for twenty-five years. I've chosen a child in every Draw because I believed it." He looked at her. "I don't know if I believe it any more. I haven't known since the boy in my doorway." He stepped aside. "Read it, Adaeze. Let them choose."

She went past him to the harp.

She took the Fork out of her robe. A sound went through the hall — a hundred people drawing breath — and she did not look round. She struck it against her bandaged palm. It hurt. The note went up into the dome, thin and pure, and met the Hunger Tone, and fought it. *Wah. Wah. Wah.* Over every channel in the Reach.

She knelt at the base of the frame and began to tune.

And she began to read.

She did not remember, afterward, the order she did it in — there was no afterward, as it turned out, or not much of one — but the people who heard it remembered, for the rest of their lives. They remembered the archivist's voice, low, steady, a little hoarse, reading from a book into the open broadcast in the order's old formal cadence, *To my successor*; and under the voice, the harp, changing. Course by course. Peg by peg. The long false drone of the Hunger Tone, which every one of them had heard every four thousand days of their lives and sung in the Hall since they could stand, beginning to shift, and slide, and fight itself, and then — wire by wire, as she read — to lie down into something else. Something wider. Something that breathed.

*The Narrowing is a natural event. The Needle breathes.*

In Tallow Under, in the light-wells, in a thousand houses with the Long Night on, people stopped singing.

*I instituted the Keeping not to keep the door but to keep the Reach.*

In the Shoals, in the habs, in the ice-farms on Corrie Rock, people turned their receivers up.

*I have ordered the harp tuned to a note of my own choosing, which I have called the Hunger Tone, and which is so chosen that a harp tuned to it can hear the waist narrow and the waist widen, but cannot hear why.*

On the bridge of the *Constant*, at the head of the Home Squadron, Admiral Tobiah Greaves sat in his command chair with his hand over his mouth.

*And let the High Warden hold one name in every giving. Not by lot.*

At the edge of the mouth, in nine small skiffs in their cradles on the Keeping barge, nine Keepers sat with their hands on their yokes and their beads at their throats and listened.

It took her twenty-two minutes to tune the nine courses and read the book. Her hands bled through the bandages by the seventh course. She did not stop. At the end of it the harp was singing its true chord over every channel in the Reach — the great slow breathing chord, the long tide under the Narrowing, turning; anyone could hear it; a child could hear it — and she had read every page of the Testament but the last, and she sat back on her heels on the floor of the harp hall in front of the harp, with the book on her knees, and the last leaf folded and sealed in grey wax, and put her thumb on the seal.

"*My name,*" she read aloud, from the outside of the fold, "*for my successor's eyes. It is not to be spoken. The order has none but the one I gave it.*"

She broke the seal.

The guard-captain had been watching the High Warden for twenty-two minutes. He had watched the High Warden stand aside, and stand still, and say nothing, while the archivist knelt at the First Warden's harp and unmade it, wire by wire, over every channel in the Reach. He had watched a hundred Wardens on their knees on the floor, some of them weeping, some of them with their hands over their ears. He was a simple man, the guard-captain, and a devout one, and he had been told since he was a boy that the name of the First Warden was not to be spoken. When she broke the seal, he drew his sidearm and shot her.

He shot her in the back. She fell forward over the book.

The hall screamed.

Hollis was across the floor before the echo died, and the six behind her, and the guard-captain went down under them; and Cassius Mor was on his knees beside the archivist, with his hands on her, turning her over, saying her name. *Adaeze. Adaeze.* There was a great deal of blood. Her spectacles had come off. She looked up at him, puzzled, the way Danny Marsh had looked up at Juno in the gallery doorway, and then past him, at the harp, singing its true chord above her into the dome.

"It breathes," she said. "Can you hear it?"

"I can hear it."

"I want to read it," she said. "The name. I want to read it with everyone."

He looked at the leaf, open now, on the floor beside her, in a spreading pool. He picked it up. His hands were shaking. He held it where she could see it, and she looked at it, and her lips moved; and she could not; and so the High Warden of the Throat, on his knees on the floor of the harp hall with the broadcast lamp burning red above him, read it aloud for her, into every house in the Reach.

"*My name is Hesper Vane,*" read Cassius Mor. "*I was Director of the Reach Concession of the Ostrander Concern, and on the second of May, twenty-two ninety-one, I made myself First Warden of the Throat. The fork on the cushion in the Lectern was not mine. It belonged to Ambrose Lyle, the Concession's reader, who heard the breath first, and proved it, and died for it in the Lectern at the hand of my Marshal, on my order, on the twenty-fourth of April. It is engraved for his daughter Clare. I have let the order read it otherwise. I find I would rather not be thanked for it.*"

He stopped. He read the last line to himself, and then aloud.

"*If you are reading this, you have kept it. I am sorry. I did not think anyone would mind for six hundred years.*"

Adaeze Quill was looking up at the harp. She did not hear the end. Her face, under the dome, under the true chord, was quite peaceful.

Mor knelt beside her for a long time with the leaf in his hand.

Then he got up. He laid the leaf on the open book on the floor beside her, and took the Fork out of her bloody hand, gently, and laid it on the floor of the harp hall in front of the harp, on the worn stone, not on its cushion. And he went to the iron door at the side of the hall that led into what was left of the archive — the racks, the crystal, the singed daybooks and the bound volumes Adaeze had carried out of the fire a month ago and carried back in again — and he stood in it, and looked back once at the hundred Wardens on the floor of the hall, and at the red broadcast lamp, still burning.

"She wrote that the alternative was fire," said Cassius Mor, to all of them, to every house in the Reach. "Let's see."

He went into the archive and closed the iron door behind him. They heard him bar it from the inside.

The smoke came under the door eleven minutes later. By then nobody in the harp hall was trying to open it.

* * *

Juno heard it from the mouth.

She had put Adaeze and Hollis and the six aboard at Dock Four in four minutes, under the torn plating, with nobody watching, as they had planned; and then she had not done what she had planned, which was to hold the *Vigil* off the dock and guard it. She had taken the *Vigil* to the mouth. To the Keeping barge, at the edge of the shear, where nine small skiffs sat in their cradles with their engines warm, waiting for the hour, and the *Ardour* sat beside them as their escort with her forward guns out. She had not been able not to. She had stood on her bridge and listened to Adaeze's voice begin, *To my successor*, and to the harp begin to change under it; and she had said *Linnet, take us to the barge,* and Linnet had.

Greaves had said he would fire on any ship within ten kilometres of the barge. He did not fire. She did not know if he had heard her coming. She thought perhaps he had, and was listening too.

The *Vigil* came to rest four hundred metres off the barge, between it and the *Ardour*, and hung there.

On the open channel the harp was singing its true chord. You could hear it in every speaker on the bridge, in every cabin, in the passages; the crew had stopped where they stood, all through the ship, and were listening. Juno stood on the bridge with her hands on the back of the helm's chair and listened to the Needle breathe, and to the archivist's voice reading over it, *I have had the Keepers flown at the hour the waist begins to turn*, and thought that she had never in her life heard anything so beautiful or so terrible, and that Pell was hearing it, four hundred metres away, in a skiff with a cradle of glass under her and a bead at her throat.

She heard the shot.

It came over the open channel, small and flat. A scream, a hundred voices. A man's voice saying a name. Then, after a long time — very long — the man's voice again, an old man's voice, shaking, reading.

*My name is Hesper Vane.*

She did not know the name. Nobody in the Reach knew the name. It meant nothing. It was a name from before the Compact, from the Blank Year, a Concession name, a company name. And then the voice went on — *the fork on the cushion was not mine; it belonged to Ambrose Lyle, the Concession's reader, who heard the breath first* — and Juno found that she had sat down, without meaning to, on the edge of the tactical console, with her hand over her mouth, the way she had seen the Admiral sit on the screen.

*It is engraved for his daughter Clare. I have let the order read it otherwise.*

FOR CL.

Not *for clarity*. *For Clare.*

"Captain," said the rating at tactical. "Captain, the readers are calling the hour. Waist's turning. On the Wardens' channel. They're calling the Keepers in."

She stood up.

On the display the nine skiffs on the barge were lighting their engines, one after another, the way they had been trained to, at the call. The line, the waist, the lever, the turn. The Wardens' readers in the Lectern had called it, as they had called it at every Keeping for three hundred years, at the hour the waist began to turn; and some of them had called it weeping, and one had called it and then begun, on the open channel, to laugh, horribly, and been cut off. And the skiffs were lifting out of their cradles, one by one, and turning their noses to the mouth.

"Get me the barge channel," Juno said. "The Keepers' channel. All nine."

"Captain, I don't know if — "

"*Get it.*"

He got it. She stood on her bridge and looked at the nine small lights against the fire at the rim of the mouth, and opened her mouth to speak, and did not know what she was going to say.

"*Juno,*" said Pell's voice, before she could say anything.

It was very calm. It came over the Keepers' channel, small and close, from the third skiff in the line.

"*Juno, are you there? Is that the* Vigil?"

"I'm here."

"*I can see you.*" A pause. "*I heard it. All of it. The harp.*" Another pause, longer. "*It's the sound, Juno. That I painted. When I was eight. That's it. That's what I heard. I always thought it was a hunger. I thought it was asking.*" Her voice shook for the first time. "*It isn't asking for anything. It's just breathing.*"

"I know," said Juno. "Pell. I know."

"*Sister Quill's dead, isn't she.*"

"I think so. Yes."

On the display the skiffs were moving. Slowly. Out from the barge, toward the mouth, toward the shear, one after another, in their line. The first. The second. Pell's, the third, its engine lit, drifting forward. On the *Ardour*, four hundred metres beyond them, the forward guns were out, and Juno knew, the way you know a thing you have known all your life without being told it, what the *Ardour*'s standing orders for the Keeping escort were. What every escort's had always been. *A Keeper who turns back with the glass is to be prevented.*

"*Juno,*" said Pell. "*What do I do?*"

And Juno, who had been trying to find words for this moment since a slip of steel foil came out of a brass drum in the Compact Hall fifty-eight days ago, found that she had none at all. That there were no words. That she had come four hundred metres from the barge to stand between her sister and a gun, and there was nothing to say.

"Whatever you want," she said. "Pell. Whatever you want. It's yours. It's always been yours."

There was silence on the channel.

Then, in the line of skiffs at the edge of the shear, the third light turned round.

It did it slowly, the way they had been trained not to, laboriously, a narrow little ship pulling its nose round against the pull of the mouth, until it was facing out. Facing the barge, and the *Vigil*, and the *Ardour*'s guns. And it stopped there.

"*I don't want to,*" said Pell Asante on the Keepers' channel, very clearly, so that the barge and the escort and the *Vigil* and the *Ardour* could all hear it. "*I'm sorry. I don't want to.*"

Juno did not breathe.

On the *Ardour*, four hundred metres away, the forward guns moved. She saw them move. She saw them come round, the long barrels swinging slowly on their mounts toward the small turned skiff at the edge of the shear, and stop, and hold, and she put her hand on the back of the helm's chair and said, "Linnet," and Linnet put the *Vigil* forward, in between.

The *Ardour*'s guns held on the *Vigil*'s flank for a long moment.

Then, very slowly, they swung back, and went in.

"*Vigil, Ardour,*" said a voice on the escort channel. A fat, frightened, decent man called Pryce, whom Juno had known for years. "*I'm not doing it, Asante. You can tell the board of inquiry. I'm not doing it. Not after that. Not after — *" His voice cracked. "*Let them come home. Let them all come home.*"

On the display, at the edge of the shear, a fourth light turned. Abbe Sorrell. Then a fifth. Then a sixth.

Three did not. Three skiffs went on, out into the shear and down toward the dark at the centre of the mouth, in their line, the way they had been trained: the lift-mechanic and two of the new names, drawn three weeks ago from the Wheel, believers. Juno watched them go. She watched them get smaller wrong. She did not try to stop them. She did not know how she would have; and she thought, watching, that they had the same right to it as Pell had to turn, and that for three hundred years nobody in the Reach had had either.

The sixth skiff came in alongside the *Vigil*, and then the fifth and the fourth, and last of all the third, very slowly, as if its pilot were not quite sure how to fly it in this direction. It came to rest so close to the corvette's flank that Juno, at the bridge's side port, could see through the two canopies her sister's face. Pell had taken off her helmet. Her hair was cut short. She was crying, and she was looking at the bead at her own throat, lying on its cord against her white Keeper's shirt, quite still.

On the open channel the harp went on breathing, over every house in the Reach, under a red broadcast lamp in a hall where nobody was singing any more, beside an iron door with smoke coming out from under it.

* * *

The door opened at thirty-one minutes past seven on the morning of the sixth of December, Anchorhold time, exactly as the archivist had said on the air that it would, two hours after the last three Keepers went in. One of them came out. The other two did not.

By noon the Reach was arguing about it.

It argued in the Assembly, which met in emergency session and adjourned in disorder and met again. It argued on every channel. It argued in the light-wells of Tallow Under and the habs of the Shoals and on the docks, and in the Compact Hall, where the Wardens who were left — there were sixty-odd of them now, without a High Warden, with their archive burned to the bare steel and their harp tuned to a note that half of them refused to sing — stood on their bench beneath the Founders' Tally and said that three had gone in, and the door had opened, and that the covenant was kept. And the other half stood beside them and said nothing. And the Hall said many things.

*It breathes,* some of them said. *You heard it. Anyone could hear it.*

*It opened because three went in,* said others. *It has always opened because they went in. Three were enough. The faithful are always enough.*

*There's no record,* said a great many. *There's no archive. The High Warden burned it. There's nothing left but a broadcast and an old woman's voice and a name nobody's ever heard of. Who was Hesper Vane? Who was Ambrose Lyle? Where's the proof?*

Juno heard it all from the *Vigil*'s bridge, at anchor off Corrie Rock under the League's flag, with a Navy warrant for her arrest on every channel and Admiral Greaves, in a long private message that she did not reply to, asking her as a personal favour not to come back to Tallow Under for a year or two. She heard it with her sister sitting on the floor of her day cabin, in her white Keeper's shirt, painting. Pell had not stopped painting since they brought her aboard. She painted the harp hall from memory. She painted it over and over: the dome, the mouth, the gold frame of the harp, and on the floor before it, very small, a figure in grey.

On the ninth, they went to Tallow Under anyway.

They went at night, in a League shuttle with its transponder off, the two of them, with Rhee at the controls saying nothing at all. Their mother met them at the back of the Compact Hall, at the low end under the gallery, where the ceiling came down and the original salt wall showed through with the marks of the cutting machines on it in long curved grooves like the inside of a shell. She had the keys. She was still the Speaker, for the moment. She did not ask why they had come. She embraced Pell without speaking, and then Juno, and then she let them in and stood back by the door and waited.

The Hall was empty and dark. Caul was down. Only the lamps along the Wardens' bench were lit, low, and in their light, on the original salt wall behind the bench, in its frame of polished brass far too fine for it, the Founders' Tally.

Juno went up to it. She had not touched it since her Naming Day.

The steel was cold. Three hundred years of hands had worn it smooth, as she remembered; at the top the names were gone entirely, only a faint unevenness, like the surface of water. She ran her fingers down it, slowly, row by row. The scratches deepened as she went down. Lower on the plate, where fewer hands had reached, they were sharper — still worn, still soft at their edges, but there. Shallow grooves under her fingertips, like braille in a language nobody spoke any more.

"Pell," she said. "Bring a lamp."

Pell brought one from the bench. Juno held it close to the steel, low, so the light lay across the plate sideways and every scratch threw a shadow.

At the very bottom of the plate, below everything, where the steel was least worn because it was hardest to reach — where you would have to kneel to touch it, and nobody had knelt in three hundred years — there were four lines. They were not like the others. The others above were in a dozen hands, deep and careful, rows of them. These four were small and scratched and uneven, as if made by a child and by people in a hurry; and they had a second word after each name, which none of the rows above had.

The first was in a hand that wavered, like a child's.

C. DACE — PILOT

Under it, in a different hand, harder, quicker:

A. LYLE — HARP

Juno's breath stopped.

Under that, in a third hand, steady and deep:

T. KESH — PILOT

And under all three, very small, almost too small to read, in a fourth hand — small and careful and upright, nothing like the others — as if it had been added later, by somebody who had come back to the plate alone:

FOR CLARE

Juno knelt on the stone floor of the Compact Hall in front of the Founders' Tally with the lamp in her hand. Pell knelt beside her. Neither of them said anything for a long time.

"Lyle," said Pell finally. "The reader. The one she said. Whose fork it was."

"Yes."

"And *pilot*." Pell put her fingertip, very gently, on the last name but one. "They didn't put *Keeper*. Whoever scratched these. They had the word. Everyone above them had it, all the way up, the old Wardens say — *Keeper*, *Keeper*, *Keeper*. And they put *pilot*." She looked at Juno. "Why would you do that? Why would you scratch somebody's name on the Founders' Tally and not call them a Keeper?"

"Because they weren't," said Juno.

She did not know how she knew. She knew. She knelt with her fingers on the three short words in their three different hands, at the bottom of the oldest thing in the Reach, where nobody had knelt to read them in three hundred years, and she knew that somebody had known. In the year of the First Keeping. The year of the Withdrawal. In the Blank Year, before the records began. Somebody had known that it breathed, and had knelt here, or somewhere, and scratched it into steel the only way they could, in one word, three times, so that it would be there for anyone who ever knelt down far enough to read it.

*Pilot. Harp. Pilot.*

*Not Keeper.*

Behind them, at the low end of the Hall, under the gallery, their mother stood by the door in the dark with her hands folded, waiting, and did not come closer.

Pell took a stylus out of the pocket of her coat. Juno did not know where she had got it; from the bench, perhaps, where the Wardens kept them for the Naming Day children. Her sister looked at the plate. Then she leaned in, below the four small lines, where there was a little bare steel left at the very edge, and scratched, slowly and very carefully, in a painter's steady hand:

A. QUILL — ARCHIVIST

She sat back on her heels and looked at it.

"She'd have wanted it right," Pell said. "The word. She'd have checked."

Juno put her arm round her. They knelt together on the stone in front of the old steel plate with the lamp between them, the Speaker's daughters, a mutineer and a Keeper who had turned round, in the dark of the Compact Hall, while all through the Reach above and around them people argued and wept and sang and stopped singing; and Juno thought of the Needle, two million kilometres away, open, breathing, as it always had and always would whatever anyone sang to it; and she did not know what would happen to the Reach now. Nobody did. That was the thing that had changed. For the first time in three hundred and seventeen years, nobody knew.

She found that she could bear it.

# Chapter 21: What the Needle Keeps

*The Ebb · 2937*

There were a hundred skiffs on Dock Four on the morning of the Sixtieth Narrowing, and Isra counted them, because counting was what she did when she could not do anything else.

They filled the hangar. They had been coming up from Tallow for a week on the Lantern's ore-haulers, two and three at a time, narrow black twelve-metre hulls with a lamp painted on every nose, and the Lantern's fitters had racked them in rows across the great cold floor under the one working light, each with its cradle slung under its belly and its cradle full: glass from the Gutter, glass from the Pans, glass torn out of the walls of Rope Street and the floors of the bone-pickers' skiffs, glass from every Return in the Ossuary that the Lantern had stripped since April. Every gram in the Reach. You could feel it in the hangar. It was like standing in a room full of held breath. The shard on its cord against Isra's chest had not stopped humming, faintly, since she came down the lift.

A hundred Keepers stood in front of them in their black, in their rows, with beads at their throats, and sang.

They were singing the Long Night. All forty verses, Isra thought; somebody had collected them, the way Hanne had, and taught them all. The Hunger Tone under it, from the hangar's speakers, from the harp hall, where the Lantern's two boy-readers sat at the console in their black coats keeping the drone. A hundred voices over it. *The door grew narrow in the dark. The Reach was cold and small.* They were mostly young. A great many of them were smiling. They looked, Isra thought, the way Pike had said they looked at the Fifty-Ninth: happy. So happy it was like a light coming off them.

She found him in the fourth row.

He was taller than everyone round him and greyer and he was not singing. He stood in his black coat with his hands at his sides and his strapped ribs under it, and the bead at his throat, and when he saw her coming through the rows he did not look surprised. He looked, for a moment, as if he were going to say something; and then he only shook his head, and smiled in his beard.

"Number forty," she said.

"Number forty."

"They put you in the fourth row."

"Tall men at the back," said Pike. "So we don't block the view." He coughed, carefully, into the rag he still carried. "You've got your heater fixed?"

"I kicked it."

"Good girl." He looked at her. He did not ask where she was going. He did not look at the bulge under her flight jacket where the Fork lay against her ribs, or at the old cittern on its strap over her shoulder. "Mind the shear," he said. "At the mouth. It comes from behind."

"I know."

"I know you know." He put out one big ruined hand and touched her hair, once, the way Hanne used to. "Go on, then."

She went.

At the far end of the hangar, by the Lamplighter's black ore-hauler, a knot of black coats stood round a small figure in black, and in the middle of them, between two of the Lantern's boys, with his hands bound in front of him with a strip of cord, Lucan. They had brought him up from the penitents' cells at dawn. He saw her across the hangar and his face did not change at all, and he did not move his head; but his eyes went to the bulge under her jacket, and away, and he closed them for a second, and opened them again.

The Lamplighter had seen her too.

Mercy Oduya stood by her ship in her black coat with her hands bare. She had not slept, Isra thought; her face was grey and swollen, and there was something in it that had not been there before the fifteenth — a stillness, a kind of looking-through. Her son lay in the harp hall under the dome, in his skiff, with nine little lamps round it. She had sat with him every night. She had not changed a single order. She had not called off a single skiff. She had only, the day after the Deacon left, struck her own name off the flagship's roster, and put it on the Keepers' list instead, at the top. Number one. She would fly the lead skiff herself.

She looked at Isra across the hangar for a long time. Then she inclined her head, the way she had to the Prior in the harp hall six weeks ago, which was more than he had done for her.

Isra did not incline hers back. She walked out of the hangar to the bone-pickers' berth at the far end of Dock Four, where the Gristle sat at her lines, and climbed in, and closed the hatch, and kicked the heater, and sat with her hands on the yoke and the cittern across her knees and the Fork against her ribs and waited for the Long Night.

* * *

The readers called the Long Night at nineteen minutes past four in the afternoon, Anchor time, and the hundred skiffs went out of Dock Four in a line behind the Lamplighter, and Isra went out of the bone-pickers' berth three minutes later, alone, with her running lights dark.

Nobody stopped her. Nobody was looking. Everything in the Reach that could fly was out at the mouth that afternoon, every Lantern cutter and ore-hauler and shuttle, lined up beyond the shear in a great ragged crescent to watch the Great Keeping go in; and every receiver in the Reach was on, in Tallow and the Gutter and the Pans and the rocks, tuned to the harp hall's channel, to the Hunger Tone and the Lamplighter's voice. Isra went out under them all in the Gristle with her transponder off, low, the way she had gone out to the Ossuary every day for ten years, and nobody so much as hailed her.

On the open channel, over the drone, she heard the flagship's reader calling the line. Not the Lantern's boys. Lucan.

"*Keepers, flagship. Hold at the outer markers. Shear is very heavy from two to six. I say again, hold. We'll wait for a gap.*"

His voice was perfectly steady.

She went in past the outer markers on her own line. Not his; hers, the Ossuary line, the one she had flown for ten years, low and to the left, under the worst of it, where the mouth's pull bent the shear-waves up and over and the old Returns drifted in the lee. She did not need a reader. She knew it. The Gristle bucked and shuddered and screamed in every seam the way she always did, and Isra turned into the waves before they came, and did not think about anything at all except Pike's voice in her head saying *it comes from behind*, and every time she turned to look behind, it did.

The Bell was where it had always been.

She saw it come out of the glow at the very edge of the mouth, where the pull held it: a small grey cylinder, nose to the dark, its lattice of wires at the tail bent and furred with frost, hanging quite still in all that violence the way a stone hangs still in a fast stream. She brought the Gristle in beside it on the lee side and put the grapple on it, gently, Pike's way, and it took her two tries, and the second time it held.

She put on her suit and went over with the cable.

Up close it was smaller than she had thought. Two metres, nose to tail. Hand-made. She could see that even under the frost: the welds were neat and slightly uneven, the welds of somebody careful working alone. On its flank, as Lucan had said, there was a little hatch. It had rusted shut. She broke it with the butt of her cutter, and inside there was a port, old and odd, a pattern of pins she did not recognise; and she cursed for a while, in her helmet, and then took the cable's head apart with her cutter's blade and her gloved fingers, and twisted wires to pins by guesswork and by the colour of the insulation, until the Gristle's board in her helmet speaker said, very faintly, *external load*.

Then she went to the tail.

The end-plate was furred with six hundred years of frost, and the socket in the middle of it was packed with it, hard and white. She chipped it out with the point of her blade, very carefully, a flake at a time. It took a long time. The shear went over her like surf. On the open channel, faint in her helmet, she heard Lucan again: "*Hold, Keepers. Hold at the markers. Gap in eight minutes. I'll call it.*" And the Lamplighter's voice, low and warm and patient, *We'll hold, Brother.*

When the socket was clear she took out the Fork.

She held it in her gloved hand at the edge of the mouth, in the grey-gold light, with the Needle filling the whole of the sky in front of her. A little brass thing, fifteen centimetres long, its tines worn bright. *For Cl.* She thought of Lucan on his knees in the harp hall at two in the morning with the hinge-pins on the floor. She thought of Hanne's hand on the low string. She thought, for no reason, of the boy in the first Return she had cut in April, with the grey rag over his face.

She put the Fork into the socket, stem first.

It went in like a key. All the way down. And at the bottom, she felt it through her glove — a small definite *click*, as some catch inside the end-plate that had waited six hundred years took hold of it, and held.

The lattice of wires at the tail shivered.

Frost came off it in a fine bright spray. The bent wires straightened, slowly, as if they were stretching after sleep, and began very faintly to hum; and the shard against Isra's chest, inside her suit, woke and hummed back. She felt it through her ribs. She had been holding her breath. She let it out.

The lattice was not facing the waist. It had drifted; it was facing off to the left, toward the crescent of watching ships. She went back to the Gristle and took the grapple controls and turned the Bell, very gently, a degree at a time, the way you would turn a sleeping child, until its humming wires faced the dark at the centre of the mouth.

Then she sat in the Gristle's pilot seat with the cable running out of her ship into the Bell and the cittern on her knees, and waited for the harp.

On the open channel the Hunger Tone droned on.

The harp did not change.

* * *

The two boys had gone to the dock to watch the Keepers out, because the Prior had told them they should — *you'll want to tell your children you saw it, boys; go on; I'll hold the drone* — and so at twenty to five on the afternoon of the Sixtieth Narrowing, Benedek Saar was alone in the harp hall with the harp, and the Lamplighter's dead son in his skiff under the dome with nine little lamps round him, and his own two sticks.

He had been alone with the harp, in that hall, for most of sixty years. He knew it the way you know a room in the dark.

He had not known, until the last few minutes, whether he would do it.

He had sat on the bench at the side of the hall since the Long Night was called, with the boys at the console in front of him singing, and listened to Lucan's voice on the channel holding the Keepers at the markers, steady and level and lying; and he had thought about eleven old men in their cells, and about a station built for six thousand, and about what Mercy Oduya would do afterward to whoever did this, and to everyone near them. He had thought about it with great care, the way he had been taught to think about everything. And then he had looked across the hall at the little black skiff under the dome, at the soft round face behind the thin glass of the canopy, and the yellow name painted above it, and the hands on the lever not pulled, and the three neat holes in the engine housing; and he had found that he was not thinking about any of it any more.

He sent the boys away.

When they had gone he got up off the bench, which took some time, and went to the console. He knew where Lucan had put the switch. Under the third floor-plate from the left, beside the reader's chair; Lucan had told him, on the night of the fourteenth, sitting on the end of his bed. The Prior had remembered every word Lucan said that night. He had gone over it every night since, in his cell, lying awake.

He did not have the Fork.

He had known that, too, for eight days. He had given it to the girl himself. He stood at the console on his sticks and looked at the harp, singing the Hunger Tone into the dome as he had made it sing every morning of his life, and he thought: *I cannot tune it true without the Fork. I am eighty-one. I have sung this note for sixty years. I do not know the other one. I heard it once, the night the boy tuned it, and I made him take it down.*

He leaned on his sticks and closed his eyes.

And over the channel, under the drone, very faint, from somewhere out at the edge of the mouth, there came a single low note, plucked on a string.

Old. Round. True.

It came again. And again. Steady, patient, every few seconds, the way you would pluck a string to give somebody a note to tune to. Not on the harp hall's channel. On the Wardens' own channel, the order's old private band, which nobody but the order had used in a hundred years, which the Lamplighter's boys did not know existed. Somebody out at the mouth had found it. Somebody had asked Lucan for it.

*The low string,* the Prior thought. *Never retuned. The tellers kept it.*

He laid his sticks down on the floor.

He knelt at the base of the frame, which he had not done in twenty years, because his knees would not allow it; and they did not allow it now, and he did it anyway; and with the girl's grandmother's low string sounding over the order's channel from two million kilometres away, every few seconds, steady, patient, he began to tune the First Warden's harp away from the Hunger Tone.

It was very slow. His hands shook. His ears were eighty-one years old. Course by course. Peg by peg. Every time the string sounded he stopped and listened and turned the peg a hair, and listened again, until the wire and the string lay down together into one note; and then the next wire; and the next. Somewhere around the third course the harp began to change. Somewhere around the sixth it began to ring. The great slow bell-sound he had heard once, through the door of this hall, five weeks ago, and had ordered taken down. It came up out of the strings round him and filled the dome, deep and layered and enormous, and went out on the harp hall's channel into every receiver in the Reach, into every house in Tallow and the Gutter and the Pans and the rocks, into every ship in the watching crescent and every skiff at the markers.

The Prior did not stop to hear what anyone said about it. He did not have time. He tuned the ninth course, the last, on his knees, with the tears running down his face into his beard; and then he reached out with one shaking hand and lifted the third floor-plate from the left, and found the switch Lucan had put there, and closed it.

* * *

Lucan heard it on the flagship's bridge, through the speakers, with his hands bound in front of him and the Lamplighter's second at his elbow: the harp changing.

He had been calling a false line for twenty-six minutes. *Hold at the markers. Shear is very heavy. Wait for a gap.* The shear was heavy; that was true. It was always heavy. He had watched the hundred skiffs hang at the outer markers in their long black line with their engines warm, waiting for his word, and the Lamplighter's skiff at the head of them, small and black with the yellow lamp on its nose; and he had listened to her voice on the channel saying *We'll hold, Brother,* patient, trusting, as the minutes went by; and he had wanted, more than he had ever wanted anything, to be sick.

And then, under the Hunger Tone, over the order's old band, which he had told Isra about at the grille of the fourth cell — very faint — a low string, plucked. Again. Again.

And then the harp began to change.

He heard it go. Course by course, slowly — whoever was doing it was doing it slowly, with old hands — the false drone sliding and fighting and lying down, wire by wire, into the bell. He knew who it was before the third course. Nobody else could have. He stood on the flagship's bridge with his bound hands and his eyes shut and listened to Benedek Saar tune the First Warden's harp on his knees, over every channel in the Reach, and he began, without any sound, to weep.

"*Brother,*" said the Lamplighter's voice on the channel. "*What is that?*"

He opened his eyes.

"That's the harp," said Lucan. "Tuned to the First Warden's Fork. That's what it sounds like when it's true."

Silence on the channel. On the bridge round him the Lamplighter's people had gone still. On the display the hundred skiffs hung at the markers. The bell rang on, louder, deeper, as the Prior reached the sixth course, the seventh.

"*It sounds like a bell,*" said Mercy Oduya.

"It is a bell. That's what's in the throat. That's the glass. Every Keeping that ever was, Lamplighter. Fifty-nine layers, at the waist, as big as the Anchor. Ringing." He made himself go on. "That's what the girl told you. On Dock Four. On your son's skiff. It's true. It's choking. It's not hungry. It never was. Every skiff we've ever sent in has made it worse. If you take a hundred in now, with every gram in the Reach — "

"*Be quiet, Brother.*"

He was quiet.

The Prior reached the ninth course. The bell filled every speaker on the bridge. Lucan heard, under it, very faint, the sound of an old man breathing hard, close to a microphone; and then a click.

And out at the mouth, at the very edge, where the grey-gold fire met the dark, something answered.

He saw it on the display. Everyone did. A point of light at the rim of the mouth — the Bell, the dead buoy, that had hung there for six hundred years — lit, suddenly, bright as a star, as the harp's ringing reached it and it took the note and threw it, all of it, every course, a single huge true note, straight down into the dark at the centre of the mouth like a stone into a well.

The Needle rang.

There was no other word for it. The mouth *rang*, from rim to rim. The bruise at its centre went white and blue and white again, and the grey-gold fire round it shivered and blurred, and the sound came back up out of the throat over every channel — not the harp now, not the Bell, but the thing itself, the mass at the waist, answering its own note: a deep, terrible, layered, rising bell-sound, louder and louder, as if something enormous were straining against itself in the dark.

On the channel the Lamplighter said, very quietly, "*Tobin.*"

Then her skiff moved.

It went forward out of the line at the markers, alone. Lucan saw it. Small and black, the yellow lamp on its nose, its engine lit, going toward the mouth, toward the ringing, toward the white-blue light at the centre.

"Lamplighter," said Lucan. "Mercy. *Don't.*"

"*I'm not taking them, Brother.*" Her voice was calm. "*Hold them. Hold the rest. You're right. I heard it. I can hear it.*" The skiff went on. "*I'm only going to look. I have to see it. I put him in there. I have to see.*"

"*Mercy — *"

"*Hold them, Brother.*"

And then the throat let go.

Afterward, nobody in the Reach could agree how it had looked. Everybody had seen it. Every ship in the crescent, every skiff at the markers, every screen in Tallow with a feed from the Anchor's telescopes; and no two people ever told it the same, and Hanne Venn would have collected all of them and sung them all and said they were all wrong. But they all began the same way. The mouth went white — pure white, the whole of it, rim to rim, for the space of a breath. And then it opened.

Not wider. *Out.* The throat turned itself inside out, the way a cough turns you inside out, and everything that had been stuck in it for six hundred years came out at once.

Ships.

Hundreds of them. More than anyone had ever imagined there could be. They came pouring out of the white in a long tumbling river, end over end, in a storm of shattering glass — skiffs, little narrow Keepers' skiffs, the black ones of the Lantern and the grey ones of the Compact and older ones, shapes nobody alive had ever seen, all of them crusted and furred and wrapped in glass that was breaking off them as they came, so that the whole river glittered, like a waterfall in sunlight, like a galaxy turning over. They came and came. They spilled out of the mouth past the markers and through the line of black skiffs and on into the Ossuary and beyond it, scattering, slowing, turning in the grey-gold light, and still more came behind them, and the glass came off them in great bright clouds and drifted, and sang — you could hear it, on every channel, the shattered glass ringing as it tumbled, a sound like ten thousand wineglasses touched with a fingertip at once.

The hundred skiffs at the markers broke and scattered before it like birds.

Lucan saw the Lamplighter's skiff for a moment, small and black against the white, right at the mouth, in the middle of the river. Its nose was up. It was not turning away. It was holding there, in the outrush, with the dead pouring past it on every side.

"*I see them,*" said Mercy Oduya on the channel, very softly. Her voice was full of wonder. "*Oh, Brother. I see them. They're all — they're all coming — *"

The river took her.

He did not see her skiff again. Nobody did.

It went on for eleven minutes. Then the white at the mouth began to fade, and the river thinned, and the last few ships came tumbling out of the light in ones and twos, slowly, almost gently, like the last leaves off a tree; and the glass-clouds drifted and spread and settled; and the ringing died away, on every channel, into silence.

Into a silence. And then, faint, under it, from the open channel where the harp still sang its true chord from the Anchor: a breath.

Lucan looked at the display.

The mouth of the Needle hung where it had always hung. But it was not a bruise any more. At its centre, where for all his life there had been a dull dark wound the colour of old blood, there was a ring. A ring of soft grey-gold light, faint and steady and very slightly irregular, like a coin worn by thumbs. The waist trace on the flagship's console, which Lucan had been watching without knowing he was watching it, said a number he had never seen it say. It was still narrowing. It was the Long Night; it would narrow for hours yet, the way it always had. But there was nothing in the waist. Nothing at all. Only the passage, wide and clear, breathing.

The Eye. His grandmother had told him once that old Wardens called it that. He had never believed there was such a thing.

"*Flagship,*" said a voice on the Keepers' channel. Thick, wet, coughing. "*Flagship, this is number forty.*" A long pause, while the voice coughed. "*I've turned round. I'm coming in. I don't — I don't want to.*" Another pause. "*Is there anybody on the second gun?*"

Lucan looked at the Lamplighter's second, beside him on the bridge, a heavy grey-haired woman with the lamp on her shoulder and her face wet, who had been staring at the empty place on the display where her prophet's skiff had been.

"No," the woman said into the channel. Her voice broke. "No, Pike. There's nobody. Come home."

* * *

They found her on the third day.

There were six hundred and forty-one of them by the Wardens' count, which the Wardens did not finish making for a month: six hundred and forty-one Returns, drifting in the Ossuary and beyond it, in a long loose cloud that stretched halfway from the mouth to Hollin, with the glass that had come off them hanging among them in slow glittering drifts. Every bone-picker in the Reach was out among them. Nobody was stripping them. Nobody had said not to; nobody needed to. They went from hull to hull in their skiffs with their lamps, slowly, looking in at the canopies, at the faces; and when they found one they knew — a family name, a Shoals clan mark painted on a nose, a Lantern lamp — they put a grapple on it, gently, and brought it home.

The Lantern's boys had found the Lamplighter's skiff on the first day, at the very front of the river, three hundred kilometres out. It was empty. The canopy was open. Nobody understood it, and everybody had a story.

Isra went out on the third day with Pike in the jump-seat, coughing, with his ribs strapped, and Lucan in the cargo bay with a lamp and a box of the Wardens' grey cloth, and the cittern wedged behind the pilot's seat where she could reach it. She did not go into the near cloud, where everyone was. She went out to the edge of the river, the far edge, where the oldest hulls had come to rest — the strange ones, the shapes nobody had a name for, the ones so furred with glass that you could not see their outlines at all.

"There," said Pike.

It was an ore-tug.

She knew that much from the shape under the glass: a stubby blunt hull built for pulling, with a tow-hook at the stern and a short wide cockpit at the bow. A design nobody had built in the Reach in four hundred years; she had seen one like it once, in a picture in Hanne's room, pinned to the wall, a drawing some teller had made from a description by some other teller. The glass on it was thicker than on anything she had ever cut. It had grown back along the hull in long sweeping plates, like the feathers of a bird folded in flight, so that the whole tug looked as if it had been flying for a very long time, very fast, into a wind.

They put the grapple on it. They went over, the three of them, Pike moving slowly, and stood on the hull.

The glass was thinnest on the canopy. Isra put her helmet lamp to it, and through it — through the milk of six hundred years — she could see the canopy beneath. It had a crack in it. An old one, forward, low, shaped like the delta of a river.

Lucan cleared the glass from the side of the nose with the flat of his cutter, very carefully, the way he had chipped the frost from the Bell's socket — she had told him about that, in the infirmary, and he had made her tell him twice. Under the glass there was paint. Old, flaking, a faded white on a hull that had been some dark colour once. Letters. He cleared them one at a time.

SMALL MERCY.

Nobody said anything.

They cut the canopy open. It took an hour. Inside, the cockpit was very cold in the way only things from the mouth were cold, and very small, and very still. It smelled, Isra thought, of nothing; and then, faintly, as the cold air moved, of something she could not place. Something sweet. Like the peel of a fruit she had once seen in a picture.

She was in the pilot's seat.

Isra had seen a great many of them. They were always in their seats. This one was a woman. Thirty-something; it was hard to tell. Dark hair, cut short. Her helmet was not on her head; it sat on the console in front of her, where she had put it, the way a pilot does on a long run. Her hands were on the yoke. Her face was turned a little upward, toward the canopy, toward the delta crack, as if she had been looking at something through it; and her eyes were open, and her mouth was open very slightly, and she was not afraid. You could see that. Whatever she had been looking at, at the end, she had not been afraid of it.

At her throat, on a cord gone black, there was a small cloudy bead of glass.

It was not lying against her skin. It was standing out from it, straight out on its cord, taut, toward the delta crack in the canopy, the way a compass needle stands toward a pole. Six hundred years in the throat, and it was still straining toward the mouth.

In the breast pocket of her coveralls, buttoned shut, there was a small hard shape. Lucan undid the button, very gently, with his gloved fingers. It was a bolt. A plain steel bolt, the kind that held a cradle-frame together. He held it in his palm and looked at it and did not know what it was, and put it back, and buttoned the pocket.

Isra did not put a rag over her face.

"The recorder," said Pike, behind them. "Under the console. They always had them, the old ones. Built to keep."

It was where he said. A box of dull grey alloy under the console, its own hull, its own seals, unbroken. Lucan put a cable from the Gristle's board to it, as Isra had to the Bell, by guesswork, by the colour of the wires. It took him a long time. Then the board in Isra's helmet said *external load*, and the little grey box under the console gave a soft click, and a light on its face that had been dark for six hundred and forty-six years came on, amber, and blinked.

And a voice came into their helmets.

"*This is Teodora Kesh.*"

A woman's voice. Low, roughened, a little breathless, very close, as if she were speaking with her mouth almost against the microphone. An accent none of them had ever heard. Isra's hand went out, without her deciding it, and closed on the back of the pilot's seat.

"*Pilot. Small Mercy. Out of Dock Four, Anchorage. First of May, twenty-two ninety-one. I'm in the throat. I'm not a Keeper. I want that on the record before anything else. My name's on a list of Keepers and I'm not one. I've got two tonnes of glass in my cradle and I'm not going to release it.*"

They listened.

They stood in the tiny frozen cockpit of the Small Mercy, at the edge of the river of the dead, the three of them, and listened to Teodora Kesh tell them who she was. A tug pilot. Forty thousand marks of debt she had inherited from her mother and had paid off by agreeing to fly into the Needle. A brother called Nico, a glass-cutter, in a cell under a station called Anchorage, for a murder nobody did. A friend called Corrigan Dace, who had flown in first, alone, a month before, and not come back — *a pilot,* she said, *not a Keeper; say that; Lulu scratched it on the Tally.* And a man called Ambrose Lyle.

"*He was the Senior Throat-Reader at the Lectern for twenty-three years,*" said the voice, "*and on the twenty-fourth of April he was murdered for what's on the spool in this recorder. He heard the Needle breathe. He's got it on here. You'll hear it. It doesn't need us. It's never needed us. The Narrowing's natural, it comes and goes on its own, it's been doing it since before there were people. The Director knew. Hesper Vane. Director of the Concession. She knew, and she made the Keeping up anyway, to keep us cutting after the Concern was gone. Her own memo's on here. Nine pages. It says* proceed *at the bottom.*"

Lucan made a small sound in his helmet.

"*And there's something else on here,*" said Teo Kesh. "*Ambrose worked it out. The glass we put in doesn't go anywhere. It sticks. At the waist. It builds up. He says sixty Keepings and it'll choke. Six hundred and fifty years. Hesper Vane said nobody'll be alive to care.*" A pause. The breath came close to the microphone. "*Well. If you're hearing this, I suppose somebody is.*"

Isra did not realise she was crying until she could not see the amber light.

The voice went on. The waist. The others releasing their glass, one after another, and the flare, and the ringing. *Something stayed,* she said. *I can hear it.* A woman called Marguerite going past on the way home, crying and laughing. *Tell them it doesn't need us. Tell them his name. Ambrose Lyle. Tell them Nico Kesh didn't kill anybody.* And then: *Turning into the wave.* And then a long time with nothing in it but engines, and breathing, and under both, faint, very faint, a sound Isra knew. A low, slow, vast rising and falling, like someone sleeping in the next room.

"*I'm past the waist,*" said Teo Kesh. Her voice had changed. It was quiet now, and slow, and full of something that was not fear. "*I think I'm past it. There's no shear. There's nothing. It's light. All of it. It's on my hands. The engines are running and I don't think I'm moving. Or everything's moving with me. I can't tell.*" A breath. "*I can hear it breathing. Ambrose, you should hear this. You should be here. You'd know what it was.*"

A long silence.

"*Clock says four minutes. Clock says — no. Clock's wrong. Clock's stopped.*" Another breath. "*I don't think I'm going through. I don't think there's a through. Not tonight. I think it's holding me. Like it's holding its breath and I'm inside the breath.*" She laughed, very softly, a sound so close and so alive that Pike, behind Isra, made a noise like something breaking. "*It's all right. It's — it's all right. The recorder'll keep. It's built to keep. Ambrose said the Needle keeps what you give it. I don't think he meant this. But it will.*"

Silence. The breath, rising and falling.

"*Nico. I kept my promise. I didn't feed it. Remember.*"

Silence.

"*Whoever you are,*" said Teodora Kesh. "*Whenever you are. It doesn't need you. It never did. Don't give it anything. Don't let anyone tell you it's hungry. It's just breathing.*" A long pause. "*Let it breathe.*"

And then, very softly, so softly the recorder barely caught it, in a voice full of wonder:

"*Oh. It's beautiful. I didn't know.*"

The amber light went on blinking. There was nothing else on the channel but the breath — the recorder's own, six hundred and forty-six years old, the long slow sound of the Needle sleeping in the next room — and then a soft click, and the spool began, and a man's voice, old and gentle and precise, said, "*This is the long breath. Forty days of it, in forty minutes. You don't have to understand anything. Just listen.*"

Lucan switched it off.

None of them said anything for a long time.

"For Clare," said Lucan at last. He said it to the frozen cockpit. He was holding the side of the canopy with one gloved hand, as if he needed to. "The Fork. In the Bell. *For Cl.* She said his name. Ambrose Lyle. His daughter's name was — " He stopped. "It doesn't say *For Clarity*. It never did. It says *For Clare*."

Isra did not know what he meant. She did not ask. She would ask later, and he would tell her, and she would tell it to somebody else, and they would tell it to somebody else, and it would be wrong by the time it reached the end, as everything was. Wrong is better than nothing. You can fix wrong.

She reached out and took the bead at Teo Kesh's throat in her gloved fingers — very gently, the way you would take a child's hand — and lifted the cord over the frozen head, and held it. It lay in her palm, a nub of cloudy glass the size of a pea, and leaned, insistently, toward the mouth. Toward the Eye.

Then she reached behind the pilot's seat for the cittern, which she had brought over without knowing why, the way you bring a thing you cannot put down, and set it on her knee in the tiny cockpit, the way Hanne used to, and plucked the low string. Once.

The old note went out into the cold. Round. True. In her palm, the bead hummed back. Against her chest, the shard. Somewhere behind them, two million kilometres away, under a dome, the First Warden's harp, still tuned to it. And under all of them, faint, steady, patient, like someone sleeping in the next room, the Needle, breathing.

"I hear you," said Isra Venn to the woman in the pilot's seat. "Teodora Kesh. Pilot. I hear you."

She plucked the string again, and began, for the first time in her life, to sing.
